Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. NOTE FOR THIS PAPER: November 2023 prints a mark allocation against EVERY question and against every limb of every limbed question, with no omission anywhere - it is the most completely marked of the 2023 sittings. Every question sums to 16, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected.

Understand this first

Start from the problem the scheme solves. A ship's machinery has to be shown to the classification surveyor on a fixed cycle, and if every item is shown at once the ship stops for a long time to let it happen. So the items are split into groups with staggered dates, and the surveyor sees roughly a fifth of them each year. Nothing about the deadline changes — every item is still examined inside the same cycle — but the work is spread, and each item can be shown at the moment the engineers were going to open it anyway. That is the whole idea: the same interval, distributed. Two things follow. First, this is an arrangement the classification society operates on behalf of the flag, not a rule the ship reads out of a convention — the convention only fixes the outer deadline. Second, it is about when class must look, not about when the ship must overhaul, which is a different system run by the company. They meet at one point: if the society approves the ship's own maintenance scheme, the maintenance the crew is already doing can count as the examination. And the sting in the tail is what happens when an item slips past its date: it becomes an outstanding requirement recorded against the ship, with a deadline of its own, and the ship's standing with insurers and charterers follows that record.

Show the core points to cover

Model written answer

(a) Significance, objectives, methodology and technology

1. What the continuous survey of machinery is, and where it sits

A cargo ship's machinery must be surveyed by or on behalf of the Administration, and SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 fixes the outer interval: a renewal survey at intervals not exceeding five years, with intermediate and annual surveys inside the cycle. Regulation 6 is the survey and inspection obligation itself and the authority under which a recognized organization acts; regulation 11 requires the condition to be maintained after survey.

Taken literally, that means every surveyable machinery item is opened, examined and tested together, once every five years. Continuous Survey of Machinery (CSM) is the classification arrangement that breaks that block apart. The surveyable items are divided into groups with individual due dates spread across the cycle, so that roughly one fifth of the items fall due each year and every item is still examined within the five years. The interval is not relaxed. Only its distribution changes.

Two points of identity matter and both are commonly got wrong. CSM is a classification arrangement, not a statutory instrument — SOLAS sets the interval, class rules distribute it, and the Administration accepts the arrangement through the recognized organization. And CSM is a survey regime, not a maintenance regime: it says when class must see an item, not when the ship must overhaul it.

2. Why it matters - the significance

  • The ship keeps trading. A whole-machinery renewal survey concentrates opening-up, dismantling and testing into one window that in practice forces an extended stay. Spreading it converts one long outage into many short ones taken alongside cargo work.
  • Workload is levelled. The engine department, the superintendent and the spares budget carry a steady annual load instead of a five-yearly spike that no crew complement can absorb.
  • Survey and maintenance converge. Items are shown to the surveyor when they are already open for planned overhaul. The same dismantling serves both purposes, which is the single largest saving in the scheme.
  • Condition is known continuously. Class holds a rolling picture of the machinery rather than a five-yearly snapshot, and deterioration is caught between renewals rather than at one.
  • It is the gateway to the modern alternatives. An approved planned maintenance scheme, and condition-based arrangements built on it, are grafted onto the continuous framework — without CSM there is nothing for them to attach to.

3. Objectives

  1. To discharge the machinery renewal survey required within the five-year cycle, item by item, without relaxing the interval.
  2. To maximise ship availability by removing the need for a single extended survey outage.
  3. To distribute survey and maintenance workload evenly across the cycle and across crews.
  4. To align class attendance with planned overhaul, so that one opening-up satisfies both.
  5. To maintain a continuous, auditable record of machinery condition in the class survey status.
  6. To detect deterioration early and allow corrective action before it becomes a defect or a casualty.
  7. To support informed docking and repair planning from a known forward schedule of due items.

4. Methodology - how the scheme actually runs

ElementHow it works in practice
Item register and groupingEvery surveyable machinery item is listed and assigned to a group with its own due date, spread so that the whole register is covered within five years
Survey status reportThe class record of what is due, what is completed and what is overdue. It is the working document, and it is what a port State control officer, a vetting inspector or a buyer's surveyor asks for
ExaminationOpening up and internal examination, dismantling where required, measurement of wear-down and clearances, pressure and hydraulic testing, non-destructive examination where called for, and functional testing of safety and control devices under working conditions
AttendanceA class surveyor attends, examines and credits the item. Crediting is the act that discharges it; work done without crediting has not been surveyed
Chief Engineer's surveySubject to approval, defined items may be examined and recorded by a suitably qualified Chief Engineer, with the records confirmed by the surveyor at his next attendance. It is a delegation of examination, not of judgement
Approved planned maintenance schemeWhere class approves the ship's PMS, maintenance carried out under it is credited against continuous survey items, subject to periodical audit of the scheme and its records
PostponementAn item may be postponed only with class agreement and within limits set by the society. Postponement is a decision, not a delay
Overdue itemsAn item passing its due date without credit or postponement becomes overdue and attracts a condition of class or equivalent recommendation with a date by which it must be discharged

The commercial edge is the condition of class. Class status drives the hull and machinery cover, the P&I entry and the charterer's willingness to fix the ship. An overdue continuous survey item is therefore not an engineering irritation; it is a trading exposure, and that is why the survey status report is managed as a live document rather than filed.

5. The impact of advanced technologies

Technology has changed how the evidence is produced, not what has to be proved:

  • Condition monitoring. Vibration analysis, lubricating oil analysis, thermography and thermodynamic performance monitoring give a quantified picture of a machine that has not been opened. Where class approves a condition-based arrangement, that evidence can substitute for scheduled opening-up.
  • The shift from calendar to condition. The scheme's logic moves from open it because it is due to open it because the data says so. Unnecessary dismantling is itself a source of failure, so this is a genuine reliability gain and not only a cost one.
  • Remote survey. Streamed video, photographic and documentary evidence assessed ashore, established at scale from 2020 onward and by this sitting a routine route for a defined subset of items. It removes the surveyor's travel from the critical path.
  • Remote inspection techniques. Drones and crawlers for spaces that would otherwise need staging; borescopes and endoscopes in place of opening up.
  • Digital survey status. Live, shared status data replacing a periodically issued document, so that ship, office and society see the same forward schedule.
  • Data and analytics. Continuous machinery data trended against a baseline, with anomaly detection prompting attention before an alarm point is reached; and electronic certificates removing the paper-currency problem entirely.

The limits are worth a mark. None of this changes the interval, and none of it displaces the surveyor's judgement — the technology produces evidence and the society decides what it discharges. Data quality, calibration and a trustworthy baseline are preconditions, not by-products; and remote survey is accepted for a defined subset of items, never as a general substitute for attendance.

(b) Challenges, and the solution to each

6. Enumerated challenges

  1. Due-date tracking across a five-year rotation. Dozens of items with individual dates, across crew changes and superintendent changes; items are missed on paper long before they are missed in the engine room.
  2. Commercial schedule and access. Short port stays, continuous cargo work and voyage patterns that never coincide with a due item. Machinery that cannot be taken out of service cannot be opened.
  3. Surveyor availability. The ship is where the surveyor is not. Attendance cost, travel and waiting time push owners toward postponement.
  4. Crew competence and continuity. The Chief Engineer's survey depends on an individual approval, and a relief who does not hold it stops that route dead. Short tenures break the continuity the scheme assumes.
  5. Record quality and integrity. Late entries, missing measurements, photographs without context, PMS records completed retrospectively. An audit that finds this withdraws the credit and can withdraw the scheme approval.
  6. Spare parts and lead time. An item opened without the parts to close it becomes an unplanned immobilisation.
  7. Unnecessary opening-up. Dismantling a healthy machine to satisfy a calendar introduces assembly error, contamination and gasket and fastener failures.
  8. Condition-monitoring data quality. Sensor drift, no baseline, alarm flooding, and nobody aboard trained to interpret a spectrum.
  9. Alignment with statutory and docking windows. Continuous items, harmonized statutory surveys and the docking cycle pull in different directions and can produce three separate outages.
  10. Change of class or ownership. Survey status transfers with the ship, and an incomplete or disputed transfer surfaces as a block of overdue items on day one.
  11. Overdue items becoming a condition of class. The consequence of every failure above, and the one with commercial teeth.

7. Matched solutions

ChallengeProposed solution
Due-date trackingA live survey-status matrix held by ship and office together, reviewed at a fixed monthly point, with a rolling twelve-month look-ahead and named ownership of each due item. Digital class status data rather than a periodic document
Commercial schedulePlan due items against the trading pattern, not the calendar - identify the long port stays, ballast legs and lay-up windows a year out and place the heavy items there. Bring the commercial operator into the survey plan
Surveyor availabilityBatch items so that one attendance credits several; use remote survey for the items the society accepts remotely; book attendance against the plan rather than against the due date
Crew competenceObtain and maintain Chief Engineer survey approval as a fleet standard rather than an individual accident; hand over the survey status formally at every relief; train on measurement and recording, not only on maintenance
Record qualityRecord at the machine and at the time - measurements, photographs with identification, the actual clearances against limits. Internal audit the records before the society does
SparesA critical-spares policy driven by the due-item look-ahead; confirm parts on board before opening up; hold the wear parts for the next twelve months of due items
Unnecessary opening-upMove to an approved planned maintenance scheme, and where the machinery and the data support it to a condition-based arrangement, so that opening-up follows evidence
Data qualityEstablish a baseline when the machine is known good; calibrate and verify sensors; set alarm limits that mean something; train one competent analyst per ship and support the ship from a shore analysis capability
Window alignmentBuild one integrated plan carrying continuous items, harmonized statutory surveys and the docking cycle together, and align the anniversary dates so the three windows overlap rather than compete
Change of class or ownershipObtain and reconcile the full survey status before transfer; treat unreconciled items as outstanding until the new society credits them
Conditions of classTreat any condition of class as a bridge-and-office level item with a named owner and a date, and close it early rather than at its limit

The single point that ties limb (b) together: almost every challenge above is a planning and information failure rather than an engineering one. The machinery is rarely the obstacle; the obstacle is that nobody knew the item was due until the window had passed. The solutions therefore cluster on visibility, on look-ahead and on approved schemes that let evidence already being generated aboard do the survey's work.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Limb (a) prints eight marks against four tasks — significance, objectives, methodologies, and the impact of advanced technologies. That is the tightest ratio on the paper and it dictates the shape: four visibly separate blocks, each short. A candidate who writes eight flowing paragraphs on how useful CSM is has answered one of the four tasks and will be marked on one.

The objectives go in a numbered list and the methodology in a table for the same reason: an examiner scanning for four discrete deliverables must be able to find each one without reading for it. The technology section closes on its limits, because a section that only enthuses reads as a brochure and the mark is for judgement.

Limb (b) prints the verb “enumerate”, which is an instruction about form. Enumerate the challenges — numbered, one line each — then pair each with its solution. Pairing them in a two-column table is what makes the second half of the limb legible; a candidate who lists eleven challenges and then eight unlabelled solutions forces the examiner to do the matching.

The distinction the whole question turns on

CSM does not extend the interval. It distributes it.

SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 fixes a machinery renewal survey at intervals not exceeding five years. The continuous arrangement changes nothing about that. It divides the surveyable items into groups with individual due dates so that about a fifth fall due each year and every item is still examined inside the five years. A candidate who writes that CSM lets a ship go longer between surveys has said the opposite of what the scheme does.

The second distinction: CSM is a class arrangement, not a statutory one. SOLAS sets the interval; class rules distribute it; the Administration accepts it through the recognized organization. And the third: a survey regime is not a maintenance regime. CSM says when class must see the item. The planned maintenance system says when the ship overhauls it. They are connected — an approved PMS lets the maintenance credit the survey — but they are not the same thing, and Q6 on this paper is about the other one.

Three regimes, one machine

RegimeQuestion it answersWho owns it
Continuous survey of machineryWhen must class see this item?Classification society, under the SOLAS interval
Planned maintenance systemWhen must we overhaul it?The company, under the ISM Code
Condition monitoringWhat state is it actually in?The ship, feeding both of the above

An approved PMS is the bridge: it lets the middle column discharge the left one. A condition-based arrangement lets the right column discharge the middle one. Explaining that chain in one sentence is worth more than another paragraph on benefits.

Common mistakes

  • Saying CSM extends the survey interval. It does not; it distributes the same five years.
  • Treating CSM as a statutory requirement. It is a classification arrangement accepted in discharge of the statutory survey.
  • Confusing CSM with the planned maintenance system, or using the two terms interchangeably.
  • Answering “significance” and stopping — leaving objectives, methodology and technology unwritten when the limb names all four.
  • Writing about hull surveys, the enhanced survey programme or docking. The question says machinery.
  • Omitting crediting. Work done and not credited has not been surveyed.
  • Omitting the condition of class, which is the commercial consequence of the whole scheme.
  • Listing technologies with no statement of their limits, so the answer reads as sales material.
  • In limb (b), giving challenges without solutions, or solutions that do not match the challenges listed.
  • Proposing “better planning” as a solution. Say what plan, held by whom, reviewed when.

Examiner traps

  • “Does continuous survey let you go more than five years?” No. The interval is unchanged; only the distribution changes.
  • “Who approves the arrangement?” The classification society, acting for the Administration as recognized organization.
  • “Roughly what proportion falls due each year?” About a fifth, which is what a five-year cycle divided into annual tranches means.
  • “Can the Chief Engineer survey items himself?” Only defined items, only with approval, and the surveyor confirms the records. The examination is delegated; the judgement is not.
  • “What happens if an item goes overdue?” A condition of class or equivalent recommendation with a date for discharge, and class itself is at risk if it is not met.
  • “Your PMS is approved. Do you still need class attendance?” Yes — the scheme is audited periodically and the surveyor still confirms and credits.
  • “What is the difference between crediting and doing the work?” Crediting is the class act that discharges the item. Uncredited work leaves the item open.
  • “Can a survey be done remotely?” For a defined subset of items that the society accepts remotely — not as a general substitute for attendance.
  • “What is the risk of opening a healthy machine?” Assembly error, contamination, gasket and fastener failure. Unnecessary dismantling causes defects, which is the argument for condition-based work.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which SOLAS regulation sets the cargo ship machinery survey interval? Chapter I regulation 10 - not exceeding five years.
  • Which regulation requires the condition to be maintained after survey? Chapter I regulation 11.
  • Name three things a surveyor does at a continuous survey item. Opens and examines it, measures wear-down and clearances, and tests it under working conditions.
  • What document tells you what is due? The class survey status report.
  • What is a condition of class? A recorded requirement that specified work be completed by a stated date for class to be retained.
  • What must you have before you open the item? The spares to close it, and the item's due date confirmed.
  • Name three condition monitoring techniques. Vibration analysis, lubricating oil analysis and thermographic or performance monitoring.
  • Why is unnecessary opening-up a hazard? Because reassembly is where defects are introduced.
  • What happens to survey status if the ship changes class? It transfers with the ship and must be reconciled and credited by the new society.

Memory framework

For limb (a), four blocks in the order the limb names them: SIGNIFICANCE, OBJECTIVES, METHOD, TECHNOLOGY. Write the headings first, then fill them.

For the method, five words in the order a real item moves: LIST, DUE, OPEN, TEST, CREDIT — and one consequence word if it does not happen: OVERDUE.

For the technology, MONITOR, REMOTE, DATA, then one word of caution: LIMITS.

For limb (b), group the challenges under four heads and the solutions fall out by themselves: TIME (schedule, access, surveyor), PEOPLE (competence, continuity, records), PARTS (spares, unnecessary opening-up), PAPER (tracking, transfer, conditions of class).

Regulation and source map

  • SOLAS chapter I regulation 6 — survey and inspection; the authority under which a recognized organization acts. Regulation 10 — surveys of hull, machinery and equipment of cargo ships; renewal at intervals not exceeding five years, with intermediate and annual surveys. Regulation 11 — maintenance of condition after survey.
  • A.1156(32) — Survey Guidelines under the Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, 2021. These are the operative guidelines at this sitting. The 33rd Assembly successor was adopted 6 December 2023, one month after the examination, and does not belong in this answer.
  • ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) (in force 1 January 2015) — section 10, maintenance of the ship and equipment, which generates the maintenance evidence an approved planned maintenance scheme relies on.
  • Classification society rules and the industry's unified requirements — these, and not SOLAS, are what actually constitute the continuous survey arrangement, the Chief Engineer's survey and the approved planned maintenance scheme. No rule or unified requirement is cited by number in this answer; see the uncertainty section.
  • India at this sitting: the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the rules made under it.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The statutory frame is primary; the scheme itself is not, and the split is declared rather than hidden.

The SOLAS chapter I survey obligations and the operative Assembly survey guidelines are cited at identity level from instruments whose identity and amendment status are recorded in the True Source corpus. The continuous survey arrangement is a classification instrument, and the corpus holds no classification layer — its class directory returns nothing for continuous survey of machinery, and its reference resolver holds 320 entries of which every one is MARPOL Annex VI. The scheme is therefore described at authoritative-secondary level.

The consequences are deliberate. No unified requirement number is attributed and no class rule is quoted. No numerical value is given for a permitted postponement, a crediting window, or the limit up to which a Chief Engineer may survey — those differ between societies and no held source records them. The five-year cycle and the approximate annual fifth are stated, because they follow from the statutory interval itself, which is primary.

The condition-based and remote survey arrangements are described generically: no society, notation or acceptance criterion is named. The technology section is engineering judgement and industry practice, attributed as such.

Currency. The one live temporal edge is the survey guidelines. A.1156(32) governs at this sitting; its 33rd Assembly successor was adopted 6 December 2023, one month after the examination, and every later paper in the corpus correctly cites the successor. That is exactly the reversal a later editor would import, and it is the reason this section exists.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  6. __________Enumerated challenges
  7. __________Matched solutions
Flashcards 10

A classification-society arrangement under which the machinery items that would otherwise be examined together at the five-yearly renewal survey are divided into groups with individual due dates spread across the cycle, so that about a fifth fall due each year and every item is still examined within the same five years.

The definition contains the distinction the question turns on - distribution, not extension.

No. SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 fixes the renewal interval at not exceeding five years and continuous survey does not touch it. Every item is still examined inside the five years; only the distribution of the examinations changes.

It is the commonest single error on this question and it inverts the whole scheme.

Continuous survey is a classification survey regime and answers when class must SEE the item. The planned maintenance system is the company's regime under the ISM Code and answers when the ship must OVERHAUL it. They meet where class approves the PMS, because approved maintenance can then be credited against continuous survey items, subject to periodical audit.

The two are routinely used interchangeably, and this paper sets both - Q1 and Q6.

Listed in the item register with a group and a due date; planned against the trading pattern with spares confirmed; opened and internally examined, clearances and wear-down measured, pressure tested where required and functionally tested under working conditions; then CREDITED by the surveyor, which is the act that discharges it. If the date passes without credit or agreed postponement the item goes overdue and attracts a condition of class with a discharge date.

List, due, open, test, credit - and the consequence if it does not happen.

Chapter I regulation 6 - survey and inspection, and the authority under which a recognized organization acts. Regulation 10 - surveys of the hull, machinery and equipment of cargo ships, with renewal at intervals not exceeding five years and intermediate and annual surveys inside the cycle. Regulation 11 - maintenance of the condition after survey.

The statutory frame is the only primary material in the answer, and it must be attributed correctly.

Not unless it was credited. Crediting by the attending surveyor - or confirmation of an approved Chief Engineer's record, or credit under an approved planned maintenance scheme - is what discharges the item. Work carried out without crediting leaves the item open and it will still go overdue on its due date.

The distinction between doing the work and discharging the survey is a standard oral question.

Changed: how the evidence is produced. Condition monitoring quantifies a machine that has not been opened; condition-based arrangements let that evidence substitute for scheduled opening-up; remote survey allows defined items to be assessed ashore from streamed and documentary evidence; drones, crawlers and borescopes replace staging and dismantling; digital status data and analytics replace a periodic report. Not changed: the interval, the surveyor's judgement, and the need for calibrated data against a trustworthy baseline. Remote survey covers a defined subset of items, never attendance generally.

The mark in this part of limb (a) is for balance - naming the limits, not only the tools.

About one fifth. It follows directly from the arithmetic of the scheme: the whole register must be covered within the five-year cycle set by SOLAS chapter I regulation 10, so dividing it into annual tranches puts roughly a fifth in each year.

It is the only safe number in the answer, because it follows from the statutory interval rather than from any society's rule.

A recorded requirement that specified work be completed by a stated date for class to be retained - the consequence of an item going past its due date without credit or agreed postponement. It matters because hull and machinery cover, the P&I entry and a charterer's willingness to fix the ship all follow class status, so an overdue survey item is a trading exposure and not just an engineering one.

It is where the whole scheme acquires commercial teeth, and it is what limb (b) is ultimately about.

TIME - schedule, access, surveyor availability: plan against the trading pattern a year out, batch items into one attendance, use remote survey where accepted. PEOPLE - competence, continuity, records: fleet-standard Chief Engineer approval, formal handover of survey status, recording at the machine. PARTS - spares lead time and unnecessary opening-up: spares driven by the due-item look-ahead, and an approved PMS so opening-up follows evidence. PAPER - tracking, class transfer, conditions of class: one live shared status matrix, reconciliation before transfer, conditions closed early.

Limb (b) is eight marks and 'enumerate' asks for countable items; four heads make eleven challenges recallable under pressure.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Continuous Survey of Machinery distributes the five-year interval; it does not extend it. SOLAS I/10 requires a cargo ship machinery renewal survey at intervals not exceeding five years; I/6 is the survey obligation; I/11 requires the condition to be maintained after survey. CSM is a classification arrangement, not a statutory one, and a survey regime, not a maintenance one. Items are grouped with individual due dates so about a fifth fall due each year and all are examined inside the cycle. Method: list, due, open, test, credit — and crediting by the surveyor is what discharges the item. Chief Engineer's survey for defined items with approval, confirmed by the surveyor. Approved PMS credits maintenance against survey items, subject to audit. An item passing its date becomes overdue and attracts a condition of class with a discharge date — and insurance, P&I and charter all follow class. Technology: condition monitoring, condition-based arrangements, remote survey, drones and borescopes, digital status, analytics — but the interval is unchanged, the surveyor still judges, and remote covers a defined subset only. Challenges cluster as TIME, PEOPLE, PARTS, PAPER, and the solutions are visibility, look-ahead and approved schemes. Operative survey guidelines at this sitting: A.1156(32), not the 33rd Assembly successor adopted one month later.
Keywords

Critical numbers
  • SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 - machinery renewal survey at intervals NOT EXCEEDING FIVE YEARS
  • Roughly one fifth of continuous survey items fall due each year, which is what a five-year cycle divided annually means
  • A.1156(32) - the 32nd Assembly survey guidelines, 2021, operative at this November 2023 sitting
  • ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92), in force 1 January 2015 - the latest Code amendment at this sitting
  • Remote survey established at scale by classification societies from 2020 onward
Critical regulation
SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 is the provision the whole scheme is built on: it requires the hull, machinery and equipment of a cargo ship to be surveyed on renewal at intervals not exceeding five years, with intermediate and annual surveys inside the cycle. Continuous Survey of Machinery does not touch that interval — it distributes it across the cycle item by item under classification society rules, accepted by the Administration through the recognized organization acting under regulation 6, with regulation 11 requiring the condition to be maintained after survey.
Major trap
Writing that continuous survey extends the interval between surveys. It does the opposite of relaxing anything: the five-year deadline in SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 is untouched and every item is still examined inside it — what changes is that the items are staggered rather than shown together. The paired traps are treating CSM as a statutory requirement when it is a classification arrangement accepted in discharge of the statutory survey, and confusing it with the planned maintenance system, which is the company's ISM-driven overhaul regime and the subject of Q6 on this same paper.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q6 - the planned maintenance system and the ISM Code, which is where the maintenance evidence behind a continuous scheme is generated · QP2311 Q3 - the on-hire survey, a commercial inspection that is often confused with the class survey described here

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

The idea underneath this question is simple and has nothing to do with regulations. However big an engine is, what really limits it is how fast the moving parts are sliding and how little time each burn has to finish. Revolutions per minute do not tell you that, because a long-stroke engine moves its piston much further in each turn than a short-stroke one does. So engineers use the average speed of the piston itself: in one turn the piston covers the stroke twice, so that average is simply twice the stroke times the turning speed. Once you have it, two things follow. First, the power an engine makes is the amount burnt in each stroke multiplied by how fast the strokes come — a pressure part and a speed part — so slowing the piston takes power straight out, and because a propeller resists much harder the faster it turns, a small slowdown removes a surprisingly large amount of fuel. Second, and this is where most people go wrong, slowing down forever does not keep saving. Everything on the engine — the injectors, the compression, the turbocharger — was matched to one design speed, and far below it the air supply falls away, the spray is coarse and the fuel burns badly. So there is a best band, not a best minimum. The last part of the question is really about honesty: on an old ship you cannot find that band until you can trust your own instruments, and you cannot blame the engine until you have ruled out a dirty hull, a rough propeller and bad fuel.

Knowledge map
Mean Piston Speed - Fuel Consumption, Rpm, Combustion and a Method for an Old Ship
  1. 1Definition, formula and units
    • mean piston speed is the AVERAGE speed of the piston over a complete revolution
    • in one revolution the piston travels TWICE the stroke - down and up
    • c_m = 2 x L x N with L in metres and N in rev/s, giving metres per second; or 2LN/60 with N in rev/min
  2. 2Why it governs fuel consumption
    • from the definition of mean effective pressure, indicated power = mean effective pressure x swept volume x cycles per second x number of cylinders
    • writing swept volume as piston area x stroke and grouping the stroke with the speed gives POWER PROPORTIONAL TO p_m x A x c_m x i
    • power is therefore a PRESSURE term times a SPEED term, and mean piston speed is the speed term
  3. 3The rpm relation
    • for a given engine the stroke is fixed, so mean piston speed is DIRECTLY PROPORTIONAL to rpm
    • across different engines the relation breaks - equal rpm with unequal strokes is unequal duty
    • this is the whole point of the long-stroke engine: a longer stroke reaches the design mean piston speed at LOWER rpm
  4. 4Mean piston speed and ideal combustion
    • ideal combustion needs four things together - enough AIR, good ATOMISATION, adequate TURBULENCE and enough TIME
    • mean piston speed sets the time directly and disturbs the others indirectly
    • HIGH piston speed - short time per cycle, so injection, ignition delay and burn-out must fit into fewer milliseconds
  5. 5Establish the baseline, then prove the measurements
    • ON AN OLD SHIP THE DATA IS MORE SUSPECT THAN THE ENGINE, and nothing else is worth doing until the instruments are trusted
    • collect the design baseline - shop and sea trial records, the maker's SFOC curve, rated power and speed, and the STROKE; calculate design mean piston speed at rated and service output
    • collect the ship's history - running hours, overhauls, previous performance reports, docking, propeller polishing and hull cleaning dates
  6. 6Run a structured performance test and reduce the data
    • choose steady test points across the intended speed range, each held genuinely steady in stable weather at constant draught and trim, on the same fuel
    • record a full set at each point - shaft speed and power, fuel rate, scavenge air pressure and temperature, exhaust temperatures before and after turbine, turbocharger speed, compression and maximum pressures per cylinder, ambient air and seawater temperature, draught, trim, wind and sea
    • correct to reference ambient conditions before comparing anything to shop trial data
  7. 7Separate the engine from the hull, the propeller and the fuel
    • a high consumption at a given mean piston speed may have nothing to do with the engine, and this is where an old ship differs from a new one
    • hull and propeller - fouling and roughness shift the whole power-speed relation, so the engine turns faster for the same ship speed
    • establish hull and propeller condition from trend, inspection and cleaning history BEFORE concluding the engine is inefficient
  8. 8Bound the answer by the real constraints
    • barred speed range from torsional vibration - a band of shaft speed that may not be run continuously
    • minimum load at which the turbocharger sustains itself and the auxiliary blowers cut out, and the maker's minimum continuous load and low-load instructions
    • cylinder lubrication - feed rate and cylinder oil base number matched to the load and the fuel actually burnt
  9. 9Decide, implement, and keep it under review
    • choose an operating BAND, not a single figure - the range that minimises fuel per mile subject to every constraint
    • convert it into a shaft speed instruction the bridge can follow, with the barred range excluded and the low-load limit stated
    • record it in the SEEMP and brief the engineers and the master on the reasoning

Memory cue. One formula: TWICE THE STROKE (1). Two consequences: POWER = PRESSURE × SPEED (2) and CUBE PER HOUR, SQUARE PER MILE (2). Two failures: too fast is TIME, too slow is AIR (4). Six steps: BASELINE, CALIBRATE, TEST, REDUCE, SEPARATE, DECIDE (5, 6, 7, 9).

1. Definition, formula and units

  • in one revolution the piston travels TWICE the stroke - down and up

  • monitor and trend continuously, with a monthly performance report against the curves so drift shows as drift
  • re-establish the baseline after any step change - hull cleaning, propeller polish, docking, major overhaul or a change of fuel
  • escalate to hardware only if the mismatch is structural - turbocharger cut-out, variable turbine area, tuning kits, re-pitch or de-rating

Model written answer

(a) Mean piston speed and its significance for fuel consumption

1. Definition, formula and units

Mean piston speed is the average speed of the piston over a complete revolution. In one revolution the piston travels twice the stroke — once down, once up — so:

FormExpressionUnits
With speed in rev/scm = 2 × L × NL in metres, N in rev/s, cm in m/s
With speed in rev/mincm = 2LN / 60N in rev/min

It is an average, not a maximum: the piston is momentarily stationary at each dead centre and reaches roughly half as much again as the mean near mid-stroke. As orders of magnitude, a large low-speed two-stroke runs about 8 to 8.5 m/s at its rated output, a medium-speed four-stroke about 9 to 11 m/s, and high-speed engines higher again.

Why engineers use it instead of rpm. Revolutions per minute compare nothing: 100 rpm on a three-metre stroke and 100 rpm on a one-metre stroke are entirely different duties. Mean piston speed puts every engine on one scale, because it measures what actually wears, heats and limits the machine — how fast the rubbing surfaces move and how little time each process has. It is the honest measure of how hard an engine is working mechanically.

2. Why it governs fuel consumption

The power identity. From the definition of mean effective pressure, indicated power is mean effective pressure × swept volume × cycles per second × number of cylinders. Writing swept volume as piston area × stroke, and grouping the stroke with the speed, gives:

Power ∝ pm × A × cm × i

So for a given engine, power is the product of a pressure term and a speed term. The pressure term is how much is burnt per stroke; the speed term is mean piston speed. Fuel burnt per hour is power × specific fuel oil consumption, so mean piston speed sits directly inside the consumption equation.

The cube law amplifies it. For a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged hull and weather conditions, delivered power varies approximately as the cube of shaft speed. Since mean piston speed is proportional to shaft speed on a directly coupled engine:

  • Power ∝ cm3, so fuel per hour falls very steeply as mean piston speed is reduced. A 10% reduction in mean piston speed removes roughly 27% of the power.
  • But the ship also goes slower, so the voyage takes longer. Consumption per mile falls roughly as the square, not the cube — still a large saving, and it is the figure that matters commercially.
  • That single distinction — per hour against per mile — is what makes slow steaming worth doing and is the most valuable sentence available in limb (a).

But lower is not always better, and this is the discriminating point. Specific fuel oil consumption is not constant. It passes through a minimum in the upper-middle part of the load range and rises at both ends:

  • At high mean piston speed — mechanical friction losses rise with piston speed, so mechanical efficiency falls; thermal loading, wear rate and cylinder oil demand all rise; and combustion has less time to complete.
  • At low mean piston speed — compression pressure and temperature fall, the turbocharger is off its matched point so scavenge air pressure and air-fuel ratio fall, injection pressure and atomisation degrade, and the engine burns dirtier for every kilowatt it makes.

The consequence for the ship: there is an optimum band of mean piston speed, not a monotonic rule. Below it, the cube-law saving is being eaten by a rising specific consumption and by fouling, deposits and maintenance cost. Finding that band is exactly what limb (c) asks for.

(b) Relation to rpm and to ideal combustion

3. The rpm relation

  • For a given engine the stroke is fixed, so mean piston speed is directly proportional to rpm. Doubling rpm doubles mean piston speed exactly.
  • Across engines the relation breaks. Two engines at the same rpm have quite different mean piston speeds if their strokes differ — which is why rpm alone tells you nothing about duty.
  • This is the whole point of the long-stroke engine. Lengthening the stroke lets an engine reach its design mean piston speed — the mechanical duty it was built for — at lower rpm. Lower rpm permits a larger, slower-turning propeller of higher open-water efficiency, which is why modern low-speed engines are long-stroke and turn slowly.
  • Stroke-to-bore ratio is the design lever. A higher ratio buys propeller efficiency and combustion time at the cost of engine height and mass.
  • On a controllable-pitch installation the proportionality survives but the propeller law does not: shaft speed may be held constant while pitch varies, so mean piston speed stays fixed while power changes. The load, not the piston speed, then moves along the SFOC curve.

4. Mean piston speed and ideal combustion

Ideal combustion needs four things together: enough air, well atomised fuel, adequate turbulence, and enough time. Mean piston speed sets the fourth directly and disturbs the other three indirectly.

Mean piston speed HIGHMean piston speed LOW
Time per cycleShort. Injection, ignition delay and burn-out must all fit into fewer millisecondsLong. Ample time, so time is not the constraint
Combustion qualityAfterburning - combustion continues into expansion, so heat is released too late to do useful workPoor atomisation and low compression temperature give slow, incomplete burning
Air supplyTurbocharger well matched; air is normally adequateTurbocharger off its matched point; scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio fall; auxiliary blowers cut in
SymptomsHigh exhaust temperatures, thermal overload, high wear and cylinder oil consumptionFouled gas ways, sooty exhaust, deposits on rings, liner and turbine; risk of scavenge fire
EfficiencyFalls - mechanical friction and late heat releaseFalls - incomplete combustion and unfavourable pressure development

The unifying statement. The maker matches injection timing and rate, nozzle geometry, compression ratio, turbocharger and scavenge arrangement to a design mean piston speed. Combustion is closest to ideal at that design point, and degrades in either direction away from it. That is why an engine run persistently far below its design point needs re-matching — turbocharger cut-out, variable turbine geometry, tuning kits, or de-rating — rather than merely being throttled back and left.

It is worth adding that combustion time is not the only casualty of high piston speed: the gas exchange itself is time-limited, so scavenging becomes less complete, residual gas rises and the following cycle starts with less fresh air.

(c) Methodology on an old ship - ascertaining optimum fuel use against desired mean piston speed

5. Establish the baseline, then prove the measurements

On an old ship the data is more suspect than the engine. Nothing below is worth doing until the instruments are trusted, and that is the order the answer is written in.

  1. Collect the design baseline. Shop trial and sea trial records, the maker's SFOC curve, rated power and speed, and the stroke. Calculate the design mean piston speed at rated output and at the service rating, so there is a target to compare against.
  2. Collect the ship's history. Running hours, overhaul records, previous performance reports, docking and propeller polishing dates, and the last hull cleaning. An old ship's consumption has drifted for reasons that are recorded somewhere.
  3. Calibrate and prove the instruments. Tachometer, torsionmeter if fitted, fuel flowmeters, temperature and pressure sensors, scavenge and exhaust instrumentation, and the indicator or electronic pressure-measurement equipment. Cross-check flowmeter against tank soundings.
  4. Fix the fuel measurement in mass, not volume. Density at 15 °C from the bunker analysis, temperature correction applied, and the daily figure reported as tonnes of a known net calorific quality — otherwise every later comparison is contaminated by fuel quality and temperature.

6. Run a structured performance test and reduce the data

  1. Choose steady test points. A series of shaft speeds spanning the intended range, each held genuinely steady in stable weather, at constant draught and trim, on the same fuel, with no auto-pilot hunting and no cargo work.
  2. Record a full set at each point. Shaft speed and power, fuel rate, scavenge air pressure and temperature, exhaust temperatures before and after turbine, turbocharger speed, compression and maximum pressures per cylinder, ambient air and seawater temperature, draught, trim, wind and sea state.
  3. Correct to reference ambient conditions before comparing anything to shop trial data. An uncorrected comparison against a test-bed figure is meaningless.
  4. Reduce each point to the two numbers that matter. Compute cm = 2LN for each test point, and compute specific fuel oil consumption in g/kWh and fuel per nautical mile.
  5. Plot them. SFOC against mean piston speed, and fuel per mile against mean piston speed. The optimum is read off the second curve, not the first — the ship is paid to move cargo, not to make kilowatts.

7. Separate the engine from the hull, the propeller and the fuel

This is where an old ship differs from a new one, and where most answers stop too early. A high consumption at a given mean piston speed may have nothing to do with the engine:

  • Hull and propeller. Fouling and propeller roughness shift the whole power-speed relation, so the engine must turn faster for the same ship speed. Establish hull and propeller condition — performance trend, inspection, polishing and cleaning history — before concluding the engine is inefficient.
  • Engine condition. Worn liners and rings, leaking exhaust valves, drifted fuel pump timing, worn or fouled injectors, a fouled turbocharger or air cooler, and fouled scavenge and gas ways. Overhaul and clean, then re-test; otherwise the optimum found is the optimum of a dirty engine.
  • Fuel quality. Calorific value, viscosity at injection temperature, and catalytic fines all move consumption independently of piston speed. This is where the fuel oil analysis report feeds in.
  • Loading condition. Draught and trim change resistance materially. Compare like with like or correct for it.

8. Bound the answer by the real constraints

  • Barred speed range from torsional vibration — a whole band of shaft speed, and therefore of mean piston speed, that may not be run continuously.
  • Minimum load at which the turbocharger sustains itself and the auxiliary blowers cut out; and the maker's stated minimum continuous load and low-load running instructions.
  • Cylinder lubrication — the feed rate and cylinder oil base number must be matched to the load and the fuel actually being burnt, or a low-load optimum is bought at the price of liner condition.
  • Manoeuvring, weather and schedule margin — a ship at its economic optimum must still be able to accelerate, keep steerage and make the berth.
  • Commercial obligations — charterparty speed and consumption warranties, laycan and schedule integrity. The optimum the engine wants may not be the optimum the charter permits.
  • Regulatory — the ship's SEEMP, with Part III mandatory since 1 January 2023, is where the chosen operating envelope is recorded and monitored. 2023 is the first CII data year and it is still running at this sitting, so this year's operating decisions are being logged into a rating that has not yet been issued to anybody.

9. Decide, implement, and keep it under review

  1. Choose an operating band, not a single figure — the mean piston speed range that minimises fuel per mile subject to every constraint above.
  2. Convert it into an order the bridge can follow: a shaft speed instruction, with the barred range excluded and the low-load limit stated.
  3. Record it in the SEEMP and brief the engineers and the master on why it is what it is.
  4. Monitor and trend continuously — a monthly performance report against the curves, so drift shows up as drift rather than as a surprise.
  5. Re-establish the baseline after any step change — hull cleaning, propeller polish, docking, major overhaul or a change of fuel.
  6. Escalate to hardware only if the mismatch is structural. If the ship genuinely now runs far below the engine's design mean piston speed, the answer is re-matching — turbocharger cut-out, variable turbine area, tuning kits, propeller re-pitch or de-rating — and that is a technical and commercial project, not a bridge order.

The closing judgement. The question asks for optimum use of fuel oil against a desired mean piston speed, and the honest answer is that the optimum is a negotiated band rather than a calculated point: the engine wants its design duty, the propeller law wants low revolutions, the charter wants the schedule and the liner wants a stable thermal load. A Chief Engineer joining an old ship earns his marks by measuring before deciding, by proving the instruments before blaming the engine, and by expressing the result in fuel per mile.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 5 + 5 + 6, and the shape follows them exactly. Limb (a) is worth five marks and prints the verb “illustrate”, which asks for the formula and the mechanism, not an essay: definition and formula first, then the two reasons it governs fuel — the power identity and the cube law — then the qualification that lower is not always better. Limb (b) is also five and has two named halves, rpm and combustion, so it gets one section each. Limb (c) carries the most marks and prints “formulate a methodology”, which is an instruction to produce numbered steps in a defensible order.

The single most valuable structural decision is in limb (c): instrumentation and fuel measurement come first. On an old ship the data is less trustworthy than the machinery, and a methodology that starts by testing the engine is a methodology that will produce a confident wrong answer.

The second is that the optimum is read off fuel per mile, not fuel per hour and not SFOC. The ship is paid to move cargo. A candidate who plots only SFOC has optimised the engine and not the voyage.

The three relationships to hold

RelationshipStatementWhat it is used for
Geometrycm = 2LNConverts any shaft speed into the engine's real mechanical duty
PowerPower ∝ pm × A × cm × iShows that power is a pressure term times a speed term, and mean piston speed is the speed term
PropellerPower ∝ cm3 (fixed pitch, unchanged hull and weather)Converts a small speed reduction into a large power reduction - the arithmetic of slow steaming

And the qualification that goes with the third: per hour the saving follows the cube; per mile it follows roughly the square, because the voyage takes longer. Get that pair right and limb (a) is secure.

Why the lowest mean piston speed is not the cheapest

This is the discriminating idea in the whole question. Two effects run in opposite directions as the engine is slowed:

  • Power demand falls as the cube. This is the saving, and it is large.
  • Specific fuel oil consumption rises once the engine is well below its matched point — lower compression pressure and temperature, turbocharger off its matching, falling scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio, degraded atomisation.

Below some point the second effect starts eating the first, and the cost of deposits, fouling, scavenge fires and extra maintenance is on top of it. So the deliverable of limb (c) is a band, and a candidate who writes “the slower the better” has missed the question.

Common mistakes

  • Giving the formula as LN rather than 2LN. The piston travels the stroke twice per revolution.
  • Quoting mean piston speed in m/min, or mixing rev/min into the rev/s form without dividing by 60.
  • Calling it the maximum piston speed. It is a mean; the peak near mid-stroke is roughly half as much again.
  • Saying fuel per mile falls as the cube. Per hour follows the cube; per mile follows roughly the square.
  • Treating SFOC as constant, and therefore concluding that the slowest speed is always the cheapest.
  • Answering limb (b) on rpm only and never reaching combustion, when the limb names both.
  • Discussing only the high piston speed case in combustion. The low-speed case — poor atomisation, turbocharger mismatch, fouling — is where the marks actually are for a slow-steaming era paper.
  • In limb (c), producing a list of things to look at rather than an ordered methodology with measurement first.
  • Forgetting that the ship is old: fouled hull, worn propeller, drifted pump timing and fouled turbocharger will all masquerade as an engine efficiency problem.
  • Omitting the constraints — barred speed range, minimum turbocharger load, cylinder oil feed rate, charterparty warranty.

Examiner traps

  • “Write the formula.” cm = 2LN, or 2LN/60 with N in rev/min. Metres per second.
  • “Why 2?” Because the piston covers the stroke twice in one revolution.
  • “Two engines both run at 100 rpm. Same duty?” Only if the strokes are equal. Mean piston speed, not rpm, is the measure.
  • “Typical value for a large two-stroke?” Of the order of 8 to 8.5 m/s at rated output; medium-speed four-strokes rather higher.
  • “Why are modern engines long-stroke?” To reach the design mean piston speed at lower rpm, which allows a larger, slower, more efficient propeller.
  • “You halve the revolutions. What happens to power?” Approximately one eighth, by the cube law — stating the fixed-pitch and unchanged-conditions assumption.
  • “Then why not run at dead slow permanently?” Because SFOC rises off the matched point, combustion degrades, deposits and fouling increase, and the schedule and charter still have to be met.
  • “What happens to combustion at high piston speed?” Less time per cycle, so afterburning — heat released too late in expansion, high exhaust temperature, thermal loading.
  • “And at low piston speed?” Turbocharger off its match, low scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio, poor atomisation, incomplete combustion, fouling and scavenge-fire risk.
  • “First thing you do on joining the old ship?” Prove the instruments and the fuel measurement. Not run a test.
  • “Which curve do you optimise?” Fuel per nautical mile against mean piston speed.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What are the units of mean piston speed? Metres per second.
  • How does mean piston speed relate to rpm? Directly proportional for a given engine, because the stroke is fixed.
  • What is stroke-to-bore ratio and why has it increased? It buys propeller efficiency and combustion time at the cost of engine height.
  • Name three measurements you take at each test point. Shaft speed and power, fuel rate, and scavenge air pressure and temperature - with maximum and compression pressures per cylinder.
  • Why correct to reference ambient conditions? Because ambient air and seawater temperature move the result, and the shop trial figure was taken at reference conditions.
  • What is afterburning? Combustion continuing into the expansion stroke, so the heat is released too late to do useful work.
  • Why do auxiliary blowers cut in? Because at low load the exhaust energy is insufficient to drive the turbocharger to the required scavenge pressure.
  • How does a fouled hull look on your data? As higher shaft speed and power for the same ship speed - an engine problem that is not an engine problem.
  • Where do you record the operating envelope you decide on? In the SEEMP.
  • What do you do if the ship is permanently mismatched to the engine? Re-match the hardware - turbocharger cut-out, variable turbine area, tuning kit, re-pitch or de-rate.

Memory framework

One formula and one word for it: TWICE THE STROKE, EVERY REVOLUTION — cm = 2LN.

For significance, two lines: POWER = PRESSURE × SPEED, and CUBE PER HOUR, SQUARE PER MILE.

For combustion, one word each way: too fast is TIME (afterburning); too slow is AIR (turbocharger off its match). Ideal combustion needs AIR, ATOMISATION, TURBULENCE, TIME — and mean piston speed sets the last and disturbs the first two.

For the methodology, six steps in a fixed order: BASELINE, CALIBRATE, TEST, REDUCE, SEPARATE, DECIDE. If it helps, remember that the first two are about trusting the numbers, the middle two about getting the numbers, and the last two about using them.

Regulation and source map

  • The physics is not regulatory and none of limbs (a) and (b) rests on an instrument. cm = 2LN is geometry; the power identity follows from the definition of mean effective pressure; the cube law is the propeller law for a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged hull and weather conditions.
  • MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 — the SEEMP, with Part III mandatory from 1 January 2023, is where the operating envelope decided in limb (c) is recorded and monitored. Annex VI at this sitting is the MEPC.328(76) revision, in force 1 November 2022.
  • MEPC.346(78), the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, are the operative guidelines at this sitting. Their 2024 successor is future and is not cited — see the uncertainty section.
  • CII applies from 1 January 2023, so 2023 is the first data year and it is still running at this examination. No ship has yet been issued a rating.
  • No engine-maker document is cited and no maker, engine type, power, rated speed or SFOC value is asserted anywhere in this answer.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

This is an engineering question and the provenance is declared as such.

The two relationships the answer rests on are derived, not asserted. cm = 2LN follows from the crank geometry, and the identity that power is proportional to mean effective pressure × piston area × mean piston speed × number of cylinders follows from the definition of mean effective pressure itself. Both derivations appear in the answer.

The numbers are the weak point and are marked as such. The typical mean piston speed ranges — about 8 to 8.5 m/s for a large two-stroke, 9 to 11 for a medium-speed four-stroke — are orders of magnitude from general engineering practice. The True Source corpus holds no engine-maker documentation, none was consulted, and no maker, engine type, rated power, rated speed or SFOC figure is presented as sourced. For the same reason the SFOC minimum is described as a region in the upper-middle load range rather than as a percentage of rated output, and no figure is given for a barred speed range, an auxiliary-blower cut-in load or a minimum continuous load — those are ship-specific and are read off the ship's own documentation, which is what the methodology instructs.

The cube law is an approximation and its assumptions are stated where it is used: fixed-pitch propeller, unchanged hull condition, unchanged weather. No claim is made for its accuracy on a fouled hull, in a seaway, or on a controllable-pitch installation off its combinator curve.

Currency. One reversal was applied. The corpus amendment register marks MEPC.346(78) as superseded by its 2024 successor and says not to cite it as current. That is correct today and wrong here: the supersession is a 2024 event and this sitting is November 2023, so the 2022 guidelines are the operative ones and the successor is not named. The register is present-tense, and its direction has to be checked on every consumption for a 2023 paper.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Definition, formula and units
  2. __________Why it governs fuel consumption
  3. __________The rpm relation
  4. __________Mean piston speed and ideal combustion
  5. __________Establish the baseline, then prove the measurements
  6. __________Run a structured performance test and reduce the data
  7. __________Separate the engine from the hull, the propeller and the fuel
  8. __________Bound the answer by the real constraints
  9. __________Decide, implement, and keep it under review
Flashcards 11

The average speed of the piston over a complete revolution. Because the piston covers the stroke twice per revolution, c_m = 2 x L x N, with L the stroke in metres and N the rotational speed in rev/s, giving metres per second; equivalently 2LN/60 with N in rev/min.

The factor of two is the mark, and it is the single most commonly dropped item in the whole question.

Because rpm compares nothing across engines. The same rpm on a three-metre stroke and a one-metre stroke are entirely different duties. Mean piston speed puts every engine on one scale by measuring what actually wears, heats and limits the machine - how fast the rubbing surfaces move and how little time each process has.

It is the conceptual point behind limb (b) and it explains the long-stroke engine in one line.

From the definition of mean effective pressure, indicated power = mean effective pressure x swept volume x cycles per second x number of cylinders. Swept volume is piston area x stroke; grouping the stroke with the speed gives power proportional to p_m x A x c_m x i. Power is therefore a pressure term times a speed term, and mean piston speed is the speed term.

It converts 'mean piston speed matters' from an assertion into a derivation, which is what limb (a) means by illustrate.

Per hour, power falls by roughly 27% by the cube law, for a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged hull and weather conditions. Per mile the saving is smaller - roughly the square - because the ship is slower and the voyage takes longer. The per-mile figure is the one that matters commercially.

Claiming the cube for per-mile consumption is the classic error in every slow-steaming answer.

Because specific fuel oil consumption is not constant. It passes through a minimum in the upper-middle load range and rises as the engine goes well below its matched point: compression pressure and temperature fall, the turbocharger is off its matching so scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio fall, and atomisation degrades. Deposits, fouling, scavenge-fire risk and maintenance cost rise on top of that. The deliverable is an optimum band, not a minimum.

It is the discriminating idea in the question and the thing limb (c) exists to find.

High: less time per cycle for injection, ignition delay and burn-out, so afterburning - heat released into expansion too late to do useful work - with high exhaust temperatures, thermal overload, wear and cylinder oil demand; gas exchange is also time-limited so scavenging is less complete. Low: the turbocharger is off its matched point, scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio fall, auxiliary blowers cut in, compression temperature and atomisation degrade, giving incomplete burning, fouled gas ways, deposits and scavenge-fire risk.

Most answers cover only the high case; the low case is where a slow-steaming era paper puts its marks.

Because a longer stroke lets the engine reach its design mean piston speed - the mechanical duty it was built for - at lower rpm. Lower rpm permits a larger, slower-turning propeller of higher open-water efficiency, and gives more time per cycle for combustion. The cost is engine height and mass.

It is the cleanest illustration that mean piston speed, not rpm, is the design constraint.

Prove the numbers. Collect the design baseline - shop and sea trial records, the SFOC curve, rated power and speed, and the stroke - and the ship's own history of overhauls, docking and hull cleaning. Then calibrate the tachometer, torsionmeter, flowmeters, pressure and temperature instrumentation and the indicator equipment, cross-check flowmeter against tank soundings, and fix fuel measurement in mass using density at 15 degrees C with temperature correction.

On an old ship the data is more suspect than the machinery; a methodology that starts with the engine produces a confident wrong answer.

Hold each test point genuinely steady in stable weather at constant draught and trim on the same fuel; record shaft speed and power, fuel rate, scavenge and exhaust conditions, turbocharger speed, compression and maximum pressures, and ambient conditions; correct to reference ambient conditions; then compute c_m = 2LN, SFOC in g/kWh and fuel per nautical mile for each point, and plot both against mean piston speed. Read the optimum off the fuel-per-mile curve.

The correction step and the choice of curve are the two places candidates lose the marks.

Hull fouling and propeller roughness, which shift the whole power-speed relation so the engine must turn faster for the same ship speed; fuel quality - calorific value, viscosity at injection temperature and catalytic fines; loading condition, since draught and trim change resistance materially; and instrument error, which on an old ship is the first suspect of all.

An old ship's consumption problem usually is not an engine problem, and saying so is what distinguishes a Chief Engineer's answer.

In the ship's SEEMP under MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, with Part III mandatory since 1 January 2023. The operative guidance at this sitting is the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.346(78). 2023 is the first CII data year and it is still running at this November sitting, so no ship has yet been issued a rating.

It is the only regulatory content in the question, and both the guidelines edition and the CII data year are easy to import wrongly from a later paper.

Quick revision
15-second recall
cm = 2LN — twice the stroke, every revolution, in m/s. It is a mean, not a maximum. Orders of magnitude: large two-stroke about 8 to 8.5 m/s at rated output, medium-speed four-stroke 9 to 11. It is used instead of rpm because it puts every engine on one scale of mechanical duty. Power ∝ pm × A × cm × i — a pressure term times a speed term. With a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged conditions, power ∝ cm3, so a 10% speed cut removes about 27% of the power — but the voyage lengthens, so fuel per mile falls roughly as the square. Lower is not automatically cheaper: SFOC has a minimum in the upper-middle load range and rises at both ends. Rpm: directly proportional at fixed stroke; long-stroke engines reach the design piston speed at lower rpm, allowing a larger, more efficient propeller. Combustion needs air, atomisation, turbulence, time — too fast costs time (afterburning, high exhaust temperature); too slow costs air (turbocharger off match, low scavenge pressure, poor atomisation, fouling, scavenge-fire risk). Old-ship method: baseline, calibrate, test, reduce, separate, decide — prove the instruments and the fuel mass measurement first, rule out hull, propeller, engine condition and fuel quality, respect the barred speed range and minimum turbocharger load, and read the optimum off fuel per mile. Record the envelope in the SEEMP; Part III has been mandatory since 1 January 2023 and 2023 is the first CII data year, still running.

Critical numbers
  • c_m = 2LN with N in rev/s, or 2LN/60 with N in rev/min - metres per second
  • The factor is TWO, because the piston covers the stroke twice per revolution
  • Typical large low-speed two-stroke: of the order of 8 to 8.5 m/s at rated output
  • Typical medium-speed four-stroke: of the order of 9 to 11 m/s
  • Propeller law: power varies approximately as the CUBE of shaft speed for a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged conditions
  • A 10% reduction in mean piston speed removes roughly 27% of the power per hour; consumption per mile falls roughly as the square
  • SEEMP Part III mandatory from 1 January 2023
  • 2023 is the first CII data year and is still running at this November sitting
Critical regulation
Limbs (a) and (b) rest on no instrument at all - they are geometry, the definition of mean effective pressure and the propeller law. The only regulatory content is in limb (c): the operating envelope decided there is recorded and monitored in the ship's SEEMP under MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, with Part III mandatory since 1 January 2023, and the operative guidance at this sitting is the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.346(78) - not the 2024 successor, which is future here.
Major trap
Concluding that the lowest mean piston speed gives the lowest fuel bill. Two effects run against each other: power demand falls as the cube of speed, which is the saving, but specific fuel oil consumption rises once the engine is well below its matched point, because compression and scavenge pressure fall, the turbocharger is off its match and atomisation degrades. There is therefore an optimum band, not a monotonic rule. The paired traps are writing the formula as LN instead of 2LN, and claiming that fuel per mile follows the cube - per hour follows the cube, per mile follows roughly the square, because the voyage takes longer.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q8 - the retrofit hardware that follows when the engine is genuinely mismatched to the speed the ship now runs at · QP2311 Q7 - the fuel oil analysis report, which is where fuel quality enters the same consumption picture

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Two quite different documents are being asked about and it is worth keeping them apart from the first line. The first is the piece of paper that travels with the cargo. It does three jobs at once: it is the ship's admission that she has the goods and what state they looked to be in; it records the terms on which they are carried; and — this is the surprising one — it is treated as standing for the goods themselves, so whoever holds it can sell them while they are still at sea and the ship must hand the cargo to whoever produces it. That third job is why signing one carelessly is so dangerous: a signature saying the cargo looked sound is relied on by a buyer thousands of miles away who will never see the ship, and no private promise from the shipper can undo the harm to him. The second document is about the ship. When a ship is handed over to a charterer, somebody writes down what state she is in and what is aboard at that exact moment, because when she comes back the two records will be compared. How thorough that has to be depends entirely on how much of the ship has been handed over and for how long. Hand over a single voyage and you need only prove she is fit for that cargo. Hand over her employment for a year and you need the fuel, the readiness and a performance yardstick. Hand over the whole ship — crew, upkeep, insurance, everything — for years, and you need to record the machinery, the certificates and every spare in the store, because nobody will remember any of it when she returns.

Knowledge map
Bill of Lading, Signing Precautions, and On-Hire Survey under Three Charters
  1. 1The three functions
    • a bill of lading is issued by or on behalf of the carrier to the shipper when goods are received for shipment or shipped on board
    • RECEIPT - it acknowledges the stated goods, marks and numbers, in the APPARENT ORDER AND CONDITION stated on its face; this is the function the ship controls
    • EVIDENCE OF THE CONTRACT OF CARRIAGE - it records the terms on which the goods are carried
  2. 2The types worth naming, and the law that governs
    • shipped (on board) against received for shipment - only the shipped bill certifies the goods are aboard, and letters of credit normally demand it
    • clean against claused or foul - a clean bill carries no notation of defective condition, and a claused bill is normally unacceptable under a letter of credit
    • order against straight against bearer - only the order bill is freely negotiable by endorsement
  3. 3The precautions that apply under any charter
    • the master signs and WHAT HE SIGNS BINDS THE OWNER
    • sign only for what is actually on board - compare against the mate's receipts and the ship's own tally, ullage or draught survey
    • CARRY THE CLAUSING ACROSS - any remark on the mate's receipt must appear on the bill; a claused receipt and a clean bill is the classic loss
  4. 4Under a VOYAGE charter - protect the incorporation and the lien
    • the owner keeps the ship and is paid FREIGHT; the bill will reach a receiver who has never seen the charterparty
    • the risk is the owner being bound to a stranger on terms wider than the charter he agreed
    • confirm the INCORPORATION CLAUSE and that it identifies the charterparty BY ITS DATE - an incorporation clause with no date is a standing defect
  5. 5Under a TIME charter - signing as presented, and the indemnity
    • the CHARTERER directs employment and normally presents the bills, and the employment clause obliges the master to sign AS PRESENTED
    • THAT OBLIGATION IS NOT UNLIMITED - it does not extend to a bill inconsistent with the mate's receipts, false, misdated, or imposing liability materially beyond the charter
    • where the charterer or his agents sign on the master's behalf, obtain WRITTEN AUTHORITY limited to signing in conformity with mate's receipts
  6. 6What the on-hire survey is for, and the common core
    • it fixes the condition of the ship and the quantities on board AT THE MOMENT RESPONSIBILITY CHANGES HANDS
    • so that a dispute months or years later is settled against a record rather than against recollection
    • it is a COMMERCIAL survey between the parties - not a class or statutory survey, and it credits nothing
  7. 7The three differentiated
    • BAREBOAT - possession and control pass; the charterer mans, operates, maintains, insures and drydocks the ship as if owner, for YEARS
    • bareboat survey: full machinery condition survey, class survey status and outstanding conditions of class, thickness measurements, docking and survey due dates, coatings, and an ITEM-BY-ITEM inventory of spares, stores, tools, plans, manuals and publications
    • VOYAGE - nothing passes; the owner keeps the ship and is paid freight to carry a cargo
  8. 8The bareboat survey - and the reasons
    • THE BARE-BOAT DELIVERY SURVEY, BY A WIDE MARGIN - days of work, and the consequences carried for the whole charter
    • it is the only one where the WHOLE SHIP changes hands - possession passes and the charterer becomes responsible for manning, operating, maintaining, insuring and drydocking
    • it is the only baseline that has to survive YEARS - redelivery is in like condition, fair wear and tear excepted, and 'like condition' can only be argued against what was written at delivery
