Remember8 route headings
Cover66 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 66 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The three functions
  • a bill of lading is issued by or on behalf of the carrier to the shipper when goods are received for shipment or shipped on board
  • RECEIPT - it acknowledges the stated goods, marks and numbers, in the APPARENT ORDER AND CONDITION stated on its face; this is the function the ship controls
  • EVIDENCE OF THE CONTRACT OF CARRIAGE - it records the terms on which the goods are carried
  • the precision that earns the mark: where a charterparty exists, the charterparty is the contract between owner and charterer and the bill is only EVIDENCE of it
  • but in the hands of a third party who is not the charterer, THE BILL IS THE CONTRACT on which that holder sues
  • DOCUMENT OF TITLE - where made out 'to order' it is transferable by endorsement and delivery, and goods are delivered only against surrender of an original
  • this is what allows cargo to be sold afloat and financed by documentary credit
2. The types worth naming, and the law that governs
  • shipped (on board) against received for shipment - only the shipped bill certifies the goods are aboard, and letters of credit normally demand it
  • clean against claused or foul - a clean bill carries no notation of defective condition, and a claused bill is normally unacceptable under a letter of credit
  • order against straight against bearer - only the order bill is freely negotiable by endorsement
  • bill of lading against SEA WAYBILL - a waybill is NOT a document of title and goods are delivered against identification
  • charterparty bill against liner bill - the charterparty bill incorporates a named charterparty, which is what limb b) turns on
  • governing law: the Hague-Visby Rules, given effect in India by the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993
  • transfer of rights under the bill: the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, the statute in force at this sitting
  • the ship herself operates under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958
3. The precautions that apply under any charter
  • the master signs and WHAT HE SIGNS BINDS THE OWNER
  • sign only for what is actually on board - compare against the mate's receipts and the ship's own tally, ullage or draught survey
  • CARRY THE CLAUSING ACROSS - any remark on the mate's receipt must appear on the bill; a claused receipt and a clean bill is the classic loss
  • NEVER sign a clean bill for cargo not in apparent good order and condition, and NEVER accept a letter of indemnity in exchange for one
  • such an indemnity is unenforceable against the party it deceives and a knowingly false bill prejudices the owner's P&I cover; clause the bill and refer to owners
  • quantity - sign for the ship's figures or qualify the shipper's figure; never adopt an unchecked shore figure as though checked
  • DATE IT CORRECTLY - the date loading completed; ante-dating or post-dating is fraud and voids cover
  • check description, marks and numbers, load and discharge ports, consignee and notify party, and freight terms; do not certify 'freight prepaid' unless authorised
  • account for the number of originals issued, and that delivery is against surrender of one original
  • deck cargo must be stated as carried on deck, and never sign for cargo not shipped
  • check the authority to sign - written authority where an agent signs, expressed as for and on behalf of the master
4. Under a VOYAGE charter - protect the incorporation and the lien
  • the owner keeps the ship and is paid FREIGHT; the bill will reach a receiver who has never seen the charterparty
  • the risk is the owner being bound to a stranger on terms wider than the charter he agreed
  • confirm the INCORPORATION CLAUSE and that it identifies the charterparty BY ITS DATE - an incorporation clause with no date is a standing defect
  • confirm the owner's LIEN on cargo for freight, deadfreight and demurrage survives through the bill
  • confirm the Paramount Clause so the applicable regime, its defences and its limits apply
  • check the cargo quantity against the charter minimum and maximum, the deadweight and the deadfreight position
  • accept no term inconsistent with the charter - a different discharge port, a different cargo description, or freight prepaid where freight is unpaid
  • refer anything unusual to owners before signing
5. Under a TIME charter - signing as presented, and the indemnity
  • the CHARTERER directs employment and normally presents the bills, and the employment clause obliges the master to sign AS PRESENTED
  • THAT OBLIGATION IS NOT UNLIMITED - it does not extend to a bill inconsistent with the mate's receipts, false, misdated, or imposing liability materially beyond the charter
  • where the charterer or his agents sign on the master's behalf, obtain WRITTEN AUTHORITY limited to signing in conformity with mate's receipts
  • the employment and indemnity clause protects a master who acted PROPERLY; it is not a licence to sign anything
  • check incorporation and the Paramount Clause as under a voyage charter
  • SWITCH BILLS and delivery without production of an original against a letter of indemnity are the two demands that arrive most often - both go to owners and P&I before anything is signed
  • freight is not the ship's concern here, the owner being paid hire; the lien question is different and the charterer's freight arrangements are not certified by the ship
6. What the on-hire survey is for, and the common core
  • it fixes the condition of the ship and the quantities on board AT THE MOMENT RESPONSIBILITY CHANGES HANDS
  • so that a dispute months or years later is settled against a record rather than against recollection
  • it is a COMMERCIAL survey between the parties - not a class or statutory survey, and it credits nothing
  • common to all three: date and time of delivery, bunkers remaining on board by grade and quantity, fresh water and lubricating oils
  • hold and tank condition, hatch cover condition, cargo gear, existing damage recorded and photographed, and the certificates on board
  • what differs is WHAT THE SURVEY HAS TO PROTECT AND FOR HOW LONG
7. The three differentiated
  • BAREBOAT - possession and control pass; the charterer mans, operates, maintains, insures and drydocks the ship as if owner, for YEARS
  • bareboat survey: full machinery condition survey, class survey status and outstanding conditions of class, thickness measurements, docking and survey due dates, coatings, and an ITEM-BY-ITEM inventory of spares, stores, tools, plans, manuals and publications
  • VOYAGE - nothing passes; the owner keeps the ship and is paid freight to carry a cargo
  • voyage survey: cargo-worthiness for THIS cargo - hold or tank cleanliness and previous cargo, hatch cover weathertightness testing, cargo gear, draught survey, certificates needed for this voyage
  • TIME - the employment of the ship passes for a period; the owner keeps crewing and maintenance, the charterer pays hire and bunkers
  • time charter survey: bunkers by grade and quantity as the central figure, cargo spaces clean and ready for the intended trade, cargo gear and its test certification, defects recorded, and the SPEED AND CONSUMPTION BASELINE against the description warranties
  • attendance and duration scale the same way - bareboat days with joint independent surveyors and often class, time charter a day or so jointly, voyage often hours with a single cargo surveyor
  • bareboat and time charter each have a MIRROR off-hire survey at redelivery; the voyage charter has none
  • THE TERMINOLOGY POINT - strictly a voyage charter attracts no ON-HIRE survey at all, because no hire is paid and the ship is never delivered; what is done is a pre-loading condition and cargo-worthiness survey
8. The bareboat survey - and the reasons
  • THE BARE-BOAT DELIVERY SURVEY, BY A WIDE MARGIN - days of work, and the consequences carried for the whole charter
  • it is the only one where the WHOLE SHIP changes hands - possession passes and the charterer becomes responsible for manning, operating, maintaining, insuring and drydocking
  • it is the only baseline that has to survive YEARS - redelivery is in like condition, fair wear and tear excepted, and 'like condition' can only be argued against what was written at delivery
  • its scope is the entire technical inventory - machinery condition and running hours, performance data, class survey status and every outstanding condition of class, thickness measurements, docking and survey due dates, coatings, and a spares, stores, tools, plans and publications inventory item by item
  • NO OTHER SURVEY OPENS THE STORE ROOMS
  • the contentious parts are the ENGINEER'S parts - machinery condition, running hours since overhaul and what spares were aboard, which only the Chief Engineer can properly record
  • the consequences reach beyond the charter - change of flag, manager and insurers, and bareboat registration or finance arrangements, so errors propagate into insurance, mortgage and registration questions
  • and nothing in it can be repeated - the ship sails, the crew changes, and the moment cannot be re-created
  • THE RANKING: bareboat first, time charter second because it is joint and establishes bunkers, readiness and the performance baseline but is bounded by the period, voyage third and narrowest
  • THE PRINCIPLE BEHIND THE RANKING - the depth of a delivery survey is set by how much responsibility passes and for how long
Model written answer

(a) What a bill of lading is

1. The three functions

A bill of lading is a document issued by or on behalf of the carrier to the shipper when goods are received for shipment or are shipped on board. Four marks buy the three functions, and the three functions are the answer:

  1. A receipt. It acknowledges that the carrier has received the stated goods — quantity, marks and numbers — in the apparent order and condition stated on its face. This is the function the ship controls and the one the master signs to.
  2. Evidence of the contract of carriage. It records the terms on which the goods are carried. Note the precision: where a charterparty exists, the charterparty is the contract between owner and charterer and the bill is only evidence of it; but in the hands of a third party who is not the charterer, the bill of lading is the contract on which that holder sues.
  3. A document of title. Where it is made out “to order”, it is transferable by endorsement and delivery, and the goods are delivered only against surrender of an original. This is what allows cargo to be sold while it is still at sea and financed by documentary credit — and it is the reason a bill of lading matters far more than a receipt.

2. The types worth naming, and the law that governs

DistinctionWhat turns on it
Shipped (on board) against received for shipmentOnly the shipped bill certifies the goods are actually on board. Letters of credit normally demand it
Clean against claused (foul)A clean bill carries no notation of defective condition. A claused bill is normally unacceptable under a letter of credit - which is why pressure is put on the master to sign clean
Order against straight against bearerOnly the order bill is freely negotiable by endorsement. A straight bill names its consignee and is not
Bill of lading against sea waybillA sea waybill is not a document of title; goods under it are delivered against identification, not presentation
Charterparty bill against liner billA charterparty bill incorporates the terms of a named charterparty, which is what limb (b) turns on

The governing law. Carriage under a bill of lading is governed internationally by the Hague-Visby Rules, given effect in India by the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993; the transfer of rights under the bill is governed by the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, which is the statute in force at this sitting. The ship itself operates under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.

(b) Precautions before signing a bill of lading

3. The precautions that apply under any charter

The master signs, and what he signs binds the owner. These come first because they apply whatever the charter:

  1. Sign only for what is actually on board. Compare the bill against the mate's receipts and the ship's own tally, ullage or draught survey.
  2. Carry the clausing across. Any remark on the mate's receipt — wet, rusty, torn bags, short, stained, atmospheric rust — must appear on the bill. A mate's receipt claused and a bill signed clean is the classic loss.
  3. Never sign a clean bill for cargo that is not in apparent good order and condition, and never accept a letter of indemnity in exchange for one. Such an indemnity is unenforceable against the party it deceives, and issuing a knowingly false bill prejudices the owner's P&I cover. Clause the bill and refer to owners.
  4. Quantity. Where the ship cannot verify the figure, sign for the ship's figures or qualify the shipper's figure appropriately — never adopt a shore figure the ship has not checked as though the ship had checked it.
  5. Date it correctly. The date is the date loading completed. Ante-dating or post-dating is fraud, however commercially convenient, and it voids cover.
  6. Check the particulars — description, marks and numbers, ports of loading and discharge, consignee and notify party, freight terms. Do not certify “freight prepaid” unless authorised.
  7. Account for the originals. Note how many originals are issued and that delivery is against surrender of one original.
  8. Deck cargo must be stated as carried on deck; and never sign a bill for cargo not shipped.
  9. Check the authority to sign. If an agent signs, obtain written authority, and see that it is expressed as being for and on behalf of the master.

4. Under a VOYAGE charter - protect the incorporation and the lien

Here the owner keeps the ship and is paid freight. The bill will reach a receiver who has never seen the charterparty, and the risk is that the owner ends up bound to a stranger on terms wider than the charter he agreed.

  • Confirm the incorporation clause — that the bill incorporates the terms of the charterparty, identified by its date. An incorporation clause with no charterparty date is a standing defect.
  • Confirm that the lien survives. The owner's lien on cargo for freight, deadfreight and demurrage must be preserved through the bill, or the only security for unpaid freight is gone once the cargo is delivered.
  • Confirm the Paramount Clause, so that the applicable carriage regime and its defences and limits apply.
  • Check the cargo quantity against the charter — the minimum and maximum quantity, the deadweight and the deadfreight position.
  • Do not accept terms inconsistent with the charter — a different discharge port, a different description of the cargo, or a freight-prepaid notation where freight has not been paid.
  • Refer anything unusual to owners before signing. Under a voyage charter the master is the owner's man and has no reason to sign anything he is not comfortable with.

5. Under a TIME charter - signing as presented, and the indemnity

Here the charterer directs the employment of the ship and normally presents the bills. The employment clause obliges the master to sign bills as presented — but that obligation is not unlimited, and the boundary is where the marks are:

  • The obligation to sign as presented does not extend to a bill that is inconsistent with the mate's receipts, that is false, that is misdated, or that would impose on the owner a liability materially beyond the charter. The master may and should refuse.
  • Where the charterer or his agents sign on the master's behalf, obtain the written authority, and see that the authority is limited to signing in conformity with mate's receipts.
  • The employment and indemnity clause is the owner's protection for complying with charterer's orders — but it protects a master who acted properly. It is not a licence to sign anything.
  • Check the incorporation and the Paramount Clause as under a voyage charter, and check that the charterer has not presented a bill that turns a period contract into an unlimited carriage liability.
  • Switch bills, and delivery without production of an original against a letter of indemnity, are the two demands that most often arrive under a time charter. Both go to owners and P&I before anything is signed. Neither is the master's decision alone.
  • Freight is not the ship's concern here — the owner is paid hire, not freight — so the lien question is different, and the charterer's freight arrangements are not certified by the ship.

(c) On-hire survey under three charter types

6. What the on-hire survey is for, and the common core

An on-hire or delivery survey fixes the condition of the ship and the quantities on board at the moment responsibility changes hands, so that a dispute months or years later can be settled against a record rather than against recollection. It is a commercial survey between the parties — it is not a class or statutory survey, and it credits nothing.

Common to all three: date and time of delivery, bunkers remaining on board by grade and quantity, fresh water and lubricating oils, hold and tank condition, hatch cover condition, cargo gear, existing damage recorded and photographed, and the certificates on board.

What differs is what the survey has to protect, and for how long:

7. The three differentiated

CharterWhat passes, and for how longWhat the survey must therefore establish
1) Bare-boat
(demise)		Possession and control, for years. The charterer mans, operates, maintains, insures and drydocks her as if owner, and redelivers her in like condition, fair wear and tear exceptedThe whole asset baseline: main and auxiliary machinery condition, running hours and recent overhauls; class survey status with every outstanding condition of class, due dates, thickness measurements and the docking cycle; all certificates; tank and hold coatings; and an item-by-item inventory of spares, stores, tools, plans, manuals and publications. Joint attendance with independent surveyors and often class; days. Mirrored by an off-hire survey years later
2) Voyage charterNothing. The owner keeps the ship entirely and is paid freight to carry one cargo between named portsCargo-worthiness for this cargo only: hold or tank cleanliness against the previous cargo, hatch cover weathertightness testing, cargo gear and its certification, a draught survey, and the certificates this voyage and this cargo need. Often one cargo surveyor; hours. No mirror survey
3) Time charterThe employment of the ship, for a period. The owner keeps crewing and maintenance; the charterer pays hire and bunkersThe hire, bunker and performance baseline: bunkers by grade and quantity, fresh water and lubricating oils; cargo spaces clean and ready for the intended trade; cargo gear and its test certification; existing damage recorded; certificate due dates falling inside the period; and the speed and consumption baseline against the description warranties. Joint independent surveyors; about a day. Mirrored by an off-hire survey at redelivery, bunkers again central

One point of terminology, stated once. Strictly, a voyage charter attracts no on-hire survey at all, because no hire is paid and the ship is never delivered into anyone's service. What is done is a pre-loading condition and cargo-worthiness survey. The limb asks for the differentiation, so it is given — but saying why the voyage case is the odd one out is itself worth a mark.

(d) Which survey is the most demanding and exhaustive

8. The bareboat survey - and the reasons

The bare-boat delivery survey, by a wide margin. As Chief Engineer I would expect to be committed to it for days, and to carry the consequences of it for the whole charter. Six reasons, in order of weight:

  1. It is the only one where the whole ship changes hands. Under a bareboat charter the charterer takes possession and becomes responsible for manning, operating, maintaining, insuring and drydocking her. Everything therefore has to be recorded, because everything is now someone else's obligation.
  2. It is the only baseline that has to survive years. Redelivery is in like condition, fair wear and tear excepted, and "like condition" can only be argued against what was written down at delivery. A voyage survey is spent when the cargo is discharged; a time charter survey is answered at redelivery within the period; the bareboat record has to hold for the life of the charter.
  3. Its scope is the entire technical inventory. Machinery condition and running hours, performance data, class survey status and every outstanding condition of class, thickness measurement records, docking and survey due dates, tank and hold coatings, and a spares, stores, tools, plans, manuals and publications inventory taken item by item. No other survey opens the store rooms.
  4. The contentious parts are the engineer's parts. In my own experience the disputes are almost never about the hull. They are about machinery condition, running hours since overhaul, and what spares were on board — exactly the three things only the Chief Engineer can properly record, and the three things that are most expensive to argue about afterwards.
  5. The consequences reach beyond the charter. A bareboat charter can carry a change of flag, of manager and of insurers, and may sit under a finance or bareboat-registration arrangement. Errors in the delivery record propagate into insurance, mortgage and registration questions, not merely into a hire dispute.
  6. Nothing in it can be repeated. The ship sails, the crew changes, and the moment cannot be re-created. A cargo survey missed can be re-done next voyage; a bareboat delivery survey missed is simply gone.

The ranking, and why the other two sit where they do. The time charter survey is second: it is genuinely joint and it establishes bunkers, cargo readiness and the speed-and-consumption baseline against which performance claims will run — substantial, but bounded by the period and by the fact that the owner still maintains the ship. The voyage survey is third and narrowest: one cargo, one voyage, essentially a cargo-worthiness inspection, and the owner never lets go of the ship at all.

The principle behind the ranking is worth stating in one line: the depth of a delivery survey is set by how much responsibility passes and for how long. Bareboat passes everything, for years. Time charter passes employment, for a period. Voyage charter passes nothing at all.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Four limbs, four marks each, all printed — so the discipline is that no limb may be allowed to eat another. Limb (a) is the one candidates over-write: it asks what a bill of lading is, and four marks buy the three functions plus a short types-and-law paragraph. A page on types with the three functions missing scores badly.

Limb (b) names two charters, so it gets three blocks: the precautions common to both first, then one block each. That ordering is deliberate — most of the marks are in the common block, and the charter-specific material is what separates a good answer from an average one.

Limb (c) prints the word “differentiate” against three named charters, which is an instruction to build a comparison table with the three as columns. A candidate who writes three separate paragraphs has not differentiated; he has described three times.

Limb (d) prints “explain with reasons” and “as a Chief Engineer”. Both matter. It wants a decision stated at the top, numbered reasons underneath, and at least one of them argued from the engineer's own position rather than from a textbook.

The printed anomaly in limb (d), and how it is handled

The paper prints “which of the above three Survey is the most demanding and exhaustive” — a singular noun after a plural numeral, and capitalised mid-sentence. It is preserved exactly as printed and not corrected.

The intended sense is plain — which of the three surveys — and the answer reads it that way. This is the house rule and it is worth understanding rather than resenting: a candidate sitting that paper saw exactly this, and MIW records what was printed. Limb (c) prints “on-hire Survey” with a capital S for the same reason, and the question number itself is printed “Q3)” where seven of the nine questions print a full stop.

The one legal point never to get wrong

A clean bill of lading is never signed for cargo that is not in apparent good order and condition, and a letter of indemnity offered in exchange for one is worthless.

The indemnity is unenforceable against the party it deceives, and issuing a bill the master knows to be false prejudices the owner's P&I cover — so the owner is left with the liability and no insurance behind it. The same applies to misdating: the date is the date loading completed, and ante-dating to catch a letter of credit expiry is fraud however routine it is made to sound.

The correct action is always the same: clause the bill to match the mate's receipt, and refer to owners and the club. It is not a decision the master takes alone under commercial pressure at three in the morning, and saying so is worth a mark.

Voyage against time: the one-line difference for limb (b)

Voyage charterTime charter
Owner is paidFreightHire
Who presents the billNormally through the owner's own chainThe charterer, and the employment clause requires signature as presented
Main risk to the ownerBeing bound to a stranger on terms wider than the charter, and losing the lien for freight, deadfreight and demurrageSigning a bill that is false, misdated, or inconsistent with the mate's receipts, in reliance on an employment clause that does not protect him if he does
The precaution that matters mostIncorporation clause with the charterparty date, and the lien preservedWritten authority where agents sign, and the limit of "as presented" understood

Common mistakes

  • In limb (a), listing types of bill of lading and never stating the three functions.
  • Saying flatly that the bill of lading is the contract of carriage. It is evidence of it — and becomes the contract in the hands of a third-party holder, which is the precise point.
  • Calling a sea waybill a document of title. It is not.
  • In limb (b), giving one undifferentiated list when the limb names two charters.
  • Omitting the letter of indemnity point, which is the most examinable thing in the limb.
  • Saying the master must sign whatever the time charterer presents. The obligation to sign as presented stops at bills that are false, misdated or inconsistent with the mate's receipts.
  • In limb (c), describing three surveys instead of differentiating them.
  • Treating the on-hire survey as a class or statutory survey. It is a commercial survey between the parties and credits nothing.
  • Missing that a voyage charter involves no hire and no delivery, so the survey there is a cargo-worthiness survey.
  • In limb (d), giving an answer with no reasons, or reasons that are assertions. “Explain with reasons” means the reasons are the marks.
  • Answering limb (d) without using the Chief Engineer's viewpoint the limb expressly gives you — machinery condition, running hours and spares inventory.

Examiner traps

  • “Give the three functions.” Receipt; evidence of the contract of carriage; document of title.
  • “Is the bill of lading the contract?” Between owner and charterer, no — the charterparty is. In a third-party holder's hands, it is the contract he sues on.
  • “Why 'to order'?” Because it makes the bill negotiable by endorsement, so the cargo can be sold afloat and financed.
  • “Shipper wants a clean bill for rust-stained steel and offers an LOI. What do you do?” Clause the bill to match the mate's receipt, refuse the LOI, refer to owners and the club. The LOI is unenforceable and cover is prejudiced.
  • “What date goes on the bill?” The date loading completed. Nothing else.
  • “Charterer's agent wants to sign for you.” Written authority, limited to signing in conformity with mate's receipts.
  • “Discharge without an original bill, against an LOI?” Owners and P&I decide, not the master.
  • “Why does the incorporation clause need the charterparty date?” Because a clause incorporating an unidentified charterparty may incorporate nothing.
  • “Who is on hire under a voyage charter?” Nobody — freight is paid, not hire. It is the trap inside limb (c).
  • “What is the single largest item in a bareboat delivery survey?” The inventory — spares, stores, tools, plans and publications, taken item by item.
  • “What condition is a bareboat ship redelivered in?” Like condition, fair wear and tear excepted — measured against the delivery record.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What is a mate's receipt and why does it matter? The ship's own record of quantity and apparent order and condition; its clausing must be carried onto the bill.
  • What is a claused bill? One bearing a notation that the goods were not in apparent good order and condition.
  • Which rules govern carriage under a bill of lading, and how do they apply in India? The Hague-Visby Rules, through the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993.
  • Which Indian Act governs transfer of rights under a bill of lading at this time? The Bills of Lading Act, 1856.
  • What is a switch bill and why is it dangerous? A replacement set issued for the same cargo; two sets in circulation is a fraud risk, and it is never the master's decision.
  • What is the owner's lien for, and how is it lost? For freight, deadfreight and demurrage; it is lost if the bill does not preserve it or the cargo is delivered without it being exercised.
  • Name three things recorded at any on-hire survey. Bunkers by grade and quantity, hold and tank condition, and existing damage.
  • Who attends a bareboat delivery survey? Independent surveyors for both parties, often with class in attendance.
  • Why is a speed and consumption baseline taken at a time charter delivery? Because performance claims under the description warranties will be measured against it.

Memory framework

Limb (a) is three words: RECEIPT, EVIDENCE, TITLE. Nothing else in that limb is worth more than those three.

Limb (b) is five checks before the pen touches paper: QUANTITY, CONDITION, DATE, TERMS, AUTHORITY — and one rule that overrides all of them: NO CLEAN BILL FOR DIRTY CARGO, AND NO LOI. Then one word for each charter: voyage is INCORPORATION (and the lien); time is AS PRESENTED (and its limits).

Limb (c) is one question asked three times: WHAT PASSES, AND FOR HOW LONG? Bareboat — everything, for years. Time — employment, for a period. Voyage — nothing, for one voyage. Every difference in the table falls out of that.

Limb (d) is the same idea used as the answer: the more that passes and the longer it passes for, the deeper the survey.

Regulation and source map

  • Hague-Visby Rules — the international carriage regime applied through the bill of lading's Paramount Clause. Named at identity level; no article is quoted.
  • Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993 (India) — the statute giving effect to the Hague-Visby Rules.
  • Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 — transfer of rights under a bill of lading. This is the Act in force at this sitting; any later recodification postdates the examination.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 — the governing Indian shipping statute at this sitting. The 2025 Act commenced 15 March 2026 and is prohibited here.
  • Standard charterparty and bill of lading forms — the voyage form and its associated bill, the time charter forms and the bareboat form are named only. No clause of any form is quoted or numbered; see the uncertainty section.
  • P&I — the club position on false and misdated bills and on letters of indemnity is settled practice, not a quoted rule.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

This question is answered out of commercial practice, and the corpus was never going to hold its sources.

The True Source corpus's reference resolver holds 320 entries and every one of them is MARPOL Annex VI. It holds no carriage-of-goods statute, no charterparty form and no bill of lading. That is not a gap in this session's research; it is the shape of the corpus. The three functions of a bill of lading are uncontroversial and consistently recorded, and are carried at authoritative-secondary level.

The consequences are deliberate. The Hague-Visby Rules, the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993, and the Bills of Lading Act, 1856 are named at identity level and nothing is quoted from any of them; no article or section number is attributed a sub-paragraph. The Hague-Visby package and unit limitation figures are not stated, because the Indian conversion is not held and nothing in this question needs them. The standard forms are named only — no incorporation, lien, employment or indemnity clause is reproduced, numbered or paraphrased as though read, because the forms are copyright industry documents.

The on-hire survey material in limbs (c) and (d) is commercial practice and engineering judgement. No survey report format, standard scope of work or industry checklist is cited, and no time or cost figure is given for any of the three surveys. The ranking in limb (d) is a reasoned judgement, offered as such, and its principle — depth follows how much responsibility passes and for how long — is stated so that the reasoning can be tested rather than merely accepted.

Currency. Two Indian statutes are at their pre-recodification stage here and both are stated as at the sitting. The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 governs — the 2025 Act commenced 15 March 2026, twenty-eight months after this examination. The Bills of Lading Act, 1856 is likewise the Act in force; its successor postdates the sitting and its identity is not asserted. Neither may be updated by a later editor without falsifying the paper.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The three functions
  2. __________The types worth naming, and the law that governs

  4. __________Under a VOYAGE charter - protect the incorporation and the lien
  5. __________Under a TIME charter - signing as presented, and the indemnity
  6. __________What the on-hire survey is for, and the common core
  7. __________The three differentiated
  8. __________The bareboat survey - and the reasons
Flashcards 11

Four marks, three functions - it is the whole of limb (a) and the most reliably examined item in the question.

The distinction is the difference between a correct answer and a nearly correct one.

It is the most common commercial pressure put on a master and the least defensible thing to give way on.

Under a voyage charter the owner is paid freight and the lien is his only security for it; under a time charter he is paid hire and the freight arrangements are not the ship's concern.

The employment and indemnity clause protects a master who acted properly; it is not a licence.

Differentiate means compare; three one-line contrasts beat three paragraphs of description.

Limb (d) says 'explain with reasons' - the principle is what turns six reasons into an argument.

All three are the instruments in force in November 2023.

The Indian layer is where an answer inherited from a later sitting would silently update two statutes and falsify the paper.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Bill of lading = RECEIPT, EVIDENCE of the contract of carriage, DOCUMENT OF TITLE. Between owner and charterer the charterparty is the contract and the bill is evidence; in a third party's hands the bill IS the contract. Order bills are negotiable by endorsement; a sea waybill is not a document of title. Law: Hague-Visby, through the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended 1993; transfer of rights under the Bills of Lading Act, 1856; ship under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. Before signing anything: quantity against mate's receipts, clausing carried across, correct date = date loading completed, particulars, originals accounted for, authority to sign. Never a clean bill for damaged cargo and never an LOI for one — unenforceable, and it prejudices P&I cover. Voyage charter: incorporation clause naming the charterparty by date, and the lien for freight, deadfreight and demurrage preserved. Time charter: sign as presented — but not if false, misdated or inconsistent with the mate's receipts; written authority where agents sign; switch bills and delivery without an original go to owners and P&I. On-hire survey fixes condition and quantities when responsibility changes hands; it is commercial, not class. Bareboat — everything passes, for years: machinery, class status, item-by-item inventory. Time — employment for a period: bunkers, readiness, speed-and-consumption baseline. Voyage — nothing passes: cargo-worthiness for one cargo. Most demanding: bareboat, because depth follows how much passes and for how long.
Keywords

Critical numbers
  • Three functions of a bill of lading: receipt, evidence of the contract of carriage, document of title
  • The bill of lading date is the date LOADING COMPLETED - no other date is permissible
  • Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993 - the Indian statute giving effect to the Hague-Visby Rules
  • Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856 - the Act in force at this November 2023 sitting
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 - the governing Indian shipping statute at this sitting
  • Three charter types, three survey depths: bareboat (years, everything), time charter (a period, employment), voyage charter (one voyage, nothing)
Critical regulation
Carriage under a bill of lading is governed by the Hague-Visby Rules, given effect in India by the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993, and applied to a particular bill through its Paramount Clause. Transfer of rights under the bill is governed at this sitting by the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, and the ship operates under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. None of these instruments is quoted in this answer and no article, rule or section is attributed a sub-paragraph.
Major trap
Signing a clean bill of lading for cargo that is not in apparent good order and condition, in reliance on a letter of indemnity. The indemnity is unenforceable against the party it deceives — the receiver or bank who relied on the clean bill — and issuing a bill the master knows to be false prejudices the owner's P&I cover, so the owner carries the liability with no insurance behind it. The same applies to ante-dating to catch a letter of credit expiry. The correct action is always to clause the bill to match the mate's receipt and refer to owners and the club. The second trap, in limb (c), is forgetting that a voyage charter involves no hire and no delivery, so it attracts no on-hire survey properly so called.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q9 - P&I cover, which is what a wrongly signed bill of lading or an accepted letter of indemnity puts at risk · QP2311 Q1 - the class survey regime, which is a different thing from the commercial on-hire survey discussed here

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

The most useful thing to hold about this Convention is what it deliberately is not. It is not about stopping oil getting into the sea — a different instrument does that, with discharge limits and hull construction. It is not about who pays afterwards — that is yet another set of rules. This one starts from the assumption that the spill has already happened and asks a much more practical question: is anybody ready, and can they help each other? Its three ideas are in its own name — be prepared, be able to respond, and co-operate — and every article serves one of them. The second thing to hold is structural, and it is where most answers go wrong: this Convention binds countries, not ships. It tells each country to require its own ships to carry an emergency plan; the rule that actually reaches the vessel, with tonnage thresholds and a list of what the plan must contain, lives in the pollution-prevention convention instead. So a question about what the ship must carry is answered out of a different instrument from the one being asked about — and saying so is the best single sentence available here. The third thing is that when a question asks about a country's role rather than a flag's, the biggest obligation comes into view: building and paying for a national response system — a plan, an authority someone can telephone at any hour, equipment sitting in a shed, and exercises — every year, whether or not anything spills.

Model written answer

(a) Key provisions and objectives, and the significance of the Convention

1. What OPRC is, and what it deliberately is not

The International Convention on Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation, 1990 was adopted on 30 November 1990 and entered into force on 13 May 1995. It was developed after the Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989, which showed that even a well-resourced State could be overwhelmed by a major spill and that there was no framework for asking anyone else for help.

It is a framework convention. It sets no discharge standard and prescribes no construction requirement. It obliges its Parties to build and maintain a capability — plans, authorities, equipment, exercises — and to make that capability available to one another. Its architecture was extended to chemicals by the OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000.

The objectives are the three nouns of its own title:

  1. Preparedness — plans, designated authorities, pre-positioned equipment, trained people and rehearsed procedures existing before the incident.
  2. Response — the ability to act promptly and effectively, through a known chain of command and known contact points.
  3. Co-operation — that States assist one another, with the legal and administrative friction that delays cross-border assistance removed in advance.

2. The key provisions, article by article

The substantive articles hold as a sequence — plan, report, react, build, share:

ArticleWhat it requires
3 — emergency plansEach Party requires ships flying its flag to carry an oil pollution emergency plan in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization, with parallel requirements on offshore units, sea ports and oil handling facilities under its jurisdiction
4 — reportingMasters and persons in charge report without delay any event involving a discharge or probable discharge of oil, to the nearest coastal State
5 — action on a reportThe Party receiving it assesses the incident and notifies all States whose interests may be affected, together with the Organization
6 — national and regional systemsThe core building obligation. A national contingency plan, a designated competent national authority and an operational contact point; and within the Party's capabilities a minimum level of pre-positioned response equipment, a programme of exercises and training, and detailed plans and communications capability
7 — co-operation in responseAdvisory services, technical support and equipment on request, subject to capabilities and resources
8 to 10Research and development; technical co-operation with developing countries; and the promotion of bilateral and multilateral agreements, from which regional arrangements grow
12The institutional role — information services, education and training, and technical services placed with IMO

3. Significance - in response, and in the sense in which it touches prevention

In response, its significance is direct. Before OPRC, a coastal State facing a major spill had no reliable mechanism to obtain foreign equipment or expertise, no agreed reporting route, and often no single authority in charge of its own response. OPRC supplies all three. Its real achievement is institutional: it converted oil spill response from an improvised national reaction into a standing, funded, exercised system with a named authority and a contact point that answers at three in the morning.

The limb also says “preventing”, and that deserves an honest answer rather than a silent one. Strictly, OPRC is not a prevention convention: preventing pollution — discharge limits, oil filtering equipment, segregated ballast, double hulls — is MARPOL's work, and liability and compensation belong to the CLC, FUND and Bunkers regime. OPRC begins where prevention has already failed. But it contributes to prevention in three real and defensible ways:

  • It prevents escalation. The quantity of oil that reaches the shoreline is decided in the first hours, and preparedness is what shortens them. A spill contained at source is a spill that never becomes a pollution incident in any meaningful sense.
  • Reporting is preventive. Article 4 requires reporting of a probable discharge as well as an actual one — so the machinery is engaged before oil is in the water.
  • Preparedness disciplines operations. The exercises, the drills and the plan required by article 6 and by the shipboard plan raise awareness and expose weaknesses in bunkering and transfer procedures, which is where most operational spills begin.

Set beside its neighbours, the division of labour is clean: MARPOL prevents, OPRC prepares and responds, and CLC, FUND and Bunkers compensate. Above all of them, UNCLOS article 199 obliges States to develop and promote contingency plans for responding to pollution incidents, and OPRC is how that obligation was given operational content.

(b) The role of Member States, and the challenges of implementation

4. The role - and a point of precision first

OPRC binds Parties, not ships. A State's obligations arise in three different capacities, and an answer that runs them together loses the analysis the limb asks for. (Strictly, the Convention speaks of Parties rather than Member States: a State is a Member of IMO, and a Party to OPRC, and the two sets are not identical.)

CapacityWhat the State must do
As flag StateRequire ships flying its flag to carry the plan (art 3); approve it, through MARPOL Annex I reg 37.1; require reporting without delay (art 4); ensure the plan is usable in the working language of the master and officers; survey, inspect and enforce; ensure crews are drilled on it
As coastal and territorial StateThe article 6 national system — the national contingency plan, the competent national authority, the operational contact point, the pre-positioned equipment, the exercise and training programme; and the article 5 duty to assess a report and notify affected States and IMO. It also imposes the plan requirement on offshore units, sea ports and oil handling facilities under its jurisdiction
As a member of the international communityCo-operate on request (art 7); take part in research and development and technical co-operation (arts 8 and 9); conclude bilateral and regional agreements (art 10); and support IMO's clearing-house role (art 12)
DomesticallyGive the Convention force in national law — in India, at this sitting, through the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the rules made under it, with a national oil spill contingency plan and a designated response authority answering to the shape article 6 requires

The analytical point: the question asks about Member States and not only about flag States, which means the article 6 national system is squarely in scope. That is the heaviest obligation in the Convention and the one that costs money every year whether or not there is a spill.

5. The challenges - analysed

  1. Resource asymmetry. Article 6 obliges a Party to build capability “within its capabilities”. That qualification is what made the Convention ratifiable, and it is also its central weakness: the obligation is genuine but elastic, and a developing coastal State with a long shoreline and a small budget may comply in form while lacking any real Tier 2 capability. Articles 9 and 10 — technical co-operation and regional agreements — exist precisely to bridge that gap, and their success is uneven.
  2. Plans on paper against rehearsed capability. A national contingency plan is cheap to write and expensive to exercise. The gap only becomes visible during an incident, which is the worst moment to discover it. Exercising at realistic scale — multi-agency, at night, in weather — is rare because it is disruptive and costly.
  3. Inter-agency co-ordination. A national response typically involves the maritime administration, the coastguard or navy, the environment ministry, port authorities, customs, fisheries and local government. The single competent national authority required by article 6 has to have real authority over all of them, and in many States it has influence rather than command.
  4. Equipment: obsolescence and geography. Booms, skimmers and dispersant stocks degrade, need maintenance and periodic replacement, and must be positioned where the risk is. A national stockpile a thousand miles from the casualty is a line in a plan, not a capability. Dispersant approval regimes also differ between States, which complicates assistance.
  5. Friction on cross-border assistance. This is the challenge OPRC was written to solve and the one that keeps recurring: customs clearance for equipment, immigration for response personnel, liability and indemnity for the assisting State, and cost recovery. Unless these are pre-agreed in bilateral or regional arrangements, help arrives after it would have mattered.
  6. Funding and cost recovery. Preparedness is a standing cost with no visible return in the years when nothing happens, so it is politically easy to defer. Recovering response costs afterwards depends on the separate CLC and FUND regime and on the polluter being identified and solvent.
  7. Training, retention and turnover. Competence decays. Trained responders move on; contact lists go stale; the person named in the plan retired two years ago. Currency of the plan is itself a continuing obligation, not a one-off act.
  8. Reporting quality at the sharp end. Article 4's duty is only as good as what actually reaches the coastal State, and reporting is often delayed by uncertainty aboard, by fear of consequences or by unclear contact routes — which is exactly why the shipboard plan must carry a procedure and a contact list, and why they must be current.
  9. Scope creep and modern spill types. The Convention was written for persistent crude in the era of the very large tanker. Non-tanker bunker spills, container-ship casualties and lost-container incidents now form a large share of real events, and OPRC's architecture must be stretched to cover them — part of why the OPRC-HNS Protocol was needed.

The analysis in one sentence: OPRC's obligations are almost all institutional and continuing rather than technical and one-off, and institutional obligations are the kind that decay quietly. The Convention's real implementation problem is not knowing what to do; it is sustaining a capability nobody wants to pay for in the years when it is not needed.

(c) Documents required to be maintained

6. What a Member State maintains

These are derived from what articles 3, 5, 6, 7, 10 and 12 require a Party to establish and communicate. The Convention does not print them as a schedule, and it is honest to say so:

  • The National Oil Spill Contingency Plan — the central article 6 document.
  • The record of the designated competent national authority and the operational contact point, kept current and communicated to IMO.
  • The inventory of pre-positioned response equipment, with its location, condition and maintenance record.
  • The exercise and training programme and its records.
  • Bilateral and regional agreements concluded under article 10.
  • Reports and notifications to IMO and to affected States under articles 5 and 12, and the national reporting arrangements that support them.
  • The national legislation and rules giving the Convention effect — in India, the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and the rules made under it.

7. What the ship carries - and why it comes out of MARPOL

The operative document is the Shipboard Oil Pollution Emergency Plan (SOPEP). OPRC article 3 requires the Party to require it; the detail is supplied by MARPOL Annex I regulation 37. That is the single most important structural fact in this question.

  • Regulation 37.1 — every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above, and every ship other than an oil tanker of 400 gross tonnage and above, shall carry a shipboard oil pollution emergency plan approved by the Administration. The two thresholds differ and the tanker threshold is the lower.
  • Regulation 37.2 — prepared on guidelines developed by the Organization, written in the working language of the master and officers, and containing “at least” four things: the reporting procedure (37.2.1, referring to MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I); the list of authorities or persons to be contacted (37.2.2); a detailed description of immediate action by persons on board (37.2.3); and the procedures and point of contact for co-ordinating shipboard action with national and local authorities (37.2.4). “At least” makes the list a floor, not a ceiling.
  • Regulation 37.3 — where Annex II regulation 17 also applies, the plan may be combined, and the combined plan is titled “Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan” — the SMPEP. This is why a chemical tanker carries an SMPEP and not a SOPEP.
  • Regulation 37.4 — all oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more shall have prompt access to computerized, shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs.

Kept with the plan, and correctly labelled as belonging to other instruments: the coastal State contact list maintained current as part of the plan; the Oil Record Book Part I, and Part II for tankers, under Annex I regulation 17; and the IOPP Certificate and its Supplement. Listing these is fine; listing them as OPRC documents is not.

The closing point that shows the two instruments have been read together: OPRC binds States; MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 binds the ship. A single document in the wheelhouse discharges an obligation created in one convention and specified in another, and it is enforced through MARPOL's machinery — verified at statutory survey, and checked by a port State control officer — because OPRC itself gives nobody any power over a ship.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 6 + 6 + 4 and the shape follows them. Limb (a) prints “Briefly”, so identity and objectives are short and the article-by-article provisions go in a table — seven rows an examiner can scan in fifteen seconds rather than a page of prose. Limb (b) prints “analyse”, which is a different instruction from “list”: the role goes in a four-row capacity table, and the challenges are given as numbered items each with its reason, closing on a single sentence that says what the challenges have in common. Limb (c) prints “enlist”, which asks for lists, and it has two of them because the limb names two holders — Member States and ships.

The highest-value paragraph in the answer is the last one, and it is worth writing even under time pressure: OPRC binds States, MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 binds the ship. It is what explains why limb (c)'s shipboard half is answered out of a different convention from the one the question names.

The printed word 'preventing', and how to handle it

Limb (a) asks about the Convention's significance in “responding to and preventing oil pollution incidents”. OPRC is not a prevention convention — that is MARPOL — and the stem is preserved as printed rather than corrected.

The wrong responses are to ignore the word, or to write a paragraph on double hulls. The right response is the one that earns marks in both directions: state the boundary, then give three defensible senses in which preparedness is preventive — it prevents escalation, because the quantity reaching the shoreline is decided in the first hours; article 4's reporting duty covers a probable discharge and so engages before oil is in the water; and the plans, drills and exercises discipline the operations, principally bunkering and transfer, where most operational spills begin.

This is a general technique worth carrying into other questions: where a stem contains a term the instrument does not actually support, name the boundary once and then answer generously within it. Never silently ignore a printed word.

Three conventions, one incident, three questions

InstrumentQuestion it answersWhen it operates
MARPOLHow do we stop it happening?Before — construction, equipment, discharge standards
OPRCIs anyone ready, and can they help each other?Before and during — plans, authorities, equipment, response
CLC / FUND / BunkersWho pays?After — liability and compensation

Member State, not flag State - and why that changes the answer

This is the most important difference between this question and every other OPRC question. A question about the flag State's duties excludes the article 6 national system, because the contingency plan, the pre-positioned equipment and the national response organisation are owed by the State in its territorial and coastal capacity, not because it registers ships.

This question asks about Member States, so article 6 is squarely in scope — and it is the centre of the limb. It is the heaviest obligation in the Convention and the one that costs money every year whether or not there is a spill. A candidate who answers this limb as though it were a flag State question has left out the most substantial thing the Convention requires.

One further precision, worth a line: the Convention speaks of Parties, not Member States. A State is a Member of IMO and a Party to OPRC, and the two sets are not identical.

Common mistakes

  • Describing OPRC as a pollution prevention convention without qualification — or, at the other extreme, refusing to engage with the printed word “preventing” at all.
  • Bringing liability, limitation or compensation into it. That is CLC, FUND and Bunkers.
  • Saying OPRC directly requires ships to carry a SOPEP. It requires Parties to require it; MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 imposes it on the ship.
  • Answering limb (b) as a flag State question and omitting the article 6 national system.
  • Listing challenges in limb (b) when the verb is analyse — each challenge needs its reason.
  • Giving one tonnage threshold for both tankers and other ships. It is 150 for tankers, 400 for others.
  • Omitting the approval requirement or the working-language requirement.
  • Treating the four SOPEP contents as exhaustive when regulation 37.2 says at least.
  • Calling a combined plan a SOPEP — it is retitled SMPEP.
  • Listing the Oil Record Book, IOPP Certificate and Garbage Management Plan as OPRC documents without labelling their real source.
  • Missing regulation 37.4 entirely.
  • In limb (c), giving only the shipboard list when the limb expressly names Member States and ships.

Examiner traps

  • “When was OPRC adopted and when did it enter into force?” Adopted 30 November 1990; in force 13 May 1995.
  • “Which casualty led to it?” The Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989.
  • “Does OPRC prevent pollution?” Not directly — prevention is MARPOL. It prevents escalation, requires reporting of a probable discharge, and disciplines operations through drills.
  • “Which article is the heaviest obligation?” Article 6 — the national system. And it is owed in the coastal capacity, not the flag capacity.
  • “Name three things article 6 requires.” A national contingency plan; a competent national authority and operational contact point; a minimum level of pre-positioned equipment with exercises and training.
  • “Your ship is 380 GT and not a tanker. Does it need a SOPEP?” No — regulation 37.1 catches other ships at 400 GT and above. A 380 GT oil tanker would, because the tanker threshold is 150 GT.
  • “Who approves the SOPEP?” The Administration.
  • “What language must it be in?” The working language of the master and officers.
  • “Name the four things the plan must contain.” Reporting procedure; contact list; immediate action description; shipboard co-ordination procedures and point of contact.
  • “Your chemical tanker has one combined plan. What is it called?” A Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan — regulation 37.3 fixes the title.
  • “What extra does a 30,000 dwt tanker need?” Prompt access to computerized shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength calculation programs — the 5,000 dwt threshold at 37.4.
  • “Whom does the master report to after a spill?” Under article 4, without delay to the nearest coastal State.
  • “What is the single biggest obstacle to cross-border assistance?” Administrative friction — customs for equipment, immigration for people, and liability and cost recovery for the assisting State.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What do the letters OPRC stand for? Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation.
  • Which article requires the emergency plan? Article 3.
  • Which article requires reporting, and to whom? Article 4 - without delay, to the nearest coastal State.
  • Which article requires assessment and notification of affected States? Article 5.
  • Which Protocol extends OPRC to chemicals? The OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000.
  • Which MARPOL regulation actually requires the SOPEP? Annex I regulation 37.
  • Where does the Organization's role sit? Article 12 - information services, education and training, and technical services.
  • What does India's national system look like in outline? A national oil spill contingency plan with a designated response authority - the shape article 6 requires.
  • Which UNCLOS article obliges contingency planning? Article 199.
  • Name two documents a State maintains under OPRC. The national contingency plan, and the record of its competent national authority and operational contact point communicated to IMO.

Memory framework

Start from the title, because the title is the objectives: PREPAREDNESS, RESPONSE, CO-OPERATION. Then the negative in one line: not prevention, not compensation — and then the three senses in which it is preventive, so the printed word is answered.

For the provisions, five verbs in article order: PLAN (3), REPORT (4), REACT (5), BUILD (6), SHARE (7 to 10), with IMO (12) on the end.

For the role, three hats: FLAG, COAST, WORLD — and remember that because the question says Member State, the coastal hat is in.

For the challenges, four words: MONEY, PRACTICE, PEOPLE, BORDERS. For the documents, two holders and one number pair: the State keeps the PLAN, AUTHORITY, EQUIPMENT, AGREEMENTS; the ship keeps the SOPEP — 150 and 400, and REPORT, CONTACT, ACT, CO-ORDINATE.

Regulation and source map

  • OPRC 1990 — adopted 30 November 1990, in force 13 May 1995. Unamended.
  • Article 3 emergency plans; article 4 reporting without delay to the nearest coastal State; article 5 assess and notify; article 6 national and regional systems; article 7 co-operation on request; articles 8 to 10 R&D, technical co-operation and bilateral agreements; article 12 IMO's institutional role.
  • OPRC-HNS Protocol, 2000 — the same architecture for hazardous and noxious substances.
  • MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 — the shipboard duty. 37.1 thresholds and approval; 37.2 guidelines, working language and the four contents; 37.3 combination and the SMPEP title; 37.4 shore-based damage stability access for tankers of 5,000 dwt and above. Unamended at this sitting.
  • MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I — the reporting obligation regulation 37.2.1 cross-refers to. Annex I regulation 17 — the Oil Record Book. Annex II regulation 17 — the NLS emergency plan.
  • UNCLOS article 199 — contingency plans against pollution; article 194 — the general duty.
  • India at this sitting: the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 and rules made under it.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The evidence splits cleanly and the split is declared rather than hidden.

The shipboard document limb is primary. MARPOL Annex I as revised is held in the True Source corpus with a full extractable text layer — resolution MEPC.117(52) — and regulation 37 was read from it in full. The thresholds, the approval requirement, the language rule, the four contents, the combination rule and the 5,000 dwt provision all come from that text.

The OPRC Convention itself is not held. That is an acquisition gap already recorded in the corpus's own question-mapping records, not an oversight of this session. The Convention's dates and its article-by-article content are carried at authoritative-secondary level; no article of OPRC is quoted verbatim anywhere in this answer and no article is attributed a sub-paragraph.

The list of documents a Member State maintains is derived, not quoted. It is what articles 3, 5, 6, 7, 10 and 12 require a Party to establish and communicate. The Convention does not print it as a schedule, and the answer says so rather than presenting a derived list as a printed one.

The challenges analysis is reasoned judgement. No compliance statistic, party count, national capability assessment or comparative study is cited, and none is available from a held source. The Indian national system is described in outline only — the shape an article 6 system takes, and the statute in force at the sitting — because no Indian contingency-plan instrument or DG Shipping circular is held. No response equipment specification, stockpile quantity or tier-response threshold is quoted; tiered response is industry practice, not a Convention requirement.

Currency, and one reversal. OPRC is unamended and regulation 37 is unamended at this sitting. The corpus records two MARPOL Annex I amendments beyond the held base — MEPC.359(79), in force 1 May 2024, and MEPC.384(81), an MEPC 81 output of 2024 — and both are future at November 2023. The later paper in this family carries a currency note on MEPC.384(81) because it amends the Protocol I that regulation 37.2.1 cross-refers to; here that amendment does not yet exist, so the note is dropped entirely rather than re-stated. Nothing about container loss reporting belongs in this answer.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 7 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What OPRC is, and what it deliberately is not
  2. __________The key provisions, article by article
  3. __________Significance - in response, and in the sense in which it touches prevention
  4. __________The role - and a point of precision first
  5. __________The challenges - analysed
  6. __________What a Member State maintains
  7. __________What the ship carries - and why it comes out of MARPOL
Flashcards 11

The International Convention on Oil Pollution Preparedness, Response and Co-operation, 1990. Adopted 30 November 1990; entered into force 13 May 1995, developed after the Exxon Valdez grounding of 1989. Its three objectives are the three nouns of its title: preparedness - plans, authorities, equipment and training in place beforehand; response - the ability to act promptly and effectively; and co-operation - mutual assistance with the friction removed in advance.

The title carries the objectives, which makes this the cheapest structured mark on the question.

Not directly - prevention is MARPOL's work and OPRC begins where prevention has failed. But three senses are defensible: it prevents escalation, because the quantity reaching the shoreline is decided in the first hours; article 4 requires reporting of a PROBABLE discharge, so the machinery engages before oil is in the water; and the plans, drills and exercises discipline bunkering and transfer operations, where most operational spills begin.

The stem prints the word, so it has to be answered - stating the boundary and then answering generously within it is the technique.

Because the article 6 national system - the contingency plan, the competent national authority and operational contact point, the pre-positioned equipment and the exercise and training programme - is owed by the State in its coastal and territorial capacity, not because it registers ships. A flag State question excludes it; a Member State question puts it at the centre, and it is the heaviest obligation in the Convention.

It is the single feature that separates this question from every other OPRC question in the corpus.

Article 3 emergency plans - ships, offshore units, sea ports and oil handling facilities. Article 4 reporting without delay to the nearest coastal State. Article 5 assess the report and notify affected States and the Organization. Article 6 the national system - contingency plan, competent authority, contact point, pre-positioned equipment, exercises and training. Article 7 co-operation in response on request. Articles 8 to 10 research and development, technical co-operation, bilateral and multilateral agreements. Article 12 IMO's institutional role.

Plan, report, react, build, share - the sequence is how the architecture stays recallable under pressure.

Not directly. Article 3 requires each Party to REQUIRE ships flying its flag to have an oil pollution emergency plan in accordance with provisions adopted by the Organization. Those provisions are MARPOL Annex I regulation 37, which supplies the thresholds, the approval requirement, the language rule and the contents. OPRC binds States; regulation 37 binds the ship.

It is the structural fact the whole question turns on, and it explains why limb (c)'s shipboard half is answered out of MARPOL.

MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.1: every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above, and every ship other than an oil tanker of 400 gross tonnage and above. The plan must be approved by the Administration. The thresholds differ and the tanker one is the lower.

Candidates routinely give a single figure for both, which loses the mark outright.

Under regulation 37.2, and the plan shall consist AT LEAST of: the procedure for the master or person in charge to report an oil pollution incident, as required by MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I; the list of authorities or persons to be contacted; a detailed description of the action to be taken immediately by persons on board; and the procedures and point of contact on the ship for co-ordinating shipboard action with national and local authorities.

Report, contact, act, co-ordinate - and that is also the order the plan is used in during a real incident.

The national oil spill contingency plan; the record of its competent national authority and operational contact point, communicated to IMO; the pre-positioned equipment inventory with location and maintenance record; the exercise and training programme and its records; bilateral and regional agreements under article 10; and the national legislation giving the Convention effect. The list is DERIVED from what the articles require a Party to establish - the Convention prints no schedule of State documents.

Limb (c) names two holders, and most answers give only the shipboard half.

A Shipboard marine pollution emergency plan (SMPEP). Regulation 37.3 permits the SOPEP to be combined with the plan for noxious liquid substances required by MARPOL Annex II regulation 17, and provides that the combined plan shall be so titled.

The retitling is expressly in the regulation and explains why chemical tankers do not carry a document called a SOPEP.

Resource asymmetry - article 6 requires capability 'within its capabilities', which made the Convention ratifiable but leaves the obligation elastic. Plans against practice - a contingency plan is cheap to write and expensive to exercise, and the gap shows only during an incident. Cross-border friction - customs for equipment, immigration for responders, liability and cost recovery, all of which must be pre-agreed or assistance arrives too late. People - competence decays and contact lists go stale, so currency of the plan is a continuing obligation.

The limb prints 'analyse', so each challenge must carry its reason - money, practice, people, borders.

MARPOL asks how we stop it happening - construction, equipment and discharge standards, before the event. OPRC asks whether anyone is ready and whether they can help each other - plans, authorities, equipment and response, before and during. CLC, FUND and Bunkers ask who pays - liability and compensation, afterwards. Above them all, UNCLOS article 199 obliges States to develop contingency plans against pollution.

Three instruments, one incident, three questions - it keeps prevention and compensation material out of an OPRC answer.

Quick revision
15-second recall
OPRC 1990 — adopted 30 November 1990, in force 13 May 1995, after the Exxon Valdez. Objectives are its three title words: preparedness, response, co-operation. It is not prevention (MARPOL) and not liability (CLC/FUND/Bunkers) — but it prevents escalation, article 4 covers a probable discharge, and drills discipline bunkering and transfer. Provisions: art 3 emergency plans (ships, offshore units, ports, oil handling facilities); art 4 report without delay to the nearest coastal State; art 5 assess and notify affected States and IMO; art 6 national system — contingency plan, competent national authority, operational contact point, pre-positioned equipment, exercises and training; art 7 co-operate on request; arts 8–10 R&D, technical co-operation, bilateral agreements; art 12 IMO. Member State, not flag State: three hats — flag (require, approve, enforce), coastal (the article 6 system, which is IN scope here), international (co-operate, agree, support IMO). Challenges: money, practice, people, borders — resource asymmetry under 'within its capabilities', plans not exercised, inter-agency authority, equipment obsolescence and geography, customs and immigration friction, funding, turnover and stale contact lists. Documents: the State keeps the national plan, the authority and contact point notified to IMO, the equipment inventory, the exercise records and the regional agreements — derived, not a printed schedule. The ship keeps the SOPEP under MARPOL Annex I reg 37: tankers 150 GT, others 400 GT, approved by the Administration, in the working language of master and officers, containing at least reporting procedure, contact list, immediate actions and co-ordination point; combined with the Annex II plan it is retitled SMPEP (37.3); tankers of 5,000 dwt need shore-based damage stability access (37.4). OPRC binds States; regulation 37 binds the ship.
