Critical numbers
  • OPRC adopted 30 November 1990; entered into force 13 May 1995
  • SOPEP threshold: oil tankers of 150 gross tonnage and above
  • SOPEP threshold: ships other than oil tankers of 400 gross tonnage and above
  • MARPOL Annex I regulation 37.2 lists FOUR mandatory contents and says 'at least'
  • Regulation 37.4 - oil tankers of 5,000 tons deadweight or more need prompt access to shore-based damage stability and residual structural strength programs
  • OPRC-HNS Protocol adopted 2000
  • The reporting cross-reference is MARPOL article 8 and Protocol I
Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 is the provision that actually reaches the ship, and it is unamended at this sitting. Regulation 37.1 requires every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above and every other ship of 400 gross tonnage and above to carry a shipboard oil pollution emergency plan approved by the Administration; 37.2 requires it to be based on the Organization's guidelines, written in the working language of the master and officers, and to consist at least of the reporting procedure, the contact list, the description of immediate action and the shipboard co-ordination procedures and point of contact. OPRC article 3 obliges the Party to require the plan; regulation 37 is what specifies it.
Major trap
Answering limb (b) as though it were a flag State question. It is not — it asks about Member States, and that puts the article 6 national system squarely in scope: the national contingency plan, the competent national authority and operational contact point, the pre-positioned equipment and the exercise and training programme. That is the heaviest obligation in the Convention and it is owed in the coastal and territorial capacity, so an answer confined to flag duties omits the most substantial thing OPRC requires. The paired traps are believing that OPRC itself requires the ship to carry a SOPEP — it binds States, and MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 binds the ship — and ignoring the printed word “preventing” instead of stating the boundary and answering within it.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q5 - the AFS Convention, the other pollution instrument on this paper, and a clean contrast in that it does regulate a substance directly · QP2311 Q9 - P&I cover, which is where the cost of an oil pollution incident actually lands on the owner

First in set First set here. The same task returns at April 2025 Q4.

Understand this first

Everything in this question follows from one fact: the paint that used to work best was banned because it poisoned the sea, and the industry has been living with the replacements ever since. Those replacements fall into two quite different camps. One camp still uses a poison, just a gentler one, and manages how fast it is released — either by letting the paint dissolve away, or by having it wear off smoothly as the water rushes past. The other camp poisons nothing at all: it makes the hull so slippery and so soft that barnacles and weed cannot get a proper grip, and the flow of water simply peels them off once the ship is moving. Both the roughness question and the oil question fall out of that split. The dissolving paints get rougher as they age, because what is left behind after the soluble part has gone is a spent, porous crust; the self-wearing ones stay smooth because the outer skin is constantly renewed; and the slippery ones start smoothest of all but only stay clean if the ship actually goes somewhere. Their weakness is exactly that: they need a certain speed before the water can strip the growth off. Blending a light oil into the slippery paint attacks that weakness — the oil creeps to the surface and makes it slipperier still, so less water flow is needed to shed the growth — but the oil supply is finite, and when it has gone the coating is back where it started. The last part of the question is about the people. Whatever goes on the hull has to be sprayed at enormous pressure onto a surface that first has to be blasted clean of the old, often far more toxic, coating — and that job is the most dangerous part of the whole operation.

Knowledge map
Antifouling Alternatives - Hull Roughness, Oil-Enhanced Foul Release, and Application Safety
  1. 1The setting, in two sentences
    • the AFS Convention 2001 entered into force 17 September 2008 and prohibited organotin compounds acting as biocides
    • the APPLICATION ban ran from 1 January 2003 and the PRESENCE ban from 1 January 2008 - every coating below exists because tributyltin was withdrawn
    • IAFS Certificate for ships of 400 GT and above on international voyages; a Declaration for ships of 24 metres or more below that
  2. 2What hull roughness is, and why it costs money
    • average hull roughness is reported in micrometres from a stylus gauge traversed over the hull, averaging the maximum peak-to-trough height over successive short intervals
    • it matters because it sits inside the boundary layer - roughness thickens the turbulent boundary layer and raises FRICTIONAL RESISTANCE, the dominant component for a full-form ship
    • as an order of magnitude only, a newbuilding is in the region of 100 micrometres, and roughness accumulates over successive dockings as paint goes over paint
  3. 3The four families compared
    • CDP - moderate at application, and ROUGHENS FASTEST of the four: the soluble matrix dissolves unevenly leaving a thick porous leached layer, and successive over-coating compounds it
    • SPC - a smooth uniform film at application, and roughness STABLE OR IMPROVING: hydrolysis plus shear removes the outermost layer continuously so the surface is polished by service and the leached layer stays thin
    • SPC therefore suits long intervals and high-activity ships
  4. 4First, the mechanism being enhanced
    • a foul release coating KILLS NOTHING - it works on adhesion
    • LOW SURFACE ENERGY - the silicone surface is poorly wetted, so biological adhesives cannot spread and key into it
    • LOW ELASTIC MODULUS - the film is soft and deforms under the attachment, peeling away from it rather than resisting in shear
  5. 5What incorporated oil does
    • silicone oils are blended into the matrix but NOT chemically bound into it, so being mobile and incompatible with the cured network they migrate steadily to the surface
    • they form a thin, continuously replenished LIQUID layer on top of the solid film, and every benefit follows from that
    • LOWER EFFECTIVE SURFACE ENERGY - the organism meets a mobile liquid rather than a solid, and the surface will not hold still
  6. 6The limits - which is where the marks are
    • THE OIL IS A FINITE RESERVOIR - it migrates out and is lost to the sea, so the enhancement DECAYS across the docking interval
    • the coating ends its life closer to an unmodified foul release film than it began
    • depletion is not uniform - it runs faster where shear and temperature are highest, so performance varies over the hull
  7. 7The hazards
    • the hazard is THE WHOLE COATING SYSTEM - primer, tie coat, and the removal of the old scheme - not only the tin alternatives
    • SOLVENT VAPOUR - narcosis, headache, loss of co-ordination, dermatitis; and flammable, so a fire and explosion hazard in any enclosed space
    • ISOCYANATES AND EPOXY AMINES in tie coats and primers - respiratory sensitisation and occupational asthma, skin sensitisation and burns; sensitisation is PERMANENT
  8. 8The controls, in the order they must be applied
    • ELIMINATE OR SUBSTITUTE - low-solvent, high-solids or water-borne schemes where the specification allows, and a non-biocidal foul release system where the trade suits it
    • ENGINEERING CONTROLS - mechanical ventilation and forced extraction in every enclosed space, wet or vacuum blasting to suppress dust, containment and screening of the dock with collection of spent abrasive, explosion-protected lighting, bonding and earthing
    • ADMINISTRATIVE CONTROLS - safety data sheets read BEFORE the work is planned; risk assessment and method statement; permit to work with separate enclosed-space entry and hot work permits; atmosphere testing before and during entry; standby person and rehearsed rescue plan; exclusion zones and control of simultaneous operations; exposure time limits; training and health surveillance for sensitisers
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. The setting, in two sentences
  2. What hull roughness is, and why it costs money
  3. The four families compared
  4. First, the mechanism being enhanced
  5. What incorporated oil does
  6. The limits - which is where the marks are
  7. The hazards
  8. The controls, in the order they must be applied

Memory cue. Four families by what the film does: DISSOLVES, POLISHES, BLENDS, RELEASES (1). Limb (a) asks one thing four times: what happens over three years (2, 3). Limb (b) is one line: SOFT AND SLIPPERY, and oil makes it slipperier but not for ever (4, 5, 6). Limb (c) hierarchy: ELIMINATE, ENGINEER, ADMINISTER, PROTECT, DISPOSE (8).

Remember8 route headings
Cover64 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 64 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The setting, in two sentences
  • the AFS Convention 2001 entered into force 17 September 2008 and prohibited organotin compounds acting as biocides
  • the APPLICATION ban ran from 1 January 2003 and the PRESENCE ban from 1 January 2008 - every coating below exists because tributyltin was withdrawn
  • IAFS Certificate for ships of 400 GT and above on international voyages; a Declaration for ships of 24 metres or more below that
  • MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, added CYBUTRYNE and the control APPLIED FROM 1 JANUARY 2023 - ten months before this examination
  • a cybutryne system may no longer be applied or re-applied, and an existing one must be removed or over-coated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled anti-fouling renewal after that date and no later than sixty months after last application
  • the four tin-free families: CONTROLLED DEPLETION POLYMER - a soluble rosin matrix that dissolves and leaves a leached layer
  • SELF-POLISHING COPOLYMER - an acrylate binder that hydrolyses at the surface and is sheared away, so the film polishes and renews itself
  • TIN-FREE HYBRID - a blend of rosin and acrylate, part depletion and part polishing
  • FOUL RELEASE - non-biocidal silicone or fluoropolymer of low surface energy and low modulus, from which fouling is released by the water flow
2. What hull roughness is, and why it costs money
  • average hull roughness is reported in micrometres from a stylus gauge traversed over the hull, averaging the maximum peak-to-trough height over successive short intervals
  • it matters because it sits inside the boundary layer - roughness thickens the turbulent boundary layer and raises FRICTIONAL RESISTANCE, the dominant component for a full-form ship
  • as an order of magnitude only, a newbuilding is in the region of 100 micrometres, and roughness accumulates over successive dockings as paint goes over paint
  • FULL REMOVAL TO BARE STEEL RESETS IT; OVER-COATING DOES NOT
  • PHYSICAL roughness - paint texture, application defects, the porous leached layer, damage, weld seams and plate undulation - is permanent until the surface is treated
  • BIOLOGICAL roughness - slime, weed and shell - is removable, but for the same nominal height it costs far more because it is compliant, irregular and thick
  • THE CONSEQUENCE - the smoothest coating is not automatically the best, and a smooth fouled surface is worse than a clean rougher one
  • so limb a) must compare roughness behaviour ACROSS THE DOCKING INTERVAL, not at the moment of application
3. The four families compared
  • CDP - moderate at application, and ROUGHENS FASTEST of the four: the soluble matrix dissolves unevenly leaving a thick porous leached layer, and successive over-coating compounds it
  • SPC - a smooth uniform film at application, and roughness STABLE OR IMPROVING: hydrolysis plus shear removes the outermost layer continuously so the surface is polished by service and the leached layer stays thin
  • SPC therefore suits long intervals and high-activity ships
  • HYBRID - between the two at application and between the two through the interval, at intermediate cost
  • FOUL RELEASE - THE SMOOTHEST AT APPLICATION, with no leached layer at all because it does not leach
  • but it is MECHANICALLY SOFT - tug contact, ice, grounding and hard brushing damage it, repair is difficult, and on a slow or idle ship biological roughness takes over
  • REPAIR AND OVER-COATING DIFFER - a self-polishing scheme can be over-coated with a compatible scheme, but nothing adheres to a foul release surface, so recoating means full removal or an approved tie coat
  • THE CLEANING REGIME INTERACTS WITH THE COATING - aggressive brushing removes an SPC's polishing film and physically damages a foul release coating
4. First, the mechanism being enhanced
  • a foul release coating KILLS NOTHING - it works on adhesion
  • LOW SURFACE ENERGY - the silicone surface is poorly wetted, so biological adhesives cannot spread and key into it
  • LOW ELASTIC MODULUS - the film is soft and deforms under the attachment, peeling away from it rather than resisting in shear
  • the established relationship: THE FORCE REQUIRED TO RELEASE AN ADHERED ORGANISM SCALES WITH THE SQUARE ROOT OF THE PRODUCT OF ELASTIC MODULUS AND SURFACE ENERGY
  • which is why BOTH must be low, and why a hard low-energy surface such as glass is not a foul release coating
  • release in service is by HYDRODYNAMIC SHEAR, so the coating has a THRESHOLD SPEED below which fouling stays
  • that threshold is the weakness, and it is what oil incorporation is aimed at
5. What incorporated oil does
  • silicone oils are blended into the matrix but NOT chemically bound into it, so being mobile and incompatible with the cured network they migrate steadily to the surface
  • they form a thin, continuously replenished LIQUID layer on top of the solid film, and every benefit follows from that
  • LOWER EFFECTIVE SURFACE ENERGY - the organism meets a mobile liquid rather than a solid, and the surface will not hold still
  • INTERFERENCE WITH ADHESIVE CURING - biological glues need a stable substrate to cure against, and the oil disrupts anchoring at the moment of settlement
  • A LOWER RELEASE THRESHOLD - less shear is needed to detach what does settle, so fouling releases at a LOWER SHIP SPEED, which is the most valuable practical gain
  • BETTER STATIC PERFORMANCE - idle time in port or at anchor is where a plain foul release coating is weakest, and the oil layer resists settlement while stopped
  • A DEGREE OF SELF-HEALING - oil migrating into a minor scratch partially restores the low-energy surface
  • IT REMAINS NON-BIOCIDAL IN MECHANISM - nothing is poisoned; the surface is simply made harder to hold on to
  • related approaches attack the same threshold - amphiphilic surfaces combining water-loving and water-repelling segments, and silicone-hydrogel systems holding a bound water layer
6. The limits - which is where the marks are
  • THE OIL IS A FINITE RESERVOIR - it migrates out and is lost to the sea, so the enhancement DECAYS across the docking interval
  • the coating ends its life closer to an unmodified foul release film than it began
  • depletion is not uniform - it runs faster where shear and temperature are highest, so performance varies over the hull
  • REPAIR AND OVER-COATING ARE HARDER STILL - unbound oil at the surface is exactly what prevents anything from sticking, including a repair patch
  • THE REGULATORY QUESTION IS OPEN - the Convention controls systems whose substances act as BIOCIDES; oil-amended systems are put forward as non-biocidal and that is the mechanism, but deliberate release into the sea attracts scrutiny and the position should be checked against the ship's approval rather than assumed
  • oil does not make the coating mechanically tougher, does not remove the threshold speed, and does not change the cost of application or the difficulty of damage repair
  • THE HONEST SUMMARY - oil incorporation widens the operating window at the cost of a benefit that fades, and does not cure mechanical fragility
7. The hazards
  • the hazard is THE WHOLE COATING SYSTEM - primer, tie coat, and the removal of the old scheme - not only the tin alternatives
  • SOLVENT VAPOUR - narcosis, headache, loss of co-ordination, dermatitis; and flammable, so a fire and explosion hazard in any enclosed space
  • ISOCYANATES AND EPOXY AMINES in tie coats and primers - respiratory sensitisation and occupational asthma, skin sensitisation and burns; sensitisation is PERMANENT
  • BIOCIDES in the antifouling - copper and organic biocides, irritant and toxic by skin contact, mist inhalation and ingestion
  • REMOVAL OF THE OLD COATING - blasting dust carrying heavy metals, organotin residues on older hulls, and now cybutryne; often the most toxic operation of the job
  • AIRLESS SPRAY - very high pressure, and HIGH-PRESSURE INJECTION INJURY looks like a pinprick and is a SURGICAL EMERGENCY
  • ENCLOSED AND CONFINED SPACES - oxygen deficiency, heavier-than-air solvent vapour, explosive atmosphere
  • PHYSICAL - work at height on staging, noise from blasting, dust, slips on overspray, simultaneous operations and hot work nearby
8. The controls, in the order they must be applied
  • ELIMINATE OR SUBSTITUTE - low-solvent, high-solids or water-borne schemes where the specification allows, and a non-biocidal foul release system where the trade suits it
  • ENGINEERING CONTROLS - mechanical ventilation and forced extraction in every enclosed space, wet or vacuum blasting to suppress dust, containment and screening of the dock with collection of spent abrasive, explosion-protected lighting, bonding and earthing
  • ADMINISTRATIVE CONTROLS - safety data sheets read BEFORE the work is planned; risk assessment and method statement; permit to work with separate enclosed-space entry and hot work permits; atmosphere testing before and during entry; standby person and rehearsed rescue plan; exclusion zones and control of simultaneous operations; exposure time limits; training and health surveillance for sensitisers
  • PPE LAST, NEVER FIRST - air-fed positive-pressure hood or full-face breathing apparatus for spray application and for isocyanate work, a cartridge respirator being insufficient; chemical-resistant coveralls and gloves selected against the actual product; eye and face protection, hearing protection, safety footwear, harness where there is a fall risk
  • EMERGENCY ARRANGEMENTS - eyewash and emergency shower at the workface, fire watch and extinguishing media, and one briefed instruction: ANY SUSPECTED HIGH-PRESSURE INJECTION INJURY GOES TO HOSPITAL IMMEDIATELY however trivial the wound looks
  • WASTE AND ENVIRONMENT - spent abrasive and paint residues containing biocides are HAZARDOUS WASTE, contained, collected and landed ashore to a licensed receiver; nothing washed into the dock or over the side; removed cybutryne and organotin residues handled on that basis without exception
  • the regulatory frame named rather than quoted - MLC 2006 Regulation 4.3, ISM Code sections 7 and 8, the SOLAS enclosed-space entry and rescue drill requirement, the ship's permit-to-work system, and the yard's own regime which usually controls work done in dock
  • THE CLOSING JUDGEMENT - the choice of anti-fouling system settles how rough the hull will be in three years, whether the coating works at the speed the ship actually steams, and what the people applying and later removing it are exposed to
Model written answer

1. The setting, in two sentences

The Anti-fouling Systems Convention, 2001, in force 17 September 2008, prohibited organotin compounds acting as biocides — the application ban from 1 January 2003 and the presence ban from 1 January 2008 — and every coating discussed below exists because tributyltin was withdrawn. Ships of 400 gross tonnage and above on international voyages carry an International Anti-fouling System Certificate; smaller ships of 24 metres or more carry a Declaration.

One control is fresh at this sitting and is worth a mark. MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, added cybutryne to the controlled substances, and the control applied from 1 January 2023 — ten months before this examination. A cybutryne-containing system may no longer be applied or re-applied, and an existing one must be removed or over-coated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system after that date and in any event no later than sixty months after its last application.

The four tin-free families, which limb (a) then compares:

FamilyHow it works
Controlled depletion polymer (CDP)A soluble rosin-based matrix. Seawater dissolves the binder and releases biocide, leaving a spent leached layer behind
Self-polishing copolymer (SPC), tin-freeAn acrylate binder that hydrolyses at the surface and is then removed by hydrodynamic shear, so the film polishes at a controlled rate and continuously renews itself
Tin-free hybridA blend of rosin and acrylate chemistries; part depletion, part polishing
Foul release (FR)Non-biocidal. A silicone or fluoropolymer surface of low surface energy and low modulus, to which fouling adheres so weakly that it is released by the water flow once the ship is moving

(a) Hull roughness of the different types

2. What hull roughness is, and why it costs money

  • Hull roughness is the texture of the underwater surface, reported as average hull roughness in micrometres: a stylus gauge is traversed over the hull and the maximum peak-to-trough height over each short interval is averaged.
  • It matters because it sits inside the boundary layer. A rough surface thickens the turbulent boundary layer and raises frictional resistance, which for a full-form ship is the dominant part of total resistance — so roughness is paid for directly in shaft power and fuel.
  • As orders of magnitude only: a newbuilding is typically in the region of 100 micrometres average hull roughness, and roughness accumulates over successive dockings as paint is applied over paint. Full removal to bare steel resets it; over-coating does not.

The distinction that must come first. Roughness has two entirely different sources and they behave differently:

  • Physical roughness — the paint film's own texture, application defects such as orange peel, dry spray and runs, the porous leached layer, mechanical damage, weld seams and plate undulation. It is permanent until the surface is treated.
  • Biological roughness — slime, weed and shell. It is removable, and for the same nominal height it is far more expensive than physical roughness because it is compliant, irregular and thick.

The consequence for the comparison: the smoothest coating is not automatically the best. A very smooth foul release surface that has fouled because the ship idles is worse than a rougher self-polishing surface that is clean. Limb (a) is answered properly only by comparing roughness behaviour across the whole docking interval, not at the moment of application.

3. The four families compared

FamilyAt applicationThrough the interval, and why
CDPModerate — rosin systems give a relatively coarse filmRoughness increases, and increases fastest of the four — the soluble matrix dissolves unevenly, leaving a thick and porous leached layer that is itself rough; and because CDP schemes are usually over-coated rather than removed, roughness compounds from docking to docking
SPCGood — a smooth, uniform filmRoughness is stable or improves — hydrolysis plus hydrodynamic shear removes the outermost layer continuously, so the surface is polished by service; the leached layer stays very thin and constant. This is why SPC suits long intervals and high-activity ships
HybridBetween the twoBetween the two — some polishing, some leaching — blended chemistry gives intermediate behaviour, at intermediate cost
Foul releaseThe smoothest of allVery low if the ship keeps moving; poor if she does not — the silicone film flows out to a near-glassy surface and does not leach at all, so there is no leached layer. But it is mechanically soft — tug contact, ice, groundings and hard brushing damage it, damage is difficult to repair invisibly, and biological roughness takes over on a slow or idle ship

Two practical points that separate a good answer. First, repair and over-coating behave differently: a self-polishing scheme can normally be over-coated with a compatible scheme, whereas a foul release surface has such low surface energy that nothing adheres to it — recoating means full removal or an approved tie coat, and that cost belongs in any roughness comparison. Second, the hull cleaning regime interacts with the coating: aggressive brushing that a hard scheme tolerates will remove the polishing film of an SPC and physically damage a foul release coating, so the cleaning method must be chosen with the coating and not against it.

(b) Enhancement of foul release coatings by oil incorporation

4. First, the mechanism being enhanced

A foul release coating kills nothing. It works on adhesion. Two surface properties govern how firmly an organism can hold on:

  1. Low surface energy. A silicone surface is poorly wetted, so the biological adhesives that barnacles and tubeworms secrete cannot spread and key into it.
  2. Low elastic modulus. The film is soft and flexible, so it deforms under the organism's attachment and peels away from it rather than resisting in shear.

The established relationship is that the force required to release an adhered organism scales with the square root of the product of the coating's elastic modulus and its surface energy — which is exactly why both have to be low, and why a hard low-energy coating is not a foul release coating. In service the release is done by hydrodynamic shear, so the coating has a threshold speed: below it, fouling stays.

That threshold is the weakness, and it is what oil incorporation is aimed at.

5. What incorporated oil does

Silicone oils are blended into the matrix but are not chemically bound into it. Being mobile and incompatible with the cured network, they migrate steadily toward the surface and form a thin, continuously replenished liquid layer on top of the solid film. The benefits follow from that one fact:

  • Lower effective surface energy. The organism now meets a mobile liquid rather than a solid, so the surface it is trying to bond to will not hold still.
  • Interference with adhesive curing. Biological glues need a stable substrate to cure against; the oil layer disrupts anchoring at the moment of settlement, which is the cheapest moment to defeat it.
  • A lower release threshold. Less shear stress is needed to detach what does settle, so fouling releases at a lower ship speed — the single most valuable practical gain, because it widens the range of ships and trades a non-biocidal coating can serve.
  • Better static performance. Idle time in port or at anchor is where a plain foul release coating is weakest; the oil layer improves resistance to settlement while the ship is stopped.
  • A degree of self-healing. Oil migrating into a minor scratch or abrasion partially restores the low-energy surface where the solid film has been marked.
  • It remains non-biocidal in mechanism. Nothing is being poisoned — the surface is simply made harder to hold on to. That is the whole commercial argument for the technology after tributyltin.

Related approaches attack the same threshold differently — amphiphilic surfaces combining water-loving and water-repelling segments so that no single adhesive chemistry works well, and silicone-hydrogel systems that hold a bound water layer at the interface. Oil incorporation is the simplest and the most widely used of the family.

6. The limits - which is where the marks are

  • The oil is a finite reservoir. It migrates out and is lost to the sea, so the enhancement decays across the docking interval. The coating ends its life closer to an unmodified foul release film than it began.
  • Depletion is not uniform. It runs faster where shear and temperature are highest, so performance varies over the hull.
  • Repair and over-coating are harder still. Unbound oil at the surface is exactly what prevents anything from sticking — including a repair patch. Surface preparation before any repair is critical and often imperfect in a dock.
  • The regulatory question is open. The Convention controls systems whose substances act as biocides. Oil-amended foul release systems are put forward as non-biocidal, and that is the mechanism; but the deliberate release of a substance into the sea to prevent settlement attracts scrutiny, and the position should be checked against the ship's approval rather than assumed. This answer takes no position on it.
  • The cost and the caveats survive. Oil does not make the coating mechanically tougher, does not remove the threshold speed, and does not change the fact that a foul release scheme is expensive to apply and unforgiving to damage.

The honest summary: oil incorporation widens the operating window of a foul release coating — lower speeds, more idle time, better early life — at the cost of a benefit that fades, and it does not cure the coating's mechanical fragility.

(c) Personnel and health safety during application

7. The hazards

Note first that the hazard is the whole coating system, not only the tin alternatives: the primer, the tie coat and the removal of the old scheme are all in scope.

HazardWhat it does
Solvent vapourNarcosis, headache, loss of co-ordination, dermatitis; and the vapour is flammable, so it is a fire and explosion hazard in any enclosed space
Isocyanates and epoxy amines in tie coats and primersRespiratory sensitisation and occupational asthma; skin sensitisation and burns. Sensitisation is permanent - once acquired, the person cannot work with the material again
Biocides in the antifouling itselfCopper and organic biocides are irritant and toxic by skin contact, inhalation of mist and ingestion
Removal of the OLD coatingBlasting dust carrying heavy metals and, on older hulls, organotin residues - and cybutryne, which now has to come off. This is often the most toxic operation of the whole job
Airless sprayVery high pressure. High-pressure injection injury - fluid driven through the skin from a leak or a careless hand - looks like a pinprick and is a surgical emergency
Enclosed and confined spacesOxygen deficiency, accumulation of heavier-than-air solvent vapour, and an explosive atmosphere
PhysicalWork at height on staging and cradles, noise from blasting, dust, slips on overspray, and dry-dock hazards including simultaneous operations and hot work nearby

8. The controls, in the order they must be applied

  1. Eliminate or substitute. Choose a low-solvent, high-solids or water-borne scheme where the specification allows, and a non-biocidal foul release system where the trade suits it. The safest exposure is the one that never occurs.
  2. Engineering controls. Mechanical ventilation and forced extraction in every enclosed space; wet or vacuum blasting to suppress dust; containment and screening of the dock, with collection of spent abrasive and paint residue; explosion-protected lighting and equipment; bonding and earthing.
  3. Administrative controls. Read the safety data sheets before the work is planned, not after. Risk assessment and method statement; permit to work, with a separate enclosed-space entry permit and hot work permit; atmosphere testing for oxygen, flammable vapour and contaminants before entry and continuously during it; a standby person and a rehearsed rescue plan; exclusion zones and control of simultaneous operations; limits on exposure time; training and health surveillance for those working with sensitisers.
  4. Personal protective equipment — last, never first. Air-fed positive-pressure hood or full-face breathing apparatus for spray application and for any work with isocyanates — a cartridge respirator is not sufficient for spraying. Chemical-resistant coveralls and gloves selected against the actual product, eye and face protection, hearing protection, safety footwear, and a harness where there is a fall risk.
  5. Emergency arrangements. Eyewash and emergency shower at the workface; fire watch and extinguishing media; and one specific instruction that must be briefed: any suspected high-pressure injection injury goes to hospital immediately, however trivial the wound looks.
  6. Waste and environment. Spent abrasive and paint residues containing biocides are hazardous waste. They are contained, collected and landed ashore to a licensed receiver; nothing is washed into the dock or over the side, and the dock's own runoff is contained. Removed cybutryne and organotin residues are handled on that basis without exception.

The regulatory frame, named rather than quoted: MLC 2006 Regulation 4.3 on health and safety protection and accident prevention; the ISM Code at sections 7 and 8, under which the procedures and the emergency arrangements are written into the safety management system; the SOLAS requirement for enclosed-space entry and rescue drills; the ship's own permit-to-work system; and the yard's occupational health regime, which for work done in dock is usually the controlling one.

The closing judgement. The three limbs of this question meet at one point: the choice of an anti-fouling system is not only an efficiency decision. It settles how rough the hull will be in three years, whether the coating works at the speed the ship actually steams, and what the people applying and later removing it will be exposed to — and the last of those is the one that is decided by whoever writes the specification, not by whoever holds the spray gun.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 6 + 6 + 4 against three limbs which have almost nothing to do with one another, and the biggest structural risk is spending the page on the preamble. The words “referring to various alternatives to tin-based antifouling paints” look like an invitation to write the standard CDP / SPC / hybrid / foul-release essay. They are not. That taxonomy is worth about two sentences and a four-row table here; everything else belongs to the three limbs.

Limb (a) is answered as a comparison across the docking interval, not as a list of coatings, and the physical-against-biological distinction is put before the comparison because the comparison makes no sense without it. Limb (b) has to start with the mechanism being enhanced — a candidate who has not said what a foul release coating does cannot explain what oil adds to it — and then closes on the limits, which is where the second half of the marks are. Limb (c) is organised by the hierarchy of control, which turns a shapeless list of precautions into an argument with an order.

The preamble does not govern limb (c)

The printed preamble governs limbs (a) and (b) fully. Limb (c) is different: personnel and health safety during application is about occupational exposure to the whole coating system — the primer, the tie coat, the solvents, and above all the removal of the old scheme, which is frequently the most toxic operation of the job and has nothing to do with which tin alternative is going on afterwards.

So the preamble is treated as governing (a) and (b) fully and (c) contextually. Saying that in one line shows the stem was read rather than skimmed.

The one relationship that makes limb (b) answerable

Release force scales with the square root of (modulus × surface energy).

That single statement is the whole of foul release technology and it explains everything the limb asks:

  • It is why a foul release coating must be both low-energy and soft. A hard low-energy coating — glass, for instance — fouls badly.
  • It is why release needs speed: the shear has to exceed the release force, so there is a threshold below which fouling stays.
  • It is why oil helps: a mobile liquid layer at the surface drives the effective surface energy down further, so the release force falls and the threshold speed falls with it.
  • And it is why oil does not fix everything: it does nothing to the coating's mechanical toughness, and once it has leached out, the relationship returns to where it started.

Roughness: two kinds, and a trap

Physical roughnessBiological roughness
What it isPaint texture, application defects, leached layer, damage, weld seams, plate undulationSlime, weed, shell
Reversible?No - it stays until the surface is treated, and over-coating compounds itYes - it cleans off
Cost per micrometreLowerMuch higher - compliant, irregular and thick

The trap: assuming the smoothest coating always wins. A foul release film is the smoothest thing available at application, but on a ship that idles it fouls, and a fouled smooth coating is worse than a clean rougher one. Roughness is a property of the ship's whole operating pattern, not of the paint tin.

Common mistakes

  • Writing the standard AFS Convention essay and running out of page before limb (b).
  • Comparing coatings only at application, when the whole question in limb (a) is what happens over three to five years.
  • Forgetting the leached layer, which is the reason CDP roughens and SPC does not.
  • Saying a foul release coating kills fouling. It does not; it is non-biocidal and works entirely on adhesion.
  • Explaining oil incorporation without first explaining the mechanism it enhances.
  • Giving the benefits of oil incorporation and stopping — the limits are half the marks: finite reservoir, decay across the interval, repair and recoating difficulty, and the open regulatory question.
  • Ignoring that a foul release surface cannot be over-coated without full removal or an approved tie coat, which is a real cost.
  • In limb (c), listing personal protective equipment first. PPE is the last line of the hierarchy, not the first.
  • Omitting the removal of the old coating, which is where the organotin and cybutryne exposure actually occurs.
  • Omitting high-pressure injection injury — the most serious acute hazard of airless spraying and the one nobody expects.
  • Treating spent abrasive and paint residue as ordinary waste. It is hazardous waste and it is landed ashore.

Examiner traps

  • “When did the AFS Convention enter into force?” 17 September 2008 — with the organotin application ban from 1 January 2003 and the presence ban from 1 January 2008.
  • “What changed on 1 January 2023?” The cybutryne control under MEPC.331(76) applied — ten months before this sitting.
  • “Your ship has cybutryne in her existing coating. What must happen?” It must be removed or over-coated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system, and in any event no later than sixty months after its last application.
  • “Which certificate, and from what size?” International Anti-fouling System Certificate for 400 GT and above on international voyages; a Declaration for ships of 24 metres or more below that.
  • “Why does a CDP get rougher?” The soluble matrix dissolves unevenly and leaves a thick, porous leached layer — and successive over-coating compounds it.
  • “Why does an SPC not?” The binder hydrolyses and is sheared away, so the film polishes itself and the leached layer stays thin.
  • “Does a foul release coating contain biocide?” No. It works on adhesion, not toxicity.
  • “Why does foul release need speed?” Because release is by hydrodynamic shear, and below a threshold speed the shear is insufficient.
  • “What does the oil do?” Migrates to the surface as a mobile layer, lowers the effective surface energy, disrupts adhesive curing, and lowers the release threshold.
  • “What is the drawback of the oil?” It is a finite reservoir; as it leaches out the benefit decays, and it makes repair and over-coating harder.
  • “Which protection for spray application?” Air-fed positive-pressure hood or full-face breathing apparatus. A cartridge respirator is not sufficient.
  • “A rating has a pinprick on his hand from the spray gun and says he is fine.” Hospital immediately. High-pressure injection injury is a surgical emergency and the wound always looks trivial.
  • “Where does the spent grit go?” Contained, collected and landed ashore to a licensed receiver. It is hazardous waste.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does AFS stand for? The Control of Harmful Anti-fouling Systems on Ships.
  • Name the four families of tin-free system. Controlled depletion polymer, self-polishing copolymer, tin-free hybrid, and foul release.
  • How is hull roughness measured? A stylus gauge traversed over the hull, averaging the maximum peak-to-trough height over successive short intervals, reported in micrometres.
  • Which costs more, physical or biological roughness? Biological, for the same nominal height.
  • Does over-coating reduce roughness? No - it compounds it. Only removal to bare steel resets it.
  • What are the two properties a foul release coating needs? Low surface energy and low elastic modulus.
  • Why does hard glass foul badly? Low surface energy but very high modulus - both terms have to be low.
  • Can you paint over a foul release coating? Not with a conventional scheme - nothing adheres to it. Full removal or an approved tie coat.
  • Which is the most dangerous coating operation? Usually the removal of the old scheme, because of the metal and organotin residues in the dust.
  • Where does PPE sit in the hierarchy of control? Last.

Memory framework

For the families, four words in order of how the film behaves: DISSOLVES, POLISHES, BLENDS, RELEASES.

For limb (a), one question asked four times: WHAT HAPPENS TO THE SURFACE OVER THREE YEARS? CDP roughens; SPC smooths; hybrid sits between; foul release is smoothest but only if the ship moves.

For limb (b), one relationship and one consequence: SOFT AND SLIPPERY — release force follows the square root of modulus times surface energy — and oil makes the surface slipperier still, but not for ever.

For limb (c), the hierarchy in five words: ELIMINATE, ENGINEER, ADMINISTER, PROTECT, DISPOSE. If you can only remember two things: air-fed hood for spraying, and injection injury goes to hospital.

Regulation and source map

  • AFS Convention 2001 — the International Convention on the Control of Harmful Anti-fouling Systems on Ships. In force 17 September 2008; organotin application ban from 1 January 2003, presence ban from 1 January 2008. International Anti-fouling System Certificate for 400 GT and above on international voyages; Declaration for ships of 24 metres or more below that.
  • MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021 — added cybutryne. Applied from 1 January 2023, ten months before this sitting. Existing systems removed or over-coated at the next scheduled anti-fouling renewal after that date, and no later than sixty months after last application.
  • MLC 2006 Regulation 4.3 — health and safety protection and accident prevention. The 2022 amendments enter force 23 December 2024 and are future here.
  • ISM Code as amended through MSC.353(92) — sections 7 (shipboard operations) and 8 (emergency preparedness), under which the procedures and rescue arrangements live in the safety management system.
  • SOLAS — enclosed-space entry and rescue drills.
  • No paint manufacturer, product or formulation is named anywhere in this answer, and every numerical figure given is expressly an order of magnitude. See the uncertainty section.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The AFS Convention is not held by the True Source corpus. The corpus's IMO instrument directory contains no anti-fouling folder, and its reference resolver holds 320 entries of which every one is MARPOL Annex VI. The Convention, its entry into force, the organotin controls, the certificate and the declaration are therefore carried at authoritative-secondary level, and no annex or regulation is quoted or attributed a paragraph. MEPC.331(76) is likewise not held: its identity, adoption date and 1 January 2023 application date are stated, and the compliance arithmetic that follows is stated as the rule it is rather than quoted.

The coatings science is industry and engineering knowledge, and the numbers are deliberately thin. The four-family taxonomy and the depletion, polishing and release mechanisms are uncontroversial and consistently recorded. Every figure in the answer is expressly an order of magnitude. No service life, polishing rate, leached-layer thickness, surface energy value, elastic modulus, threshold release speed or roughness-to-power coefficient is presented as sourced, because none is held and all of them differ between products. No paint manufacturer, product, brand or formulation is named anywhere.

The release relationship is used qualitatively. That the release force scales with the square root of the product of modulus and surface energy is the established relationship, and it is used here to explain why both must be low. It is not used to compute anything and no coefficient is given.

One question is left open on purpose. Whether an oil-amended foul release coating falls to be treated as biocidal is a live regulatory question, and this answer takes no position because no held instrument or determination resolves it. Recording it as a question is more honest than resolving it by assertion.

Limb (c) carries two further limits. No exposure limit, threshold value or biological monitoring criterion is quoted for any solvent, isocyanate or biocide — those are national occupational hygiene limits, they differ between jurisdictions, and none is held. And the Organization's recommendations for entering enclosed spaces aboard ships are referred to descriptively rather than by resolution number, because the current instrument identity could not be established from a held source.

Currency. The cybutryne control is the live date in this question and it must stay framed as at this sitting: it applied from 1 January 2023, so at November 2023 it is ten months old and the sixty-month compliance arithmetic is still running for most ships. An answer written from a later sitting would treat the transition as historical, which it is not here.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The setting, in two sentences
  2. __________What hull roughness is, and why it costs money
  3. __________The four families compared
  4. __________First, the mechanism being enhanced
  5. __________What incorporated oil does
  6. __________The limits - which is where the marks are
  7. __________The hazards
  8. __________The controls, in the order they must be applied
Flashcards 10

Controlled depletion polymer - a soluble rosin matrix that dissolves to release biocide, leaving a leached layer. Self-polishing copolymer - an acrylate binder that hydrolyses at the surface and is sheared away, so the film polishes and renews itself. Tin-free hybrid - a blend of the two chemistries. Foul release - non-biocidal silicone or fluoropolymer with low surface energy and low modulus, from which fouling is released by the water flow.

Dissolves, polishes, blends, releases - the four verbs carry the whole taxonomy.

The cybutryne control introduced by MEPC.331(76) applied. A cybutryne-containing system may no longer be applied or re-applied, and an existing one must be removed or over-coated with a sealer coat at the next scheduled renewal of the anti-fouling system after that date, and in any event no later than sixty months after its last application.

At a November 2023 sitting the control is ten months old and the transition is still running - a later paper would treat it as history.

Physical roughness is the paint film's own texture, application defects, the porous leached layer, damage, weld seams and plate undulation - permanent until the surface is treated, and compounded by over-coating. Biological roughness is slime, weed and shell - removable, but for the same nominal height it costs substantially more because it is compliant, irregular and thick.

The comparison in limb (a) makes no sense until the two are separated.

The CDP's soluble rosin matrix dissolves unevenly and leaves a thick, porous leached layer that is itself rough, and CDP schemes are usually over-coated rather than removed so the roughness compounds from docking to docking. The SPC's binder hydrolyses at the surface and is then sheared away, so the film is polished by service and the leached layer stays very thin and constant.

The leached layer is the mechanism, and an answer without it is describing rather than explaining.

No. A foul release film is the smoothest at application, but it needs the ship to exceed a threshold speed for the flow to strip fouling off. On a slow or idle ship it fouls, and a smooth fouled surface is worse than a clean rougher one. Roughness is a property of the ship's operating pattern as much as of the paint.

It is the judgement the limb is really testing, and it prevents a one-dimensional answer.

It kills nothing - it works on adhesion. It needs low surface energy, so biological adhesives cannot spread and key into the surface, and low elastic modulus, so the film deforms and peels away from the attachment. The force to release an adhered organism scales with the square root of the product of modulus and surface energy, which is why both must be low; release in service is by hydrodynamic shear above a threshold speed.

This one relationship makes the whole of limb (b) answerable.

Being blended in but not chemically bound, the oil migrates to the surface and forms a thin, continuously replenished mobile liquid layer. That lowers the effective surface energy, disrupts the curing and anchoring of biological adhesives at the moment of settlement, lowers the shear stress and therefore the ship speed needed for release, improves resistance to settlement while the ship is idle, and partially self-heals minor surface damage.

Every benefit follows from the single fact that the oil is unbound and mobile.

The oil is a finite reservoir: it leaches out and the enhancement decays across the docking interval, faster where shear and temperature are highest, so the coating ends its life closer to an unmodified film. Unbound oil also makes repair and over-coating harder, because it is exactly what stops anything sticking. And whether an oil-amended system falls to be treated as biocidal is an open regulatory question. It does nothing for the coating's mechanical fragility.

Half the marks in limb (b) are in the limits, and most answers stop at the benefits.

Eliminate or substitute - low-solvent, high-solids or water-borne schemes, or a non-biocidal system. Engineering - ventilation and extraction, wet or vacuum blasting, dock containment and residue collection, explosion-protected equipment. Administrative - safety data sheets read at planning, risk assessment and method statement, permit to work with enclosed-space and hot work permits, atmosphere testing, standby and rescue plan, exclusion zones, exposure limits, training and health surveillance. PPE last - air-fed hood for spraying, chemical-resistant clothing and gloves. Then emergency arrangements and hazardous waste disposal ashore.

PPE first is the classic wrong answer; the hierarchy is what turns a list into an argument.

Removing the OLD coating. Blasting dust carries heavy metals and, on older hulls, organotin residues, and now cybutryne which has to come off - and it is easy to plan the job around the new paint and forget the old. The acute hazard of the spraying itself is high-pressure injection injury from an airless gun: it looks like a pinprick and is a surgical emergency requiring immediate hospital treatment.

Both are routinely omitted, and both are the things a Chief Engineer would actually be held responsible for.

Quick revision
15-second recall
AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008 — organotin application ban 1 January 2003, presence ban 1 January 2008; IAFS Certificate at 400 GT, Declaration at 24 m. MEPC.331(76) added cybutryne, applied 1 January 2023 — ten months before this sitting, transition still running: removal or sealer coat at the next anti-fouling renewal, and no later than sixty months after last application. Four families: CDP dissolves, SPC polishes, hybrid blends, foul release releases. Roughness is average hull roughness in micrometres from a stylus gauge; it thickens the boundary layer and raises frictional resistance; over-coating compounds it, only removal to bare steel resets it. Physical roughness is permanent, biological is removable but costs far more per micrometre. Through the interval: CDP roughens fastest (thick porous leached layer), SPC stays smooth or improves (polished by service, thin leached layer), hybrid between, foul release smoothest but only if the ship moves — and nothing adheres to it, so recoating means full removal or a tie coat. Foul release kills nothing: release force scales with the square root of modulus × surface energy, so both must be low, and release is by hydrodynamic shear above a threshold speed. Oil incorporation — unbound silicone oil migrates to the surface as a mobile layer, lowers effective surface energy, disrupts adhesive curing, lowers the threshold speed, improves static performance and partially self-heals. Limits: finite reservoir, benefit decays, uneven depletion, repair and over-coating harder, biocidal status an open question. Safety: hierarchy — eliminate, engineer, administer, protect, dispose; the most toxic operation is usually removing the old coating; spraying needs an air-fed hood, not a cartridge; injection injury goes to hospital; spent grit is hazardous waste landed ashore.

Critical numbers
  • AFS Convention in force 17 September 2008
  • Organotin application ban from 1 January 2003; presence ban from 1 January 2008
  • MEPC.331(76) adopted 17 June 2021; cybutryne control applied from 1 January 2023
  • Existing cybutryne systems: removed or over-coated at the next scheduled anti-fouling renewal, and no later than SIXTY MONTHS after last application
  • IAFS Certificate for ships of 400 gross tonnage and above on international voyages; Declaration for ships of 24 metres or more below that
  • Average hull roughness is reported in micrometres; a newbuilding is of the order of 100 micrometres - an order of magnitude only
  • Release force scales with the SQUARE ROOT of (elastic modulus x surface energy)
Critical regulation
The AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008, is the instrument behind every coating in this question: it banned the application of organotin biocides from 1 January 2003 and their presence on ship surfaces from 1 January 2008. The live control at this sitting is MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, which added cybutryne and applied from 1 January 2023 - ten months before this examination, with existing systems running to the next scheduled anti-fouling renewal and in any event to no later than sixty months after last application.
Major trap
Answering the preamble instead of the limbs. The opening words invite the standard AFS Convention and CDP/SPC/hybrid/foul-release essay, and none of the three printed limbs asks for it — the taxonomy is worth two sentences and a table, and everything else belongs to roughness, oil incorporation and safety. The technical paired traps are assuming the smoothest coating is always best (a fouled foul release surface on an idle ship is worse than a clean self-polishing one, and biological roughness costs far more per micrometre than physical), and explaining oil incorporation without first stating the mechanism it enhances — release force follows the square root of modulus times surface energy, so both must be low, and the oil's whole function is to lower the threshold speed at which shear can strip the fouling off.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q4 - the OPRC Convention, the other pollution instrument on this paper and a clean contrast in that it regulates preparedness rather than a substance · QP2311 Q2 - mean piston speed and fuel consumption, which is where the hull roughness penalty discussed here is actually paid

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Underneath the four terms there is one loop, and seeing it is the whole question. Somebody has to decide how often each machine is opened, tested or looked at. That decision generates work. The work only counts if somebody writes down what they found — not that they did it, but what condition the machine was actually in and what they measured. Those written findings are what reveal that something is not as it should be. And when something is not as it should be, the response has to go further than replacing the broken part: it has to find out why it broke and stop that happening again — which very often means changing how often the job is done, and the loop closes. The systematic approach is just the name for keeping that loop closed rather than letting it run in one direction. The distinction that carries the most marks is between three different things people all call 'fixing it': putting the machine back into service, removing the reason it failed, and making sure the same reason cannot bite on the sister ship. Only the first of those is what most people actually do. And the second idea worth holding is that the equipment most likely to fail when it is finally needed is the equipment that never runs — emergency generators, standby pumps, emergency steering — which is why the rules insist those are deliberately tested on a schedule rather than left to look after themselves.

Knowledge map
PMS and the ISM Code - Corrective Action, Records, System and Intervals
  1. 1The structure behind the four printed terms
    • the four terms line up almost exactly with what ISM Code section 10 requires the Company to ensure
    • CORRECTIVE ACTION PROCESS - that appropriate corrective action is taken, read with section 9 on reporting, investigation and analysis and measures to prevent recurrence
    • MAINTENANCE RECORDS - that records of these activities are maintained, read with section 11 on documentation and document control
  2. 2The three things that are not the same
    • CORRECTION fixes the symptom and restores the function - renew the failed bearing
    • CORRECTIVE ACTION removes the cause so it does not happen again on this ship - correct the lubricating oil supply pressure and the alarm setting that let it fail
    • PREVENTIVE ACTION stops it occurring elsewhere - fleet circular, revised PMS interval, amended procedure on sister ships
  3. 3The process, end to end
    • DETECTION AND REPORTING - non-conformities, defects, near misses, accidents and hazardous occurrences reported to the Company with the possible cause where known
    • this works only under a NO-BLAME REPORTING CULTURE; a system that punishes reports stops receiving them and goes blind
    • IMMEDIATE CONTAINMENT - make safe, isolate, alternative arrangement, inform the master and the Designated Person Ashore, notify class or flag where statutory or class-credited
  4. 4What a record must contain, and what makes it good
    • equipment identity and tag, the job specification performed, the date and the RUNNING HOURS AT EXECUTION
    • AS-FOUND AND AS-LEFT CONDITION - the single most valuable field and the one most often left blank
    • MEASURED VALUES AGAINST LIMITS - clearances, wear-down, insulation resistance, pressures, alignment - NOT A TICK
  5. 5How a mediocre record system is actually improved
    • STANDARDISE THE JOB CARD so every occurrence asks the same fields, and make measurement fields MANDATORY - a system that accepts 'OK' will be given 'OK'
    • DEMAND AS-FOUND CONDITION, not only as-left; without it there is no basis for changing an interval
    • RECORD AT THE MACHINE AND AT THE TIME, not from memory at the end of the watch
  6. 6The closed loop
    • 'systematic' means maintenance generated by a METHOD rather than by whoever notices something
    • REGISTER EVERY ITEM - a complete equipment register with a tagging hierarchy so nothing exists outside the system
    • ASSESS CRITICALITY - identify equipment and technical systems whose SUDDEN OPERATIONAL FAILURE MAY RESULT IN A HAZARDOUS SITUATION; the step most often skipped or copied unread from a sister ship
  7. 7Where an interval comes from
    • STATUTORY - SOLAS and MARPOL inspection, testing and drill requirements; the Company CANNOT change them
    • CLASSIFICATION - survey items and the terms of an approved maintenance scheme; changeable only with the society's agreement, and the credit is at risk if not
    • MAKER'S RECOMMENDATION - changeable with justification, but warranty and technical support may follow the recommendation
  8. 8Optimising an interval, and changing one safely
    • TOO SHORT - unnecessary opening-up, wasted spares and manhours, and DEFECTS INTRODUCED BY REASSEMBLY; dismantling a healthy machine is a real source of failure
    • TOO LONG - wear beyond limits, consequential damage, unplanned breakdown, and on a critical item a safety or pollution consequence
    • OPTIMISATION RUNS ON EVIDENCE - as-found condition records, defect and failure history, condition-monitoring trends, maker guidance and criticality
  9. 9Periodicity of inspections
    • CONTINUOUS AND DAILY - watchkeeping rounds, alarm and parameter monitoring
    • WEEKLY AND MONTHLY - statutory life-saving and fire-fighting appliance inspections and tests, emergency equipment tests, and safety committee inspections
    • BEFORE DEPARTURE - steering gear testing and the associated checks

Limb (c): REGISTER, RATE, CHOOSE, SPECIFY, PLAN, DO, REVIEW (6). Limb (d) sources: STATUTE, CLASS, MAKER, COMPANY (7); bases: CALENDAR, HOURS, USE, CONDITION (7).

Remember9 route headings
Cover76 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 76 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The structure behind the four printed terms
  • the four terms line up almost exactly with what ISM Code section 10 requires the Company to ensure
  • CORRECTIVE ACTION PROCESS - that appropriate corrective action is taken, read with section 9 on reporting, investigation and analysis and measures to prevent recurrence
  • MAINTENANCE RECORDS - that records of these activities are maintained, read with section 11 on documentation and document control
  • SYSTEMATIC APPROACH - the requirement for PROCEDURES to maintain the ship in conformity with the rules and the Company's own additional requirements, the critical equipment obligation, and integration into the operational maintenance routine
  • MAINTENANCE INTERVALS AND PERIODICITY - that inspections are held at appropriate intervals
  • ONE BOUNDARY BEFORE STARTING - the PMS is the Company's regime under the ISM Code and says when the ship OVERHAULS the item; the class survey regime says when class must SEE it
  • they meet where class APPROVES the PMS, so maintenance can be credited against continuous survey items subject to periodical audit - one sentence, because the survey side is Q1 on this paper
2. The three things that are not the same
  • CORRECTION fixes the symptom and restores the function - renew the failed bearing
  • CORRECTIVE ACTION removes the cause so it does not happen again on this ship - correct the lubricating oil supply pressure and the alarm setting that let it fail
  • PREVENTIVE ACTION stops it occurring elsewhere - fleet circular, revised PMS interval, amended procedure on sister ships
  • the Code makes it explicit: corrective action procedures must include MEASURES INTENDED TO PREVENT RECURRENCE
  • a replaced bearing with no other change is a CORRECTION, and closing a non-conformity on it is the commonest audit finding in this area
3. The process, end to end
  • DETECTION AND REPORTING - non-conformities, defects, near misses, accidents and hazardous occurrences reported to the Company with the possible cause where known

  • IMMEDIATE CONTAINMENT - make safe, isolate, alternative arrangement, inform the master and the Designated Person Ashore, notify class or flag where statutory or class-credited
  • INVESTIGATION AND ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS - the Code requires the occurrence to be investigated and analysed, not merely logged; repeated questioning, cause-and-effect analysis or fault tree, run PAST the equipment to the system
  • DECIDE CORRECTIVE AND PREVENTIVE ACTION, distinguishing the three acts
  • ASSIGN IT - a named owner, the resources and a target date; unassigned actions do not happen
  • IMPLEMENT AND RECORD - which is where limb b) attaches
  • VERIFY CLOSE-OUT AND EFFECTIVENESS - two separate checks: was it done, and did it work? An action that was done and did not work is REOPENED
  • DISSEMINATE - lessons learned to sister ships, amend the SMS procedure, the job specification or the PMS INTERVAL, which is where corrective action feeds limb d)
  • TREND AND REVIEW - repeat defects analysed across the fleet, the loop verified by internal safety audit and by management review
4. What a record must contain, and what makes it good
  • equipment identity and tag, the job specification performed, the date and the RUNNING HOURS AT EXECUTION
  • AS-FOUND AND AS-LEFT CONDITION - the single most valuable field and the one most often left blank
  • MEASURED VALUES AGAINST LIMITS - clearances, wear-down, insulation resistance, pressures, alignment - NOT A TICK
  • spares used by part number and quantity, so consumption reconciles with the inventory
  • who did it and how long it took; test results and photographs; any defect found and its disposition
  • for deferred jobs, the deferral record with its risk assessment and approval
  • supporting certificates - servicing, calibration, lifting gear, and class credit records
  • QUALITY ATTRIBUTES - complete, contemporaneous, attributable, accurate, legible, retrievable, retained and CONTROLLED
  • control is section 11 - documents in force at the right place, obsolete versions removed, the electronic system backed up
5. How a mediocre record system is actually improved
  • STANDARDISE THE JOB CARD so every occurrence asks the same fields, and make measurement fields MANDATORY - a system that accepts 'OK' will be given 'OK'
  • DEMAND AS-FOUND CONDITION, not only as-left; without it there is no basis for changing an interval
  • RECORD AT THE MACHINE AND AT THE TIME, not from memory at the end of the watch
  • LINK SPARES CONSUMPTION TO THE JOB so inventory, history and ordering reconcile

  • REVIEW OVERDUE AND DEFERRED JOBS at a fixed monthly point, with reason and approval visible
  • AUDIT THE RECORDS INTERNALLY BEFORE ANYONE ELSE DOES, and check that what is recorded matches the condition of the machine
