  • HAND RECORDS OVER FORMALLY AT EVERY CREW CHANGE so continuity survives the relief
  • FEED THE ANALYSIS BACK INTO THE PMS - which is the point of the whole exercise
  • WHY IT MATTERS BEYOND THE AUDIT - class credit under an approved scheme, port State control, vetting, warranty and insurance claims, on-hire and off-hire and sale disputes, root cause analysis and interval optimisation
6. The closed loop
  • 'systematic' means maintenance generated by a METHOD rather than by whoever notices something
  • REGISTER EVERY ITEM - a complete equipment register with a tagging hierarchy so nothing exists outside the system
  • ASSESS CRITICALITY - identify equipment and technical systems whose SUDDEN OPERATIONAL FAILURE MAY RESULT IN A HAZARDOUS SITUATION; the step most often skipped or copied unread from a sister ship
  • CHOOSE A STRATEGY PER ITEM matched to criticality - preventive on calendar or running hours, condition-based, run to failure for non-critical items, and statutory or class-fixed where the interval is set outside the Company
  • WRITE THE JOB SPECIFICATION - what is done, by whom, with what tools, spares, permits and precautions, and what is measured and recorded
  • PLAN AND SCHEDULE against reality - manning, port calls, weather, cargo operations, survey and docking windows; a plan that ignores the trading pattern generates deferrals
  • EXECUTE AND RECORD under the permit-to-work and risk assessment regime
  • REVIEW AND IMPROVE - feed defects, condition data and audit findings back into criticality, strategy and interval; THAT FEEDBACK IS WHAT MAKES IT A SYSTEM RATHER THAN A SCHEDULE
  • CRITICAL EQUIPMENT attracts specific measures to promote reliability, expressly including REGULAR TESTING OF STANDBY ARRANGEMENTS AND EQUIPMENT NOT IN CONTINUOUS USE - emergency generator, emergency fire pump, standby pumps, emergency steering, lifeboat engines
  • EQUIPMENT THAT IS NEVER USED IS EXACTLY THE EQUIPMENT THAT FAILS WHEN IT IS NEEDED
  • and those tests must be INTEGRATED INTO THE SHIP'S OPERATIONAL MAINTENANCE ROUTINE - in the PMS, not on a separate list
7. Where an interval comes from
  • STATUTORY - SOLAS and MARPOL inspection, testing and drill requirements; the Company CANNOT change them
  • CLASSIFICATION - survey items and the terms of an approved maintenance scheme; changeable only with the society's agreement, and the credit is at risk if not
  • MAKER'S RECOMMENDATION - changeable with justification, but warranty and technical support may follow the recommendation
  • COMPANY STANDARD - changeable, and this is where fleet experience and analysis are applied
  • CONDITION - the interval is set by the MACHINE, and the Company sets the monitoring regime instead
  • THE FOUR BASES on which an interval is expressed - CALENDAR time, RUNNING HOURS, USE or cycles, and CONDITION
  • choosing the wrong basis is a real error - a standby pump maintained on running hours may never come due at all, which is why the standby-testing obligation exists
8. Optimising an interval, and changing one safely
  • TOO SHORT - unnecessary opening-up, wasted spares and manhours, and DEFECTS INTRODUCED BY REASSEMBLY; dismantling a healthy machine is a real source of failure
  • TOO LONG - wear beyond limits, consequential damage, unplanned breakdown, and on a critical item a safety or pollution consequence
  • OPTIMISATION RUNS ON EVIDENCE - as-found condition records, defect and failure history, condition-monitoring trends, maker guidance and criticality
  • EXTENDING AN INTERVAL IS A CONTROLLED CHANGE - justified from data, risk assessed, approved by the Company's technical management, agreed with class or the maker where credited or under warranty, and recorded
  • shortening one is not free either, and the same evidence should support it
  • DEFERRAL IS NOT INTERVAL CHANGE - it needs a procedure: risk assessment, compensating measures, approval ashore, class or flag agreement if statutory or credited, a new date and a record
  • SILENT DEFERRAL IS THE FAILURE MODE, and a monthly review of overdue items is what catches it
9. Periodicity of inspections
  • CONTINUOUS AND DAILY - watchkeeping rounds, alarm and parameter monitoring
  • WEEKLY AND MONTHLY - statutory life-saving and fire-fighting appliance inspections and tests, emergency equipment tests, and safety committee inspections
  • BEFORE DEPARTURE - steering gear testing and the associated checks
  • PERIODIC SHIPBOARD INSPECTIONS - the master's and senior officers' inspections of accommodation, catering and working spaces, a seafarer welfare requirement as well as a maintenance one
  • ANNUAL AND MULTI-ANNUAL - servicing and thorough examination of life-saving appliances, launching appliances and release gear under the dedicated maintenance-requirements instrument in force since 1 January 2020, and the class and statutory survey cycle
  • INTERNAL SAFETY AUDITS on board and ashore AT INTERVALS NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS - the Code's own number
  • MANAGEMENT REVIEW - the periodical evaluation of the efficiency of the system, where the trends from limb b) are supposed to arrive
  • THE CLOSING POINT - the four terms are one loop: intervals generate work, work generates records, records reveal non-conformities, non-conformities drive corrective action, and corrective action changes the intervals
  • a ship where the loop is open, where records are written but never read, has a planned maintenance system on paper and a reactive one in fact
Model written answer

1. The structure behind the four printed terms

The four terms are not a random list. Read against ISM Code section 10, they line up almost exactly with what that section requires the Company to ensure:

Printed termWhere it comes from in section 10
(a) Corrective action processThat appropriate corrective action is taken — read with section 9, which requires reporting, investigation and analysis, and corrective action including measures to prevent recurrence
(b) Developing and improving maintenance recordsThat records of these activities are maintained — read with section 11 on documentation and document control
(c) Systematic approach of maintenanceThe opening requirement for procedures to maintain the ship in conformity with the rules and with the Company's own additional requirements; the critical equipment obligation; and the requirement that inspections and measures be integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine
(d) Maintenance intervals and periodicity of inspectionsThat inspections are held at appropriate intervals

One boundary before starting. The planned maintenance system is the Company's regime under the ISM Code and answers when the ship overhauls the item. The classification survey regime answers when class must see it. They meet at one point — where class approves the PMS, maintenance done under it can be credited against continuous survey items, subject to periodical audit. That link is worth one sentence here and no more; the survey side is Q1 on this paper.

(a) Corrective action process

2. The three things that are not the same

Most weak answers use one word for three different acts. Section 9 requires all three and only names two:

What it doesExample
CorrectionFixes the symptom and restores the functionRenew the failed bearing
Corrective actionRemoves the cause so it does not happen again on this shipCorrect the lubricating oil supply pressure and the alarm setting that let it fail
Preventive actionStops it occurring elsewhere — sister ships, similar machineryFleet circular, revised PMS interval, amended procedure

The Code makes this explicit: the procedures for corrective action must include measures intended to prevent recurrence. A replaced bearing with no change to anything else is a correction, and closing a non-conformity on it is the commonest audit finding in this area.

3. The process, end to end

  1. Detection and reporting. Non-conformities, defects, near misses, accidents and hazardous occurrences reported to the Company — with the possible cause where it is known. This only works under a no-blame reporting culture; a system that punishes reports stops receiving them and goes blind.
  2. Immediate containment. Make safe, isolate, put an alternative arrangement in place, inform the master and the Designated Person Ashore, and notify class or flag where the item is statutory or class-credited.
  3. Investigation and root cause analysis. Section 9 requires the occurrence to be investigated and analysed, not merely logged. Repeated questioning, cause-and-effect analysis or fault tree, run past the equipment to the system — the procedure, the training, the spare quality, the interval.
  4. Decide corrective and preventive action, distinguishing the three acts above.
  5. Assign it. A named owner, the resources, and a target date. Unassigned actions do not happen.
  6. Implement and record — which is where limb (b) attaches.
  7. Verify close-out AND effectiveness. Two separate checks: was it done, and did it work? An action that was done and did not work is reopened, not closed.
  8. Disseminate. Lessons learned to sister ships; amend the SMS procedure, the job specification or the PMS interval where the analysis points there. This is where corrective action feeds limb (d).
  9. Trend and review. Repeat defects analysed across the fleet; the whole loop verified by internal safety audit and by management review, and the efficiency of the system periodically evaluated.

(b) Developing and improving maintenance records

4. What a record must contain, and what makes it good

Content — a maintenance record that will survive an audit carries:

  • the equipment identity and its tag; the job specification performed; the date and the running hours at execution;
  • as-found condition and as-left condition — the single most valuable field, and the one most often left blank;
  • measured values against limits — clearances, wear-down, insulation resistance, pressures, alignment — not a tick;
  • spares used, by part number, and the quantity, so consumption reconciles with the inventory;
  • who did it and how long it took; test results and photographs; and any defect found and its disposition;
  • for deferred jobs, the deferral record with its risk assessment and approval;
  • and the supporting certificates — servicing, calibration, lifting gear, and class credit records.

Quality — the attributes that make it evidence rather than paperwork: complete, contemporaneous, attributable, accurate, legible, retrievable, retained and controlled. The last is section 11: documents in force at the right place, obsolete versions removed, and the electronic system backed up.

5. How a mediocre record system is actually improved

  1. Standardise the job card so that every occurrence of the same job asks for the same fields, and make the measurement fields mandatory — a system that accepts “OK” will be given “OK”.
  2. Demand as-found condition, not only as-left. Without it there is no basis for changing an interval, and limb (d) becomes guesswork.
  3. Record at the machine and at the time, not from memory at the end of the watch.
  4. Link spares consumption to the job, so the inventory, the maintenance history and the ordering all reconcile.
  5. Trend the data. A record system that is only ever written to is a filing cabinet; one that is read produces interval changes, repeat-defect analysis and early warning.
  6. Review overdue and deferred jobs at a fixed point every month, with the reason and the approval visible.
  7. Audit the records internally before anyone else does — sample them, and check that what is recorded matches the condition of the machine.
  8. Use a computerised system properly: mandatory fields, offline capability, synchronisation with shore, backup, and access control. Digitisation without discipline simply produces bad records faster.
  9. Hand records over formally at every crew change, so continuity survives the relief.
  10. Feed the analysis back into the PMS — which is the point of the whole exercise.

Why the quality matters, beyond the audit: records are the evidence for class credit under an approved scheme; for port State control and vetting inspections; for warranty, insurance and damage claims; for on-hire, off-hire and sale disputes; for root cause analysis; and for interval optimisation. A ship with poor records does not merely fail an audit — it loses arguments it would otherwise win.

(c) Systematic approach of maintenance

6. The closed loop

“Systematic” means the maintenance is generated by a method rather than by whoever notices something. Seven steps, and the last one closes back onto the first:

  1. Register every item. A complete equipment register with a tagging hierarchy — system, sub-system, item — so nothing exists outside the system and every record has somewhere to attach.
  2. Assess criticality. Identify the equipment and technical systems whose sudden operational failure may result in a hazardous situation. This is an express requirement, and it is the step most often skipped or copied unread from a sister ship.
  3. Choose a strategy per item, matched to that criticality — not one policy for the whole engine room:
StrategyBasisWhere it fits
Preventive, calendar or running-hourTime or hours elapsedThe bulk of machinery; simple and auditable
Condition-basedMeasured condition — vibration, oil analysis, thermography, performanceHigh-value or hard-to-open machinery, where opening up is itself a risk
Run to failure (corrective)Deliberate acceptanceNon-critical items with no safety or pollution consequence and a spare available
Statutory or class-fixedThe rule, not the engineerAnything where the interval is set outside the Company. Not negotiable
  1. Write the job specification — what is done, by whom, with what tools, spares, permits and safety precautions, and what is to be measured and recorded.
  2. Plan and schedule against reality: manning, port calls, weather, cargo operations, and the survey and docking windows. A plan that ignores the trading pattern generates deferrals.
  3. Execute and record, under the permit-to-work and risk assessment regime.
  4. Review and improve — feed defects, condition data and audit findings back into criticality, strategy and interval. That feedback is what makes it a system rather than a schedule.

Two obligations inside the loop that are routinely omitted. First, critical equipment attracts specific measures to promote reliability — and expressly the regular testing of stand-by arrangements and of equipment or technical systems not in continuous use: the emergency generator, the emergency fire pump, standby pumps, emergency steering, lifeboat engines. Equipment that is never used is exactly the equipment that fails when it is. Second, those tests and inspections must be integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine — they belong in the PMS, not on a separate list somebody remembers.

(d) Maintenance intervals and periodicity of inspections

7. Where an interval comes from

SourceCan the Company change it?
Statutory — SOLAS and MARPOL inspection, testing and drill requirementsNo. Fixed by the instrument
Classification — survey items and the terms of an approved maintenance schemeOnly with the society's agreement, and the credit is at risk if not
Maker's recommendationYes, with justification — but warranty and technical support may follow the recommendation
Company standardYes — this is where fleet experience and analysis are applied
ConditionThe interval is set by the machine; the Company sets the monitoring regime instead

The four bases on which an interval is expressed: calendar time; running hours; use or cycles; and condition. Choosing the wrong basis is a real error — a standby pump maintained on running hours may never come due at all, which is precisely why the standby-testing obligation exists.

8. Optimising an interval, and changing one safely

Both directions cost money and one of them costs more than money:

  • Too short — unnecessary opening-up, wasted spares and manhours, and defects introduced by reassembly. Dismantling a healthy machine is a real source of failure, not a neutral act.
  • Too long — wear beyond limits, consequential damage, unplanned breakdown, and on a critical item a safety or pollution consequence.

Optimisation therefore runs on evidence: as-found condition records, defect and failure history, condition-monitoring trends, maker guidance and criticality. Extending an interval is a controlled change — justified from data, risk assessed, approved by the Company's technical management, agreed with class or the maker where the item is credited or under warranty, and recorded. Shortening one is not free either, and the same evidence should support it.

Deferral is not interval change. Where a job cannot be done when due, the SMS must have a procedure: risk assessment, compensating measures, approval at the right level ashore, agreement of class or flag if the item is statutory or credited, a new date, and a record. Silent deferral is the failure mode, and a monthly review of overdue items is what catches it.

9. Periodicity of inspections

Inspections are a distinct rhythm from maintenance jobs, and the limb names them separately:

  • Continuous and daily — watchkeeping rounds, alarm and parameter monitoring.
  • Weekly and monthly — the statutory life-saving and fire-fighting appliance inspections and tests, emergency equipment tests, and the safety committee's own inspections.
  • Before departure — steering gear testing and the associated checks.
  • Periodic shipboard inspections — the master's and senior officers' inspections of accommodation, catering and working spaces, which are a seafarer welfare requirement as well as a maintenance one.
  • Annual and multi-annual — servicing and thorough examination of life-saving appliances, launching appliances and release gear, under the dedicated maintenance-requirements instrument in force since 1 January 2020; and the class and statutory survey cycle.
  • Internal safety audits — on board and ashore, at intervals not exceeding twelve months. This is the Code's own number and is worth stating exactly.
  • Management review — the periodical evaluation of the efficiency of the system, which is where the trends from limb (b) are supposed to arrive.

The closing point that ties all four limbs together. The four terms are one loop, not four subjects. Intervals generate the work; the work generates records; the records reveal non-conformities; the non-conformities drive corrective action; and corrective action changes the intervals. The systematic approach is simply the name for that loop being closed. A ship where the loop is open — where records are written but never read — has a planned maintenance system on paper and a reactive one in fact.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Four limbs, four marks each, printed as bare terms with almost no verb attached to them. That is the difficulty: the stem gives four nouns and leaves the candidate to supply the question. The structural key is that the four terms are, almost exactly, the four things ISM Code section 10 requires the Company to ensure — inspections at appropriate intervals, reporting of non-conformity, appropriate corrective action, and records of these activities.

Opening the answer with that mapping does three things at once. It shows the candidate recognised what the examiner was quoting from. It turns four disconnected essays into one loop. And it makes the closing paragraph write itself: intervals generate work, work generates records, records reveal non-conformities, non-conformities drive corrective action, and corrective action changes the intervals.

The other structural decision is to keep the class survey material to a single sentence. An approved planned maintenance scheme can be credited against continuous survey items — that is the whole link, and Q1 on this paper is where the survey side belongs. Writing it twice loses time in both.

The printed comma

The stem prints “...and ISM code with the following terms,” — a comma where a colon is required to introduce four lettered items. It reads as though the terms were a continuation of the sentence rather than a list. It also prints “ISM code” with a lower-case c, ends three limbs with semicolons and the fourth with a full stop, and capitalises “Inspections” mid-phrase.

All of it is preserved exactly as printed. The practical consequence is that the candidate must supply the structure the punctuation does not: four headings, four marks, four blocks, and no drift between them.

Correction, corrective action, preventive action

What it addressesBearing failure example
CorrectionThe symptomRenew the bearing
Corrective actionThe cause, on this shipFix the oil supply pressure and the alarm setting that allowed it
Preventive actionThe same cause elsewhereFleet circular, revised interval, amended procedure on the sister ships

The Code requires corrective action procedures to include measures intended to prevent recurrence, which is why a renewed bearing and a closed report is an audit finding rather than a closure. If you remember nothing else about limb (a), remember that replacing the part is not corrective action.

The two parts of section 10 that get left out

  • Critical equipment. The Company must identify equipment and technical systems whose sudden operational failure may result in a hazardous situation, and provide specific measures to promote reliability — expressly including regular testing of stand-by arrangements and of equipment not in continuous use. Emergency generator, emergency fire pump, standby pumps, emergency steering, lifeboat engines. Equipment that is never used is exactly the equipment that fails when it is needed.
  • Integration. Those inspections and measures must be integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine — in the PMS, not on a separate list somebody keeps in a drawer. An auditor who finds critical equipment tests outside the PMS has found a non-conformity.

A related trap in limb (d): a standby pump maintained on running hours may never come due at all, because it does not run. That is precisely why the standby-testing obligation exists and why the basis of an interval matters as much as its length.

Common mistakes

  • Writing one essay on planned maintenance and hoping it covers four limbs. Four terms, four headings, four marks each.
  • Never mentioning the ISM Code, when the stem names it.
  • Using “corrective action” to mean repairing the defect.
  • Omitting verification of effectiveness — an action that was done but did not work is reopened, not closed.
  • Describing records as a list of forms rather than as evidence, and never saying what makes a record good.
  • Leaving out as-found condition, which is what makes interval optimisation possible at all.
  • Answering limb (c) with the words “planned maintenance system” and no method — register, criticality, strategy, specification, plan, execute, review.
  • Omitting critical equipment and the standby-testing obligation.
  • In limb (d), giving intervals without their source, and so implying the Company can change a statutory one.
  • Treating interval extension as an engineering decision alone. It is a controlled change — justified, risk assessed, approved, and agreed with class or the maker where credited.
  • Confusing deferral with interval change. They are different acts with different approvals.
  • Forgetting the twelve-month internal audit ceiling, which is the one number the Code itself supplies.

Examiner traps

  • “Which section of the ISM Code covers maintenance?” Section 10 — maintenance of the ship and equipment. Section 9 covers reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences.
  • “You renewed the failed bearing. Is the non-conformity closed?” No — that is a correction. Corrective action removes the cause and must include measures to prevent recurrence.
  • “What is critical equipment?” Equipment and technical systems whose sudden operational failure may result in a hazardous situation.
  • “Give three examples and say what the Code requires for them.” Emergency generator, emergency fire pump, standby steering. Specific measures to promote reliability, including regular testing of standby arrangements and equipment not in continuous use.
  • “Where must those tests live?” Integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine — in the PMS.
  • “How often are internal safety audits?” At intervals not exceeding twelve months, on board and ashore.
  • “Who may extend a maintenance interval?” The Company for its own standards, with justification and risk assessment; not at all for a statutory interval; and only with the society's agreement where the item is class-credited.
  • “What is the risk of maintaining too often?” Wasted resource, and defects introduced by unnecessary dismantling and reassembly.
  • “A job cannot be done when due. What do you do?” Follow the deferral procedure — risk assessment, compensating measures, approval ashore, class or flag agreement if statutory or credited, a new date, and a record.
  • “Why does class care about your maintenance records?” Because under an approved planned maintenance scheme those records are what the credit against continuous survey items rests on, and the scheme is audited.
  • “What single field would you add to a poor job card?” As-found condition, with measured values against limits.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does the ISM Code require the Company to establish for maintenance? Procedures to ensure the ship is maintained in conformity with the relevant rules and with the Company's own additional requirements.
  • Name the four things section 10 requires the Company to ensure. Inspections at appropriate intervals; non-conformity reported with its possible cause; appropriate corrective action taken; and records of these activities maintained.
  • Who is the Designated Person Ashore and what is his role here? The link between ship and highest level of management, with access to the highest level, to whom non-conformities are reported.
  • Name four maintenance strategies. Calendar or running-hour preventive, condition-based, run to failure, and statutory or class-fixed.
  • What is a criticality assessment for? To decide which items get which strategy, and to identify the critical equipment the Code requires to be identified.
  • Name three condition-monitoring techniques. Vibration analysis, lubricating oil analysis, and thermographic or performance monitoring.
  • What makes a maintenance record good evidence? Complete, contemporaneous, attributable, accurate, legible, retrievable, retained and under document control.
  • Where does document control sit in the Code? Section 11, documentation.
  • What is the difference between deferring a job and extending an interval? Deferral moves one occurrence with a risk assessment and approval; extension changes the standard and needs justification, risk assessment and, where credited, class agreement.

Memory framework

One sentence for the whole answer: section 10 wants inspections at intervals, non-conformities reported, corrective action taken, and records kept — which is limbs (d), (a), (a) and (b), with (c) as the method that holds them together.

For limb (a), nine steps but four words: REPORT, INVESTIGATE, ACT, VERIFY — and one distinction: SYMPTOM, CAUSE, ELSEWHERE.

For limb (b), the good-record test in one line: WHAT DID YOU FIND, WHAT DID YOU MEASURE, WHAT DID YOU USE, WHAT DID YOU LEAVE?

For limb (c), seven steps: REGISTER, RATE, CHOOSE, SPECIFY, PLAN, DO, REVIEW — and the review step is what makes it a system.

For limb (d), four sources and four bases: sources STATUTE, CLASS, MAKER, COMPANY; bases CALENDAR, HOURS, USE, CONDITION. And one number: TWELVE MONTHS for internal audits.

Regulation and source map

  • ISM Code, base A.741(18) of 1993, mandatory through SOLAS chapter IX from 1 July 1998, as amended through MSC.353(92), in force 1 January 2015. That is the operative text at this sitting and is the latest Code amendment recorded.
  • Section 9 — reports and analysis of non-conformities, accidents and hazardous occurrences; corrective action including measures intended to prevent recurrence. Section 10 — maintenance of the ship and equipment: procedures, inspections at appropriate intervals, reporting, corrective action, records, critical equipment and standby testing, and integration into the operational maintenance routine. Section 11 — documentation and document control. Section 12 — internal safety audits at intervals not exceeding twelve months, and periodical evaluation of the efficiency of the system.
  • MSC.428(98) — maritime cyber risk management in the SMS; operative since the first Document of Compliance verification after 1 January 2021.
  • MSC.402(96) — maintenance, thorough examination, operational testing, overhaul and repair of lifeboats and rescue boats, launching appliances and release gear. In force 1 January 2020 through MSC.404(96). Its MSC.559(108) amendment enters force 1 January 2026 and is future here.
  • Classification society rules — approval of a planned maintenance scheme and its credit against continuous survey items. No society, scheme or software product is named; see the uncertainty section.
  • The Assembly guidance on implementation of the ISM Code by Administrations operative at this sitting is the predecessor of the December 2023 resolution. It is referred to descriptively and not by number.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

The primary layer here is instrument identity and status, not text, and the distinction is worth being exact about.

The ISM Code amendment chain is primary: the corpus central amendment register records the base, the mandatory route through SOLAS chapter IX, and the chain to MSC.353(92), in force 1 January 2015 and recorded as the latest Code amendment. The Code text itself is not held as an extractable corpus object, so sections 9, 10, 11 and 12 are described and not quoted, and no sub-paragraph is reproduced verbatim.

The mapping of the four printed terms onto section 10 is a structural reading derived in this session, not a statement printed in any instrument. It is offered as a reading and labelled as one, because it is the answer's spine and the reader is entitled to know where it came from.

The planned maintenance content is industry practice and engineering judgement. No company procedure, maintenance management software product, class approval scheme or society is named anywhere, and no numerical maintenance interval is asserted for any item of machinery — intervals are described by their source and their basis instead, because they are set by maker recommendation, class or statutory requirement and company standard, and none of those is held. The one number given, twelve months for internal safety audits, is the Code's own.

Currency, and one reversal. A.1188(33), the 2023 Guidelines on implementation of the ISM Code by Administrations, is a 6 December 2023 instrument — one month after this sitting. It is therefore future and is not cited, and the operative Assembly guidance here is its predecessor, referred to descriptively rather than by number because its identity could not be established from a held source. MSC.559(108) is future for the same reason, entering force 1 January 2026. Both are exactly the kind of instrument a later editor would import.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The structure behind the four printed terms
  2. __________The three things that are not the same
  3. __________The process, end to end
  4. __________What a record must contain, and what makes it good
  5. __________How a mediocre record system is actually improved
  6. __________The closed loop
  7. __________Where an interval comes from
  8. __________Optimising an interval, and changing one safely
  9. __________Periodicity of inspections
Flashcards 11

That inspections are held at appropriate intervals; that any non-conformity is reported, with its possible cause if known; that appropriate corrective action is taken; and that records of these activities are maintained. All four sit under the opening requirement to establish procedures ensuring the ship is maintained in conformity with the relevant rules and with the Company's own additional requirements.

These four are, almost term for term, the four limbs the paper prints - recognising that is the answer's spine.

Correction fixes the symptom and restores function - renew the failed bearing. Corrective action removes the cause so it does not recur on this ship - correct the oil supply pressure and the alarm setting that allowed the failure. Preventive action stops the same cause biting elsewhere - a fleet circular, a revised interval, an amended procedure on the sister ships. The Code requires corrective action procedures to include measures intended to prevent recurrence.

One word is routinely used for three different acts, and only one of them is what the Code asks for.

Report the non-conformity with its possible cause; contain it and make safe, informing the master and the DPA and notifying class or flag where statutory or credited; investigate and analyse the root cause past the equipment to the system; decide corrective and preventive action; assign an owner, resources and a date; implement and record; verify both that it was done and that it worked, reopening it if not; disseminate to sister ships and amend the procedure or the interval; then trend and verify through internal audit and management review.

Report, investigate, act, verify - and the verification of EFFECTIVENESS is what most systems omit.

Equipment and technical systems the sudden operational failure of which may result in a hazardous situation. The Company must identify them and provide specific measures to promote their reliability, including the regular testing of standby arrangements and of equipment or technical systems not in continuous use - and those measures must be integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine rather than kept on a separate list.

It is the part of section 10 most often omitted, and the integration requirement is a standing audit finding.

As-found condition, with measured values against limits rather than a tick. Without as-found condition there is no evidence of how the machine actually was when it was opened, and therefore no basis on which to lengthen or shorten the interval - which means the whole feedback loop that makes a planned maintenance system 'planned' cannot run.

It connects limb (b) to limb (d) in one sentence and is the highest-value practical point in the question.

That it is complete, contemporaneous, attributable to a named person, accurate with measured values, legible, retrievable, retained for the required period, and under document control with obsolete versions removed and the electronic system backed up. Records in that state support class credit under an approved scheme, port State control, vetting, warranty and insurance claims, charter disputes, root cause analysis and interval optimisation.

The commercial consequences are what turn a bureaucratic answer into a Chief Engineer's answer.

Register every item with a tagging hierarchy so nothing sits outside the system; assess criticality and identify the critical equipment; choose a maintenance strategy per item - calendar or running-hour preventive, condition-based, run to failure, or statutory and class-fixed; write the job specification with tools, spares, permits and what is to be measured; plan and schedule against manning, port calls and the survey and docking windows; execute and record under permit to work; and review, feeding defects, condition data and audit findings back into criticality, strategy and interval.

The seventh step is what makes it a system rather than a schedule.

Statutory intervals from SOLAS and MARPOL - the Company cannot change them. Classification intervals, including the terms of an approved maintenance scheme - only with the society's agreement, and the survey credit is at risk if not. Maker's recommendations - changeable with justification, though warranty and technical support may follow them. Company standards - changeable, and this is where fleet experience is applied. Condition-based intervals are set by the machine, the Company setting the monitoring regime instead.

Implying that the Company may vary a statutory interval is the structural error in limb (d).

Because unnecessary dismantling introduces defects - assembly error, contamination, gasket and fastener failure - in addition to wasting spares and manhours. Too seldom risks wear beyond limits, consequential damage and unplanned breakdown, and on a critical item a safety or pollution consequence. Both directions are errors, which is why interval optimisation runs on as-found records, failure history and condition trends.

It is the judgement behind condition-based maintenance and it links limb (d) back to limb (b).

Follow the deferral procedure: risk assess it, put compensating measures in place, obtain approval at the right level ashore, obtain class or flag agreement if the item is statutory or class-credited, set a new date and record all of it. Extending the interval is a different act - a controlled change to the standard, justified from data, risk assessed, approved by technical management and agreed with class or the maker where credited. Silent deferral is the failure mode and a monthly overdue review is what catches it.

Confusing a one-off deferral with a permanent interval change is how a PMS quietly stops being planned.

On board and ashore at intervals not exceeding twelve months, under ISM Code section 12, which also requires the Company to conduct a periodical evaluation of the efficiency of the safety management system.

It is the one number the Code itself supplies in this area, and it is the mechanism by which the whole loop is checked.

Quick revision
15-second recall
The four printed terms ARE ISM Code section 10: inspections at appropriate intervals (d), non-conformity reported with possible cause, appropriate corrective action taken (a), and records of these activities maintained (b) — with the procedures, critical equipment and integration requirements making up the systematic approach (c). Section 9 requires reporting, investigation and analysis, and corrective action including measures to prevent recurrence. Correction fixes the symptom; corrective action removes the cause; preventive action stops it elsewhere. Process: report → contain → investigate root cause → decide corrective and preventive → assign owner and date → implement and record → verify close-out AND effectiveness → disseminate → trend and audit. Records must carry as-found and as-left condition, measured values against limits, running hours, spares by part number, and who did it — complete, contemporaneous, attributable, accurate, retrievable, retained and under document control (section 11). Improve them by standardising job cards, making measurement fields mandatory, recording at the machine, trending, monthly overdue review, internal audit and feedback into the PMS. Systematic approach: register → criticality → strategy per item → job specification → plan against the trading pattern → execute → review. Critical equipment — sudden failure may cause a hazardous situation — gets specific reliability measures including regular testing of standby arrangements and equipment not in continuous use, and those tests must be integrated into the operational maintenance routine. Intervals come from statute (unchangeable), class (society's agreement), maker and Company; bases are calendar, running hours, use and condition. Too short introduces defects; too long risks failure. Extension is a controlled change; deferral is a different act with its own approval. Internal safety audits at intervals not exceeding twelve months.

Critical numbers
