Critical regulation
ISM Code section 10 is the whole question. It requires the Company to establish procedures to ensure the ship is maintained in conformity with the relevant rules and regulations and with any additional requirements the Company establishes; and, in meeting that, to ensure that inspections are held at appropriate intervals, that any non-conformity is reported with its possible cause where known, that appropriate corrective action is taken, and that records of these activities are maintained. It further requires critical equipment to be identified and given specific reliability measures including regular testing of standby arrangements and equipment not in continuous use, and requires those inspections and measures to be integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine. Read it with section 9 for corrective action including measures to prevent recurrence, section 11 for document control, and section 12 for internal audits at intervals not exceeding twelve months.
Major trap
Using “corrective action” to mean repairing the defect. Replacing the failed component is a correction; corrective action removes the cause, and the Code expressly requires the procedures to include measures intended to prevent recurrence, which reaches beyond this ship to the sister vessels. A non-conformity closed on a replaced part with nothing else changed is the commonest audit finding in this area. The paired traps are omitting critical equipment and the standby-testing obligation — equipment that never runs is exactly what fails when it is needed — and implying in limb (d) that the Company may change a statutory interval, when it may change only its own standards and, with the society's agreement, class-credited ones.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q1 - the continuous survey of machinery, the class survey regime that an approved planned maintenance scheme is credited against · QP2311 Q2 - performance monitoring and condition data, which is what a condition-based maintenance strategy runs on

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

There is one idea holding this whole question together: a fuel is bought against a written description, and everything else follows from whether it matches. The description is an international specification — a list of properties with limits, plus a catch-all sentence saying the fuel must not contain anything that harms people, endangers the ship or damages the machinery. That catch-all matters more than it looks, because it is what you rely on when a fuel wrecks an engine while passing every number in the table. Two things about that description are easy to get wrong. It is tied to a particular version, and versions differ, so a fuel can pass against one and fail against another. And it describes the fuel as it arrives at the ship's side — not as it should be by the time it reaches the injector. Closing that gap is exactly what the settling tanks and separators are for, which is why a perfectly acceptable delivery can still ruin liners if the treatment plant is run badly. When the laboratory report comes back, the instinct is to start fixing things. The better instinct is to stop: confirm the sample really is the fuel you were given, check whether the failing number is outside the limit by more than the test's own margin of error, keep the fuel in its own tanks and unburnt, and tell the supplier inside whatever time the contract allows. Burning the fuel while arguing about it destroys both the evidence and the argument. Only then does the question of what to do with it arise — and the sorting that matters is between the faults you can manage by heating, cleaning and easing the load, and the three you cannot: a fuel that will catch fire too easily, a fuel that breaks the law on sulphur, and a fuel that has something in it that should never have been there.

Knowledge map
ISO 8217 Marine Fuel Standards, the Fuel Oil Analysis Report and Corrective Action
  1. 1The present standards - and which edition is present
    • the marine fuel specification is ISO 8217, Petroleum products - Fuels (class F) - Specifications of marine fuels
    • THE EDITION MATTERS AND MUST BE STATED - the specification has been revised in 2005, 2010, 2012 and 2017
    • THE PRESENT EDITION AT THIS SITTING IS THE SIXTH, ISO 8217:2017
  2. 2Salient features of the specification
    • TWO TABLES - one for distillate grades and one for residual grades, the residual grades ordered principally by kinematic viscosity
    • THE GENERAL REQUIREMENTS CLAUSE IS THE MOST IMPORTANT SENTENCE IN THE STANDARD - the fuel shall be a homogeneous blend of hydrocarbons free from any material at a concentration that causes harm to personnel, jeopardises the safety of the ship or adversely affects machinery performance
    • that clause catches contamination the numerical table never anticipated, and it is what a damage claim is founded on when every tabulated limit was met
  3. 3The content of a fuel oil analysis report
    • SAMPLE AND DELIVERY IDENTITY - ship, port, date, supplier and barge, the bunker delivery note reference, the grade and quantity ordered, the SAMPLE SEAL NUMBER, the sampling point and method, and the type of sample
    • this section is what makes the rest admissible - an analysis of a sample that cannot be tied to the delivery proves nothing
    • THE RESULTS TABLE - for every parameter the result, the unit, the specification limit for the grade ordered, THE TEST METHOD, and a pass or fail indication
  4. 4Before any corrective action - four steps that come first
    • VERIFY THE IDENTITY OF THE SAMPLE - seal number against the bunker delivery note, grade against what was ordered; a report on the wrong sample has caused an unnecessary debunkering
    • APPLY THE PRECISION - a result marginally outside a limit may be within the reproducibility of the method, and under ISO 4259 that is not a proven off-specification fuel
    • SEGREGATE AND DO NOT CONSUME - keep the bunker in its own tanks, do not commingle, do not put it into service until cleared; ONCE IT IS BURNT THE EVIDENCE AND THE CLAIM ARE GONE
  5. 5Corrective action, observation by observation
    • VISCOSITY HIGH - check heater capacity and trace heating, raise temperatures within limits under viscometer control; if injection viscosity cannot be reached, segregate and blend down or land the fuel
    • VISCOSITY LOW - reduce heating and cool where a cooler is fitted, control to the minimum injection viscosity, watch for fuel pump leakage
    • DENSITY HIGH - confirm the separator's rated capability, select the correct gravity disc or interface, reduce throughput and raise separation temperature, use two separators where fitted
  6. 6The judgement to close on
    • THREE FINDINGS ARE NOT TREATABLE AND THE FUEL IS NOT BURNT - flash point below the statutory minimum, sulphur above the applicable limit where it applies, and chemical or used-lubricating-oil contamination
    • everything else is a question of treatment, load and monitoring
    • knowing which class a finding falls into - TREAT IT OR REFUSE IT - is the whole of the engineering judgement this question tests
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. The present standards - and which edition is present
  2. Salient features of the specification
  3. The content of a fuel oil analysis report
  4. Before any corrective action - four steps that come first
  5. Corrective action, observation by observation
  6. The judgement to close on

Memory cue. Standards: 8217 SPECIFIES, 8216 CLASSIFIES, 4259 DECIDES (1) - and always the EDITION (1). Features: GENERAL CLAUSE, SULPHUR DEFERS, DELIVERY NOT INLET (2). Report: WHO, WHAT, DERIVED, COMMENT, VERDICT, LAB (3). Before acting: IDENTIFY, PRECISION, SEGREGATE, NOTIFY (4). Then TREAT IT OR REFUSE IT (6).

Remember6 route headings
Cover54 core points beneath them

Memorise the 6 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 54 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The present standards - and which edition is present
  • the marine fuel specification is ISO 8217, Petroleum products - Fuels (class F) - Specifications of marine fuels
  • THE EDITION MATTERS AND MUST BE STATED - the specification has been revised in 2005, 2010, 2012 and 2017
  • THE PRESENT EDITION AT THIS SITTING IS THE SIXTH, ISO 8217:2017
  • a purchase is made against a NAMED EDITION, because parameters and limits differ between editions and a fuel on-spec against one may be off-spec against another
  • ISO 8216-1 - classification and designation of marine fuels
  • ISO 4259 - precision of test methods and interpretation of results; the most important supporting standard in practice because it decides whether a marginal result is genuinely off-spec
  • ISO/PAS 23263:2019 - guidance on fuels meeting the 0.50% sulphur limit, their compatibility and stability
  • and the individual test method standards for density, viscosity, sulphur, water, ash, carbon residue, sediment, flash point, pour point and metals
2. Salient features of the specification
  • TWO TABLES - one for distillate grades and one for residual grades, the residual grades ordered principally by kinematic viscosity
  • THE GENERAL REQUIREMENTS CLAUSE IS THE MOST IMPORTANT SENTENCE IN THE STANDARD - the fuel shall be a homogeneous blend of hydrocarbons free from any material at a concentration that causes harm to personnel, jeopardises the safety of the ship or adversely affects machinery performance
  • that clause catches contamination the numerical table never anticipated, and it is what a damage claim is founded on when every tabulated limit was met
  • SULPHUR DEFERS TO STATUTE - the purchaser specifies it in accordance with the relevant statutory requirements, so MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14 governs: 0.50% m/m globally since 1 January 2020 and 0.10% m/m in emission control areas
  • BIO-CONTENT IS EXPLICIT - the 2017 edition introduced distillate grades permitting a defined proportion of fatty acid methyl ester, distinguished by an F in the designation, alongside grades that must be substantially free of it
  • THE SPECIFICATION IS A DELIVERY SPECIFICATION, NOT AN ENGINE-INLET SPECIFICATION - the abrasive particulate permitted at delivery is substantially higher than engine makers accept at the inlet, and closing that gap is the fuel treatment plant's whole job
  • FLASH POINT IS STATUTORY, NOT COMMERCIAL - the 60 degree Celsius minimum for fuel oil in machinery spaces is a SOLAS requirement, so a low result is a safety breach
  • PRECISION IS BUILT IN - read with ISO 4259, a result outside a limit by less than the method's reproducibility is not proof of an off-spec fuel
  • GROUP THE PARAMETERS BY THE FAILURE THEY THREATEN - handling, combustion, wear, corrosion, stability, safety, contamination, statutory
3. The content of a fuel oil analysis report
  • SAMPLE AND DELIVERY IDENTITY - ship, port, date, supplier and barge, the bunker delivery note reference, the grade and quantity ordered, the SAMPLE SEAL NUMBER, the sampling point and method, and the type of sample
  • this section is what makes the rest admissible - an analysis of a sample that cannot be tied to the delivery proves nothing
  • THE RESULTS TABLE - for every parameter the result, the unit, the specification limit for the grade ordered, THE TEST METHOD, and a pass or fail indication
  • the method must be stated because the limit only means anything against the method
  • DERIVED AND CALCULATED VALUES - the calculated carbon aromaticity index as an indication of ignition quality, the estimated net specific energy, and often a recommended purifier temperature and throughput
  • COMMENTS AND RECOMMENDATIONS - the laboratory's narrative on marginal or adverse results, handling and treatment advice, and any warning about commingling
  • THE VERDICT - on specification, off specification, or on specification with cautions - and for a marginal result a statement of the METHOD PRECISION so the reader can see whether the deviation is real
  • LABORATORY IDENTITY AND TRACEABILITY - the laboratory, its accreditation, dates of receipt and testing, and the report reference, without which the report is not usable in a claim
4. Before any corrective action - four steps that come first
  • VERIFY THE IDENTITY OF THE SAMPLE - seal number against the bunker delivery note, grade against what was ordered; a report on the wrong sample has caused an unnecessary debunkering
  • APPLY THE PRECISION - a result marginally outside a limit may be within the reproducibility of the method, and under ISO 4259 that is not a proven off-specification fuel
  • SEGREGATE AND DO NOT CONSUME - keep the bunker in its own tanks, do not commingle, do not put it into service until cleared; ONCE IT IS BURNT THE EVIDENCE AND THE CLAIM ARE GONE
  • NOTIFY INSIDE THE CONTRACTUAL WINDOW - owners, charterers, supplier and club as appropriate, with a letter of protest; the applicable period is read from the contract, not assumed
  • and usually a joint analysis of the MARPOL delivered sample is the next step
  • the regulatory scaffolding is MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18 - the bunker delivery note with the prescribed information retained three years, the MARPOL delivered sample sealed and signed and retained at least twelve months and analysed by the prescribed method, port State inspection and certified copies, and port State follow-up where non-compliant fuel is found
5. Corrective action, observation by observation
  • VISCOSITY HIGH - check heater capacity and trace heating, raise temperatures within limits under viscometer control; if injection viscosity cannot be reached, segregate and blend down or land the fuel
  • VISCOSITY LOW - reduce heating and cool where a cooler is fitted, control to the minimum injection viscosity, watch for fuel pump leakage
  • DENSITY HIGH - confirm the separator's rated capability, select the correct gravity disc or interface, reduce throughput and raise separation temperature, use two separators where fitted
  • HIGH CCAI - poor ignition quality; raise fuel and jacket temperatures, maintain scavenge air temperature, retard timing where permitted, reduce load, ensure injector condition, blend with a better-igniting fuel
  • WATER HIGH - drain tanks frequently, settle at temperature, purify at reduced throughput, TEST FOR CHLORIDES to establish whether it is seawater, and if seawater in quantity consider debunkering
  • CATALYTIC FINES HIGH - the most destructive fuel fault; settle and drain, purify at recommended temperature and reduced throughput in series or parallel, SAMPLE AT THE ENGINE INLET to prove removal, reduce load, blend down, and debunker if extreme
  • TOTAL SEDIMENT HIGH - segregate and do not commingle, compatibility spot test before any unavoidable mixing, increase purification and filter and tank cleaning, use promptly, debunker if severe
  • VANADIUM AND SODIUM HIGH - high-temperature corrosion of valves and nozzle rings; purify thoroughly since sodium is largely water-borne, maintain valve rotation and temperatures, shorten inspection intervals, additive only where the maker permits
  • ASH AND CARBON RESIDUE HIGH - deposits and fouling; optimise combustion, increase turbocharger and gas-way cleaning, review cylinder oil feed rate and base number
  • POUR POINT HIGH - keep tanks, lines and filters heated above the pour point with a margin, avoid cold spots and dead legs, plan changeovers so cold fuel is never drawn
  • USED LUBRICATING OIL CONTAMINATION - do not use, segregate, notify supplier owners and club at once, joint analysis of the MARPOL sample, DEBUNKER
  • ACID NUMBER HIGH - segregate, monitor fuel system components, consider blending down or landing
  • HYDROGEN SULPHIDE HIGH - treat as a PERSONNEL HAZARD first: gas monitoring, no tank entry without full enclosed-space procedure, ventilation, restricted sounding and ullaging
  • FAME ABOVE GRADE - keep water out and drain tanks frequently, use promptly, monitor filters, biocide only where the maker permits
  • FLASH POINT BELOW 60 DEGREES CELSIUS - DO NOT USE; segregate, inform master, owners, flag and supplier, land the fuel; NEVER corrected by blending
  • SULPHUR ABOVE THE APPLICABLE LIMIT - do not burn where the limit applies, change over to compliant fuel, notify flag, port State and supplier and record the sequence, retain the MARPOL sample; a non-availability report is a record of a supply failure and NOT a defence for burning fuel already known to be non-compliant
  • LOW NET SPECIFIC ENERGY - a commercial loss; adjust consumption baselines and performance monitoring, and raise a commercial claim
6. The judgement to close on
  • THREE FINDINGS ARE NOT TREATABLE AND THE FUEL IS NOT BURNT - flash point below the statutory minimum, sulphur above the applicable limit where it applies, and chemical or used-lubricating-oil contamination
  • everything else is a question of treatment, load and monitoring
  • knowing which class a finding falls into - TREAT IT OR REFUSE IT - is the whole of the engineering judgement this question tests
  • and one closing structural point - the specification governs the fuel AS DELIVERED, while the engine is protected by the treatment plant and how it is run
  • a perfectly on-specification bunker will still destroy liners if the separators run at full throughput and the tanks are never drained
  • THE ANALYSIS REPORT TELLS YOU WHAT YOU HAVE BEEN GIVEN; IT DOES NOT TELL YOU WHAT WILL ARRIVE AT THE INJECTOR
Model written answer

1. The present standards - and which edition is present

The marine fuel specification is ISO 8217, “Petroleum products — Fuels (class F) — Specifications of marine fuels”. It is the standard against which a bunker is bought, delivered and disputed.

The edition matters and must be stated. The specification has been revised several times — 2005, 2010, 2012 and 2017 — and the present edition is the sixth, ISO 8217:2017. A purchase is made against a named edition, because the parameters and their limits differ between them, and a bunker meeting one edition may be off-specification against another. Naming the standard without naming the edition is an incomplete answer.

A complete answer names the supporting standards as well, because the specification does not work alone:

StandardWhat it does
ISO 8217The specification. Grades, parameters and limits for marine distillate and residual fuels
ISO 8216-1Classification and designation — the system by which a grade such as a residual marine grade of a given viscosity gets its name
ISO 4259Precision of test methods and interpretation of results. The most important of the supporting standards in practice, because it is what decides whether a marginal result is genuinely off-spec
ISO/PAS 23263:2019Guidance on fuels meeting the 0.50% sulphur limit — compatibility, stability and the handling of blended low-sulphur fuels
The individual test methodsDensity, kinematic viscosity, sulphur, water, ash, carbon residue, total sediment, flash point, pour point and metals — each parameter is defined by the method used to measure it

2. Salient features of the specification

  1. Two tables. One for distillate grades and one for residual grades, the residual grades ordered principally by kinematic viscosity. The grade name is the shorthand by which the fuel is ordered.
  2. The general requirements clause is the most important sentence in the standard. It requires the fuel to be a homogeneous blend of hydrocarbons and to be free from any material at a concentration that causes harm to personnel, jeopardises the safety of the ship, or adversely affects the performance of the machinery. That clause catches contamination the numerical table never anticipated — chemical waste, used lubricating oil, styrene, phenols — and it is what a claim is founded on when a fuel damages an engine while meeting every tabulated limit.
  3. Sulphur defers to statute. The standard requires the purchaser to specify the maximum sulphur content in accordance with the relevant statutory requirements. So the operative sulphur limit is MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14 — 0.50% m/m globally since 1 January 2020 and 0.10% m/m in emission control areas — and not a number in the specification.
  4. Bio-content is now explicit. The 2017 edition introduced distillate grades that permit a defined proportion of fatty acid methyl ester, distinguished by an F in the grade designation, alongside grades that must be substantially free of it. That matters operationally because FAME brings oxidation stability, microbial growth and filter-blocking questions with it.
  5. The specification is a delivery specification, not an engine-inlet specification. A residual fuel may be perfectly on-spec at the manifold and still unfit to burn until it has been settled and purified. The clearest example is abrasive particulate: the concentration permitted at delivery is substantially higher than the concentration engine makers accept at the engine inlet. The gap between those two is the fuel treatment plant's whole job.
  6. Flash point is a statutory matter, not a commercial one. The minimum of 60 degrees Celsius for fuel oil used in machinery spaces is a SOLAS requirement. A fuel below it is not a quality dispute; it is a safety breach.
  7. Precision is built in. Because every parameter is a measurement, the standard is read with ISO 4259: a result that differs from the limit by less than the method's reproducibility is not proof of an off-spec fuel. This is what decides most marginal disputes.

Grouping the parameters by the failure they threaten is how the specification becomes usable rather than memorised:

What it threatensParameters
Handling and treatmentKinematic viscosity, density, pour point, water
Combustion and ignitionCalculated carbon aromaticity index, micro carbon residue, viscosity at injection
Abrasive wearAluminium plus silicon — the catalytic fines
High-temperature corrosionVanadium, sodium, and the ratio between them; ash
Stability and compatibilityTotal sediment, potential and aged; FAME content; oxidation stability
Safety and healthFlash point, hydrogen sulphide
ContaminationUsed lubricating oil markers — calcium with zinc, calcium with phosphorus; acid number
StatutorySulphur

3. The content of a fuel oil analysis report

A report has six parts. Candidates normally give the second and omit the rest, and the omitted parts are where the marks are:

  1. Sample and delivery identity. Ship, port, date, supplier and barge, the bunker delivery note reference, the grade and quantity ordered, the sample seal number, the sampling point and method, and the type of sample — the MARPOL delivered sample, the ship's own sample, or a commercial sample. This section is what makes the rest admissible: an analysis of a sample that cannot be tied to the delivery proves nothing.
  2. The results table. For every parameter: the result, the unit, the specification limit for the grade ordered, the test method used, and a pass or fail indication. The test method must be stated because the limit only means anything against the method.
  3. Derived and calculated values. The calculated carbon aromaticity index as an indication of ignition quality; the estimated net specific energy, which is what the fuel is actually worth per tonne; and often a recommended purifier temperature and throughput.
  4. Comments and recommendations. The laboratory's narrative on the marginal or adverse results, the handling and treatment advice, and any warning about commingling with the fuel already on board.
  5. The verdict. An overall statement — on specification, off specification, or on specification with cautions — and, where a result is marginal, a statement of the precision of the method under ISO 4259 so that the reader can see whether the deviation is real.
  6. Laboratory identity and traceability. The laboratory, its accreditation, the date of receipt and of testing, and the report reference — without which the report is not usable in a claim.

4. Before any corrective action - four steps that come first

This is the part a Chief Engineer is actually marked on, and it is almost always missing:

  1. Verify the identity of the sample. Seal number against the bunker delivery note, grade against what was ordered. A report on the wrong sample has caused a debunkering that was never needed.
  2. Apply the precision. A result marginally outside a limit may be within the reproducibility of the test method. Under ISO 4259, that is not a proven off-specification fuel — and treating it as one starts a claim that will fail.
  3. Segregate and do not consume. Keep the bunker in its own tanks, do not commingle it, and do not put it into service until it is cleared. Once it is burnt, the evidence and the claim are gone.
  4. Notify inside the contractual window. Owners, charterers, the supplier and the club as appropriate, with a letter of protest. Supply contracts commonly impose a short period for a quality claim — the applicable period is read from the contract, not assumed — and a joint analysis of the MARPOL delivered sample is usually the next step.

The regulatory scaffolding behind those steps is MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18: the bunker delivery note with the information the Annex prescribes, retained on board for three years; the MARPOL delivered sample, sealed, signed and retained for at least twelve months, to be analysed by the prescribed method; the port State's right to inspect the note and take certified copies; and the port State's follow-up duties where non-compliant fuel is found.

5. Corrective action, observation by observation

Adverse observationWhat it threatensCorrective action
Viscosity higher than the grade orderedCannot reach injection viscosity; poor atomisation, afterburning, foulingCheck heater capacity and trace heating; raise settling, service and injection temperatures within limits and control on a viscometer; if injection viscosity still cannot be reached, segregate and blend down or land the fuel
Viscosity lower than expectedLeakage past fuel pump plungers, loss of injection pressure, poor lubrication of the pump elementsReduce heating; where a cooler is fitted, control to the minimum injection viscosity; watch for pump leakage and sticking
Density highSeparation efficiency falls; water and solids may not be removedConfirm the separator's rated capability for the density; select the correct gravity disc or set the clean-oil interface; reduce throughput and raise separation temperature to the recommended figure; use two separators where fitted
High calculated carbon aromaticity indexPoor ignition quality — long ignition delay, rapid pressure rise, knock, high peak pressure, liner and bearing damageRaise fuel and jacket cooling water temperatures; maintain scavenge air temperature; retard injection timing where the engine permits; reduce load and avoid rapid load changes; ensure injector condition and correct opening pressure; blend with a better-igniting fuel
Water highLoss of energy, sludge, filter blocking, corrosion; if seawater, sodium-driven high-temperature corrosionDrain settling and service tanks frequently; settle at temperature; purify at reduced throughput; test for chlorides to establish whether it is seawater; if seawater in quantity, treat as a serious contamination and consider debunkering
Aluminium plus silicon high — catalytic finesSevere abrasive wear of liners, rings, fuel pumps and injectors — the single most destructive fuel faultSettle for as long as possible with frequent tank draining; run separators at the recommended temperature and reduced throughput, in series or parallel as fitted; sample at the engine inlet, not just at the manifold, and verify the removal actually achieved; reduce load; blend down; and where the delivered concentration is extreme, debunker
Total sediment high (potential or aged)Instability or incompatibility; sludging, separator overload, filter and purifier blockingKeep the fuel segregated and do not commingle; carry out a compatibility spot test before any mixing becomes unavoidable; increase purification and filter and tank cleaning frequency; use the fuel promptly; if severe, debunker
Vanadium and sodium high, or an adverse sodium-to-vanadium ratioHigh-temperature corrosion of exhaust valves, seats and turbocharger nozzle ringsPurify thoroughly — sodium is largely water-borne and is removed with the water; maintain valve rotation and correct valve temperatures; monitor exhaust valve condition and shorten inspection intervals; use an approved fuel additive only where the maker permits
Ash, micro carbon residue highDeposits on rings, liner, exhaust valves and turbine; fouling and gas-way depositsOptimise combustion — injection condition, air supply, load; increase turbocharger and gas-way cleaning; review cylinder oil feed rate and base number
Pour point highWax; blocked lines, filters and suction strainers, especially in cold weatherKeep tanks, lines and filters heated and traced above the pour point with a margin; avoid cold spots and dead legs; plan tank changeovers so cold fuel is never drawn
Used lubricating oil contamination — calcium with zinc or phosphorusAbrasive and corrosive damage; and a breach of the specification's general requirements clause however the numbers readDo not use; segregate; notify supplier, owners and club at once; obtain joint analysis of the MARPOL delivered sample; debunker
Acid number highCorrosion of fuel pumps, injectors and the fuel systemSegregate; monitor fuel system components closely; take advice; consider blending down or landing the fuel
Hydrogen sulphide highToxic to personnel in tank atmospheres and around sounding pipes and ventsTreat as a personnel hazard first: gas monitoring, no tank entry without full enclosed-space procedure, ventilation, restricted sounding and ullaging, and a briefing to all concerned
FAME above the grade, or present in a grade that should be free of itOxidation, microbial growth, sludge and filter blocking; poor storage lifeKeep water out — drain tanks frequently; use the fuel promptly; monitor filters; treat only with an approved biocide and only where the maker permits
Flash point below 60 degrees CelsiusFire and explosion. A SOLAS breach, not a commercial disputeDo not use. Segregate, inform master, owners, flag and supplier immediately, and land the fuel. It is never corrected by blending
Sulphur above the applicable statutory limitNon-compliance with MARPOL Annex VI — detention, fine, and a port State recordDo not burn it where the limit applies; change over to compliant fuel; notify flag, port State and supplier and record the sequence; retain the MARPOL delivered sample for verification. A non-availability report is a record of a supply failure, not a defence for burning a fuel you already know is non-compliant
Low net specific energyHigher consumption for the same work — a commercial loss rather than a technical faultAdjust consumption expectations and performance monitoring baselines; raise a commercial claim on the quantity-energy shortfall

6. The judgement to close on

Three findings are not treatable and the correct action is not to burn the fuel: a flash point below the statutory minimum, sulphur above the applicable limit where that limit applies, and chemical or used-lubricating-oil contamination. Everything else in the table is a question of treatment, load and monitoring. Knowing which class a finding falls into — treat it, or refuse it — is the whole of the engineering judgement this question is testing.

And one closing structural point: the specification governs the fuel as delivered, while the engine is protected by the fuel treatment plant and the way it is run. A perfectly on-specification bunker will still destroy liners if the separators are run at full throughput and the tanks are never drained. The analysis report tells you what you have been given; it does not tell you what will arrive at the injector.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The paper prints 16 marks in one unlimbed block, and hides three tasks inside two sentences: name the standards and give their salient features; state the content of an analysis report; and give the corrective action for each adverse observation. Because no marks are apportioned, the candidate must apportion them, and the apportionment is the first decision of the answer.

The third task is the largest — the word is “each” — and it goes in a three-column table: observation, what it threatens, what you do. A table is not decoration here; it is the only format in which fifteen or more findings can be delivered inside an examination and still be read. The first task gets a table of standards and a numbered list of features. The second gets six numbered parts.

The highest-value section is the one nobody writes: what happens before any corrective action — check the sample identity, apply the precision, segregate and do not burn, notify inside the contractual window. That is the difference between an engineer's answer and a textbook's.

The edition is the answer

The standard is ISO 8217. The present edition at this sitting is the sixth, ISO 8217:2017.

This matters more than it looks. A bunker is bought against a named edition, and the parameters and limits differ between editions — a fuel that is on-spec against one may be off-spec against another. Naming the standard without its edition answers half the first task.

It also matters for a reason peculiar to this paper: the seventh edition was published in 2024, eight months after this examination. Every later paper in the corpus that sets this question is entitled to call the 2024 edition current. Here it does not exist. That is the single most dangerous inherited error available on this paper, and it is the reason the uncertainty section below exists.

The three sentences that carry the first task

  • The general requirements clause — the fuel shall be a homogeneous blend of hydrocarbons, free from any material at a concentration that causes harm to personnel, jeopardises the safety of the ship or adversely affects machinery performance. It catches what the numerical table cannot, and it is what a damage claim is built on when every tabulated limit was met.
  • Sulphur defers to statute. The purchaser specifies it in accordance with the relevant statutory requirements, so the operative limits are MARPOL's — 0.50% globally, 0.10% in emission control areas — not the standard's.
  • The specification is a DELIVERY specification. What is acceptable at the manifold is not what is acceptable at the injector, and the gap between the two is precisely what the settling tanks and separators exist to close.

ISO 4259: the standard that decides disputes

Every parameter is a measurement, and every measurement has a reproducibility. A result that lies outside a limit by less than the method's reproducibility is not proof that the fuel is off-specification.

The practical consequences are two. A Chief Engineer who declares a fuel off-spec on a marginal result starts a claim that will fail and may have segregated a perfectly usable bunker. And on the other side, a marginal sulphur result close to a statutory limit is exactly where the argument between ship, supplier and port State is conducted. Naming ISO 4259 and saying what it does is worth a mark on its own.

Treat it, or refuse it

ClassFindingsAction
RefuseFlash point below 60 °C; sulphur above the applicable statutory limit; chemical or used-lubricating-oil contaminationDo not burn. Segregate, notify, land the fuel. Never blend to correct a flash point
TreatViscosity, density, water, catalytic fines, sediment, vanadium and sodium, ash, pour point, FAMESettling, purification, temperature and throughput control, load management, monitoring and shortened inspection intervals
AbsorbLow net specific energyA commercial loss. Adjust consumption baselines and claim

Being able to place a finding in the right row is the judgement the question is testing. Most candidates put everything in the middle row.

Common mistakes

  • Naming ISO 8217 without its edition.
  • Naming only ISO 8217 and omitting ISO 4259, which is the one that decides disputes.
  • Reciting grade tables and limit values instead of explaining the features of the specification.
  • Omitting the general requirements clause, which is the most important sentence in the standard.
  • Saying the standard sets the sulphur limit. It defers to MARPOL.
  • Skipping the content of the report altogether — this paper asks for it and the later member of this family does not, so an answer inherited from that sitting omits it.
  • Giving the results table as the whole report and omitting sample identity, method, precision and laboratory traceability.
  • Jumping straight to corrective action without verifying sample identity, applying precision, segregating and notifying.
  • Burning the fuel while the dispute is open, which destroys both the evidence and the claim.
  • Treating a low flash point as a quality problem to be blended away. It is a safety breach and the fuel is landed.
  • Treating a non-availability report as a licence to burn non-compliant fuel. It records a supply failure; it is not a defence for burning fuel you already know is non-compliant.
  • Giving catalytic fines only a delivery limit and never mentioning engine-inlet monitoring, which is where the damage is actually prevented.

Examiner traps

  • “Name the marine fuel standard and its edition.” ISO 8217 — the sixth edition, of 2017, at this sitting.
  • “Which standard tells you whether a marginal result is really off-spec?” ISO 4259 — precision of test methods and interpretation of results.
  • “Where does the sulphur limit come from?” MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14, not ISO 8217. 0.50% globally, 0.10% in emission control areas.
  • “What is the minimum flash point and whose rule is it?” 60 degrees Celsius for fuel oil used in machinery spaces — a SOLAS requirement.
  • “How long do you keep the MARPOL delivered sample and the bunker delivery note?” The sample for at least twelve months; the note for three years.
  • “Your analysis shows high aluminium plus silicon. What is the risk and what do you do first?” Abrasive wear of liners, rings, pumps and injectors. Settle, drain, purify at reduced throughput and correct temperature — and sample at the engine inlet to prove the removal.
  • “High CCAI — what is it telling you?” Poor ignition quality: long ignition delay, rapid pressure rise, knock.
  • “High sodium with high vanadium?” High-temperature corrosion of exhaust valves and turbine nozzle rings — and sodium is often seawater, so purify.
  • “Total sediment high. May you mix it with the fuel already on board?” No — segregate, and do a compatibility spot test before any unavoidable mixing.
  • “The fuel is off-spec. Can you burn it while you argue?” No — burning it destroys the evidence and usually the claim.
  • “Flash point 55 degrees. What do you do?” Do not use, segregate, notify master, owners, flag and supplier, and land it. It is never blended up.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does ISO 8217 stand for? Petroleum products - fuels (class F) - specifications of marine fuels.
  • Which standard classifies and designates the grades? ISO 8216-1.
  • Name four things on a fuel oil analysis report other than the results. Sample and delivery identity with the seal number, the test method for each parameter, the laboratory's comments and recommendations, and the overall verdict with the laboratory's accreditation.
  • What is CCAI and what does it tell you? The calculated carbon aromaticity index, derived from density and viscosity, indicating ignition quality.
  • Why does density matter for purification? Because separation depends on the density difference between oil and water, and a high-density fuel needs the correct gravity disc or interface setting and reduced throughput.
  • What temperature do you purify heavy fuel at, and why not higher? At the recommended separation temperature - high enough for low viscosity, not so high that water flashes off and disturbs the interface.
  • Why sample at the engine inlet? Because the delivery limit for catalytic fines is far above what the engine will tolerate, and only an inlet sample proves the treatment plant is actually working.
  • What is a letter of protest for? To put the supplier on notice within the contractual window and preserve the claim.
  • Who else gets a copy of an off-spec report? Owners, charterers where relevant, the supplier, and the club.
  • What is the difference between the MARPOL sample and the commercial sample? The MARPOL delivered sample is sealed and signed at delivery for statutory verification and retained for at least twelve months; commercial samples are for the ship's own testing.

Memory framework

For the standards, four names and one qualifier: 8217 SPECIFIES, 8216 CLASSIFIES, 4259 DECIDES, 23263 GUIDES — and always say the edition.

For the features, three sentences: GENERAL REQUIREMENTS CLAUSE, SULPHUR DEFERS TO STATUTE, DELIVERY NOT ENGINE INLET.

For the report, six words: WHO, WHAT, DERIVED, COMMENT, VERDICT, LAB.

Before acting, four: IDENTIFY, PRECISION, SEGREGATE, NOTIFY.

For the corrective actions, group them by what they attack: HANDLING (viscosity, density, pour point, water), COMBUSTION (CCAI, carbon residue), WEAR (catalytic fines), CORROSION (vanadium, sodium, acid), STABILITY (sediment, FAME), SAFETY (flash point, hydrogen sulphide), LAW (sulphur). And close on TREAT IT OR REFUSE IT.

Regulation and source map

  • ISO 8217 — the marine fuel specification. Sixth edition, 2017, is the present edition at this sitting. ISO 8216-1 classification and designation; ISO 4259 precision and interpretation of results; ISO/PAS 23263:2019 guidance on 0.50% sulphur fuels; and the individual test method standards.
  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18 — fuel oil availability and quality. The bunker delivery note with the information the Annex prescribes and its three-year retention; the MARPOL delivered sample, sealed and signed, retained for at least twelve months and analysed by the prescribed method; port State inspection of the note and certified copies; and port State follow-up on non-compliant fuel. Cited at identity level; no paragraph is quoted.
  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14 — 0.50% m/m sulphur globally from 1 January 2020, 0.10% m/m in emission control areas.
  • MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76) — in force 1 November 2022. See the uncertainty section on the corpus register's date.
  • SOLAS — the 60 degree Celsius minimum flash point for fuel oil used in machinery spaces. A statutory requirement, not an ISO one.
  • No laboratory, testing service, fuel additive, treatment product or engine maker is named anywhere in this answer, and no limit value is quoted from the specification.

Uncertainty and currency

This question has the sharpest evidence split on the paper: the regulation is well held and the standard cannot be held at all.

MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18 is the best-evidenced provision in this paper. The True Source corpus holds its complete paragraph tree as canonical objects — the availability duty, the whole non-availability evidence regime, the fuel oil quality requirements, the bunker delivery note and its appendix information, the three-year retention, port State inspection, the MARPOL delivered sample and its twelve-month retention and prescribed analysis, and the port State follow-up duties. The standing corpus position is that MARPOL Annex VI resolves to identity and provenance and never to text, so every reference here is made at identity level and no paragraph is quoted.

Two directional corrections were applied to the corpus record. First, the register records the entry into force of the 2021 revised Annex VI a year late; the correct date, 1 November 2022, is used, and an existing correction request is carried rather than re-raised. Second, the resolver annotates the fuel oil quality paragraphs as replaced by an MEPC 81 resolution — that resolution is a 2024 output and is future at this sitting, so the paragraphs are taken in their pre-replacement form and the annotation is not consumed.

No ISO standard is held and none can be. ISO standards are commercially licensed documents. The edition identity, the structure of the specification and the identity of the supporting standards are carried at authoritative-secondary level, and no limit value is presented as a quotation from the specification. Where a figure appears in this answer it is either fixed by statute or convention — the 0.50% and 0.10% sulphur limits, the 60 degree Celsius flash point — or is expressly flagged as engine-maker guidance, as with the engine-inlet limit for abrasive particulate, which is substantially lower than the delivery limit and differs between makers. No grade table is reproduced and no laboratory, additive or treatment product is named.

Currency — and this is the paper's most dangerous single line. The present edition of the marine fuel specification at this sitting is the sixth, of 2017. The seventh edition was published in 2024 and is eight months future. The later member of this family sits at August 2024, after that publication, and is entitled to call the newer edition current. Importing that statement here would put into a candidate's hands an edition that did not exist when he sat the paper. The statement must remain framed as at November 2023.

One further limit: no contractual notice period is stated as a legal requirement. Supply contracts commonly impose a short window for a quality claim, and the answer says the applicable period must be read from the contract rather than assumed, because no supply contract or standard term is held.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 6 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The present standards - and which edition is present
  2. __________Salient features of the specification
  3. __________The content of a fuel oil analysis report
  4. __________Before any corrective action - four steps that come first
  5. __________Corrective action, observation by observation
  6. __________The judgement to close on
Flashcards 11

ISO 8217, Petroleum products - Fuels (class F) - Specifications of marine fuels, in its sixth edition of 2017. Supporting: ISO 8216-1 for classification and designation; ISO 4259 for the precision of test methods and interpretation of results; and ISO/PAS 23263:2019 for guidance on fuels meeting the 0.50% sulphur limit, together with the individual test method standards.

The edition is half the mark, and ISO 4259 is the standard candidates most often omit.

That the fuel shall be a homogeneous blend of hydrocarbons, free from any material at a concentration that causes harm to personnel, jeopardises the safety of the ship, or adversely affects the performance of the machinery. It matters because it catches contamination the numerical table never anticipated - chemical waste, used lubricating oil - and it is the clause a damage claim rests on when the fuel met every tabulated limit.

It is the most important sentence in the standard and the one most often left out of an answer.

The deferral is a feature of the standard and shows the candidate understands how specification and regulation interlock.

Not necessarily. ISO 8217 is a DELIVERY specification. The abrasive particulate concentration permitted at the manifold is substantially higher than engine makers accept at the engine inlet, so the fuel must be settled and purified before it reaches the injector. Closing that gap is the fuel treatment plant's whole purpose.

It is the single most useful structural idea in the question and it explains why the report never tells the whole story.

Sample and delivery identity - ship, port, date, supplier and barge, bunker delivery note reference, grade and quantity ordered, sample seal number, sampling point and sample type. The test method for each parameter alongside the result and the limit. Derived values such as the calculated carbon aromaticity index and estimated net specific energy. The laboratory's comments and treatment recommendations. An overall verdict with a precision statement for marginal results. And laboratory identity, accreditation and dates.

This paper asks for report CONTENT and the later member of this family does not, so an answer inherited from that sitting omits it entirely.

Verify the sample identity - seal number against the bunker delivery note, grade against what was ordered. Apply the ISO 4259 precision, since a result outside a limit by less than the method's reproducibility does not prove an off-specification fuel. Segregate the bunker and do not consume or commingle it. And notify owners, charterers, the supplier and the club inside the contractual window, with a letter of protest, usually leading to joint analysis of the MARPOL delivered sample.

Burning the fuel while the dispute is open destroys both the evidence and the claim - it is the Chief Engineer's judgement mark.

The bunker delivery note must carry the information the Annex prescribes and be retained on board for three years. The MARPOL delivered sample must be sealed and signed and retained for at least twelve months, and analysed by the prescribed method. The port State may inspect the note and take certified copies, and has follow-up duties where non-compliant fuel oil is found.

Three years and twelve months are the two retention numbers examiners ask for, and they are the evidence chain behind any dispute.

Catalytic fines - severe abrasive wear of liners, rings, fuel pumps and injectors, the most destructive fuel fault there is. Settle for as long as possible with frequent tank draining, run the separators at the recommended temperature and reduced throughput in series or parallel, blend down and reduce load. What proves it worked is SAMPLING AT THE ENGINE INLET, not at the manifold - the delivery limit is far above what the engine tolerates.

Engine-inlet monitoring is what separates a real answer from a recital of the delivery limit.

Three. A flash point below the statutory minimum of 60 degrees Celsius - a SOLAS breach and never corrected by blending. Sulphur above the applicable statutory limit where that limit applies. And chemical or used-lubricating-oil contamination, shown by calcium with zinc or phosphorus, which breaches the general requirements clause however the rest of the numbers read. Everything else is a question of treatment, load and monitoring.

Sorting findings into 'treat it' and 'refuse it' is the judgement the whole question is testing.

No. It records a supply failure - that compliant fuel could not be obtained despite best efforts, with the evidence the Party may require. It is not a defence for burning a fuel you already know to be non-compliant. Change over to compliant fuel where the limit applies, notify flag, port State and supplier, record the sequence, and retain the MARPOL delivered sample for verification.

It is the standard misconception about the non-availability regime and it has real detention consequences.

Not necessarily. Every parameter is a measurement and every method has a reproducibility. Under ISO 4259, a result lying outside a limit by less than the method's reproducibility is not proof of an off-specification fuel. Declaring off-spec on a marginal result starts a claim that will fail and may segregate a usable bunker unnecessarily.

ISO 4259 is what actually decides marginal disputes, and naming it is worth a mark on its own.

Quick revision
15-second recall
ISO 8217 — Petroleum products, fuels (class F), specifications of marine fuels. The present edition at this sitting is the SIXTH, of 2017. Supporting: ISO 8216-1 classification, ISO 4259 precision and interpretation (the one that decides marginal disputes), ISO/PAS 23263:2019 on 0.50% sulphur fuels, and the individual test methods. Features: two tables, distillate and residual; the general requirements clause — homogeneous hydrocarbons, free from any material harming personnel, endangering the ship or damaging machinery; sulphur defers to MARPOL (0.50% global, 0.10% ECA); FAME-permitting distillate grades marked with an F; a delivery specification, not an engine-inlet one; flash point 60 °C is SOLAS, not ISO. Report content: sample and delivery identity with seal number; results with limit and test method; derived values — CCAI, net specific energy; comments; verdict with precision; laboratory accreditation. Before acting: identify the sample, apply ISO 4259 precision, segregate and do not burn, notify inside the contractual window — regulation 18 gives the BDN (3 years) and the MARPOL delivered sample (12 months minimum). Corrective action by group: handling — heat or cool to injection viscosity, gravity disc and reduced throughput for high density, drain and settle for water, heat above pour point; combustion — high CCAI means raise temperatures, retard timing, reduce load, blend; wear — catalytic fines: settle, drain, purify at temperature and low throughput, sample at the engine inlet; corrosion — vanadium and sodium: purify, valve rotation, shorter intervals; stability — sediment: segregate, compatibility test, no commingling; safety — H2S is a personnel hazard. Refuse, do not treat: flash point below 60 °C, sulphur above the limit, used-lube or chemical contamination.

Critical numbers
  • ISO 8217 - the SIXTH edition, of 2017, is the present edition at this November 2023 sitting
  • MARPOL Annex VI regulation 14 - 0.50% m/m sulphur globally from 1 January 2020; 0.10% m/m in emission control areas
  • Minimum flash point 60 degrees Celsius for fuel oil used in machinery spaces - a SOLAS requirement
  • Bunker delivery note retained on board for THREE YEARS
  • MARPOL delivered sample retained for at least TWELVE MONTHS
  • MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76) - in force 1 November 2022
  • The abrasive particulate concentration permitted at DELIVERY is substantially higher than engine makers accept at the ENGINE INLET - maker guidance, not specification
Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18 is the regulatory scaffolding behind every step of the response to an adverse report: it prescribes the bunker delivery note and the information it must carry, requires it to be retained on board for three years, requires the MARPOL delivered sample to be sealed and signed and retained for at least twelve months and analysed by the prescribed method, gives the port State the right to inspect the note and take certified copies, and imposes follow-up duties where non-compliant fuel oil is found. Read with regulation 14, which supplies the sulphur limits the specification itself defers to. Annex VI at this sitting is the MEPC.328(76) revision, in force 1 November 2022.
Major trap
Naming the standard without its edition — and, worse on this paper, naming the wrong one. At November 2023 the present edition is the sixth, of 2017; the seventh edition was published in 2024 and did not exist when this paper was sat. The second trap is starting corrective action before the governance steps: verify the sample identity, apply the ISO 4259 precision, segregate and do not burn the fuel, and notify inside the contractual window. Burning a disputed bunker destroys both the evidence and the claim. The third is failing to separate the findings that are treated from the three that are refused — a flash point below 60 °C, sulphur above the applicable statutory limit, and used-lubricating-oil or chemical contamination.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q2 - fuel consumption and engine performance, where a fuel's calorific value and viscosity are actually paid for · QP2311 Q6 - the planned maintenance system, which is where a fuel-driven change to purifier settings or overhaul intervals has to be recorded

First in set First set here. The same task returns at August 2024 Q6.

Understand this first

The idea behind this question is a mismatch that lasted for years. Ships were designed and their engines built to run at one speed, and then the industry decided — first to save money, later because the rules demanded it — to run them much slower. Slowing down saves a great deal of fuel, because the water resists a propeller far harder the faster it turns. But an engine is a matched set of parts: the turbocharger, the injection timing, the compression, the lubrication and the propeller were all chosen together for one duty, and taking the speed away without changing anything else breaks the match. The first thing to fail is the air supply, because the turbocharger is driven by exhaust the engine is no longer producing enough of, and everything else follows from that — poor burning, soot everywhere, cold surfaces that let acid form and eat the liners, fires in the scavenge spaces and in the exhaust boiler. So the retrofits are not a list of clever products; each one is an answer to one of those failures, and they group naturally: get the air back, get the compression and timing back, protect the liners, re-match the propeller, and deal with the heat that is no longer there. The last of those catches people out — a slower ship makes less waste steam, so the auxiliary boiler starts burning fuel and quietly gives back part of the saving. What makes this a question of its moment rather than a general one is that by now the slow speed is often not a choice: the ship's installed power has been deliberately capped to meet an efficiency requirement, which means she will spend the rest of her life at a load her engine was never designed for.

Knowledge map
Marine Diesel Engine Developments and Retrofits for Slow and Ultra-Slow Steaming
  1. 1Why the question exists - and why it is regulatory by this sitting
    • slow steaming began as a COMMERCIAL response - with a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged conditions, delivered power varies roughly as the CUBE of shaft speed
    • so a modest speed reduction removes a large amount of fuel, and ships built for one speed were run for years at another
    • by this sitting it is no longer only commercial
  2. 2Developments in marine diesel engines
    • GEOMETRY - long-stroke and ultra-long-stroke designs at high stroke-to-bore ratio, reaching the design mechanical duty at lower shaft speed and permitting a larger, slower, more efficient propeller
    • CONTROL - electronically controlled, camshaft-less engines with common rail or servo-actuated injection and electronically actuated exhaust valves
    • THIS IS THE SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT DEVELOPMENT FOR THIS QUESTION - injection timing, rate and profile and valve timing become SOFTWARE, so the engine can be re-optimised without new hardware
  3. 3What actually goes wrong at prolonged low load
    • NOT ENOUGH AIR - the turbocharger is off its matched point, scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio fall, and auxiliary blowers run for long periods; THIS IS THE ROOT FAILURE
    • LOW COMPRESSION PRESSURE AND TEMPERATURE, lengthening ignition delay and worsening combustion
    • POOR ATOMISATION at low injection pressure, giving incomplete combustion, smoke and unburnt fuel
  4. 4Air-side retrofits - the first resort
    • TURBOCHARGER CUT-OUT - on an engine with two or more turbochargers, one is blanked off so the rest work near their design point and scavenge pressure is restored
    • the cheapest effective measure on a multi-turbocharger engine; needs a cut-out kit with blanking and isolating arrangements, and is reversible for high-load running
    • VARIABLE TURBINE AREA - an adjustable nozzle ring changes the turbine's effective area with load, converting a fixed match into a variable one
  5. 5Combustion-side retrofits
    • LOW-LOAD OR PART-LOAD TUNING - on an electronically controlled engine, a software package re-optimises injection timing, rate and profile and exhaust valve timing for the load range actually used
    • no hardware changes, normally reversible - the least disruptive retrofit available
    • VARIABLE INJECTION TIMING ADJUSTMENT - on a mechanically controlled engine, re-setting the VIT and where necessary re-timing the fuel cams
  6. 6Lubrication and wear retrofits
    • ELECTRONICALLY CONTROLLED TIMED CYLINDER LUBRICATION replacing mechanical lubricators - feed proportional to load and fuel sulphur, injection timed to the ring pack
    • feed can therefore be reduced with load WITHOUT LOSING THE NEUTRALISING DUTY; on a slow-steaming ship this is what stands between low load and corrosive liner wear
    • CYLINDER OIL BASE NUMBER MATCHED to the fuel and the load - too low invites cold corrosion, too high leaves deposits
  7. 7Propulsion-train and heat-balance retrofits
    • the engine is not the only thing matched to the old speed
    • PROPELLER RE-PITCHING OR REPLACEMENT - a new propeller optimised for the speed and power now used, often larger in diameter and lower in revolutions; the largest single efficiency gain on a genuinely re-purposed ship
    • ENERGY-SAVING DEVICES - pre-swirl ducts and stators, boss cap fins, rudder bulbs and twisted rudders, recovering swirl energy and mattering more at the lower wake speeds of slow steaming
  8. 8The regulatory retrofit unique to this period
    • ENGINE POWER LIMITATION AND SHAFT POWER LIMITATION - for much of the existing fleet the practical EEXI compliance route was not to change the ship but to LIMIT THE POWER THAT MAY BE DELIVERED
    • engine power limitation is applied to the engine's own control or fuel index; shaft power limitation through shaft power measurement and a limiting control
    • the limitation must be VERIFIABLE and sealed or otherwise secured, with its arrangement and setting recorded in the ship's technical file and onboard management manual
  9. 9Operational measures, and the judgement
    • PERIODIC LOAD-UP RUNS to burn off deposits and dry out gas ways
    • INCREASED TURBOCHARGER, GAS-WAY AND ECONOMISER CLEANING, with soot blowing and water washing on a revised schedule
    • RAISED JACKET COOLING WATER TEMPERATURE where the maker permits, to keep liner metal temperatures up and cold corrosion down
