Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 73 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Why the question exists - and why it is regulatory by this sitting
  • slow steaming began as a COMMERCIAL response - with a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged conditions, delivered power varies roughly as the CUBE of shaft speed
  • so a modest speed reduction removes a large amount of fuel, and ships built for one speed were run for years at another
  • by this sitting it is no longer only commercial
  • MARPOL Annex VI as revised is IN FORCE SINCE 1 NOVEMBER 2022
  • EEXI and the CII rating regime have applied since 1 JANUARY 2023 - eleven months
  • EEXI compliance is verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date
  • for much of the existing fleet the practical route to compliance was to LIMIT THE INSTALLED POWER
  • the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy was adopted 7 July 2023, four months before this examination, and sets the direction beyond EEXI
  • so the engineering question is precise - AN ENGINE DESIGNED TO RUN NEAR ITS RATED DUTY IS NOW REQUIRED TO SPEND ITS LIFE A LONG WAY BELOW IT
2. Developments in marine diesel engines
  • GEOMETRY - long-stroke and ultra-long-stroke designs at high stroke-to-bore ratio, reaching the design mechanical duty at lower shaft speed and permitting a larger, slower, more efficient propeller
  • CONTROL - electronically controlled, camshaft-less engines with common rail or servo-actuated injection and electronically actuated exhaust valves
  • THIS IS THE SINGLE MOST IMPORTANT DEVELOPMENT FOR THIS QUESTION - injection timing, rate and profile and valve timing become SOFTWARE, so the engine can be re-optimised without new hardware
  • AIR SIDE - high-efficiency and two-stage turbocharging, variable turbine area, exhaust bypass, power turbines and waste heat recovery; air supply becomes adjustable rather than fixed at one match
  • EMISSIONS - primary measures including injection and valve timing, scavenge air moisturising and exhaust gas recirculation, and secondary measures including selective catalytic reduction for Tier III
  • kept SHORT here because it is a large subject of its own; it matters only because after-treatment has a minimum temperature that very low load can fall below
  • LUBRICATION - electronically controlled timed cylinder lubrication with feed proportional to load and fuel sulphur, and anti-polishing rings
  • FUELS - dual-fuel operation on gas and methanol in service; AMMONIA AND HYDROGEN UNDER DEVELOPMENT AND NOT IN COMMERCIAL SERVICE at this sitting, and AMMONIA IS NOT ZERO-EMISSION AT THE FUNNEL - unburnt ammonia slip and nitrous oxide, a far more potent greenhouse gas than carbon dioxide, both require after-treatment
  • DIGITAL - continuous performance monitoring, electronic indication, remote diagnostics and predictive maintenance, which make a retrofit's effect measurable
  • THE DEVELOPMENT THAT CHANGED RETROFITTING - on a mechanical engine timing lives in a camshaft and changing it is a hardware job; on an electronic engine it lives in a control system
3. What actually goes wrong at prolonged low load
  • NOT ENOUGH AIR - the turbocharger is off its matched point, scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio fall, and auxiliary blowers run for long periods; THIS IS THE ROOT FAILURE
  • LOW COMPRESSION PRESSURE AND TEMPERATURE, lengthening ignition delay and worsening combustion
  • POOR ATOMISATION at low injection pressure, giving incomplete combustion, smoke and unburnt fuel
  • DEPOSITS on rings and ring grooves, in scavenge spaces, gas ways, turbine and exhaust gas economiser - hence SCAVENGE FIRES AND ECONOMISER SOOT FIRES, the real safety consequence
  • COLD CORROSION - sulphuric acid condensing on the liner where metal temperatures fall, if cylinder oil base number and feed rate are not matched to load and fuel
  • TURBOCHARGER SURGING and unstable running near the barred speed range
  • FALLING WASTE HEAT - lower exhaust temperature and mass flow, less steam, and the auxiliary boiler firing to replace the heat the main engine is no longer wasting
  • PROPELLER MISMATCH - the propeller was designed for a speed the ship no longer runs at
  • RISING SFOC - more fuel per kilowatt precisely when the point was to burn less
4. Air-side retrofits - the first resort
  • TURBOCHARGER CUT-OUT - on an engine with two or more turbochargers, one is blanked off so the rest work near their design point and scavenge pressure is restored
  • the cheapest effective measure on a multi-turbocharger engine; needs a cut-out kit with blanking and isolating arrangements, and is reversible for high-load running
  • VARIABLE TURBINE AREA - an adjustable nozzle ring changes the turbine's effective area with load, converting a fixed match into a variable one
  • NOZZLE RING EXCHANGE OR TURBOCHARGER RE-MATCHING - where the low-load duty is permanent, a smaller nozzle ring or a smaller or higher-efficiency turbocharger permanently re-matches the air side
  • EXHAUST GAS BYPASS AND WASTE-GATE RE-SETTING, so the bypass no longer robs the turbine of energy it now needs
  • AUXILIARY BLOWER CONTROL - revised cut-in and cut-out logic and, where fitted, frequency-controlled blowers running continuously at reduced speed rather than cycling
5. Combustion-side retrofits
  • LOW-LOAD OR PART-LOAD TUNING - on an electronically controlled engine, a software package re-optimises injection timing, rate and profile and exhaust valve timing for the load range actually used
  • no hardware changes, normally reversible - the least disruptive retrofit available
  • VARIABLE INJECTION TIMING ADJUSTMENT - on a mechanically controlled engine, re-setting the VIT and where necessary re-timing the fuel cams
  • COMPRESSION RATIO INCREASE - by shims or a modified piston crown, restoring compression pressure and temperature at low load and shortening ignition delay; must be checked against the maximum pressure limit
  • SLIDE-TYPE FUEL VALVES in place of conventional valves, eliminating the sac volume and the after-dripping that causes smoke and deposits at low load
  • FULL DE-RATING - the permanent option: re-tuning to a LOWER RATED OUTPUT, typically combining new injection timing, a compression ratio change, a new turbocharger nozzle ring and sometimes new fuel valve nozzles
  • the engine is then genuinely designed for the duty it performs and its specific consumption at the new operating point improves; the most thorough answer and the least reversible
6. Lubrication and wear retrofits
  • ELECTRONICALLY CONTROLLED TIMED CYLINDER LUBRICATION replacing mechanical lubricators - feed proportional to load and fuel sulphur, injection timed to the ring pack
  • feed can therefore be reduced with load WITHOUT LOSING THE NEUTRALISING DUTY; on a slow-steaming ship this is what stands between low load and corrosive liner wear
  • CYLINDER OIL BASE NUMBER MATCHED to the fuel and the load - too low invites cold corrosion, too high leaves deposits
  • ANTI-POLISHING RINGS at the top of the liner, to scrape the deposit collar off the piston crown and control bore polishing
  • REVISED SCAVENGE SPACE INSPECTION AND DRAIN MONITORING, with drain oil analysis to see wear and residual base number before they become damage
  • this is a PMS CHANGE and belongs in the maintenance system, not in somebody's notebook
7. Propulsion-train and heat-balance retrofits
  • the engine is not the only thing matched to the old speed
  • PROPELLER RE-PITCHING OR REPLACEMENT - a new propeller optimised for the speed and power now used, often larger in diameter and lower in revolutions; the largest single efficiency gain on a genuinely re-purposed ship
  • ENERGY-SAVING DEVICES - pre-swirl ducts and stators, boss cap fins, rudder bulbs and twisted rudders, recovering swirl energy and mattering more at the lower wake speeds of slow steaming
  • HULL COATING AND CLEANING REGIME - a slower ship gives a foul release coating less shear to work with, so the coating and cleaning policy must be revisited with the speed
  • TORSIONAL VIBRATION RE-ASSESSMENT - changing propeller, pitch or engine rating MOVES THE BARRED SPEED RANGE, and the new range must be established and observed; a safety item, not an optimisation
  • EXHAUST GAS ECONOMISER - lower exhaust mass flow and temperature mean less steam and more soot
  • retrofits are soot blowers, water-washing arrangements, dry-running protection and revised circulation control, and the economiser fire risk rises sharply
  • AUXILIARY BOILER - it now fires much more often, so burner control, maintenance and fuel consumption all move, and PART OF THE MAIN ENGINE SAVING IS QUIETLY GIVEN BACK
  • WASTE HEAT RECOVERY - systems sized for full load produce much less at low load; bypass and control changes may be needed and a power turbine may cease to be usable below a certain output
8. The regulatory retrofit unique to this period
  • ENGINE POWER LIMITATION AND SHAFT POWER LIMITATION - for much of the existing fleet the practical EEXI compliance route was not to change the ship but to LIMIT THE POWER THAT MAY BE DELIVERED
  • engine power limitation is applied to the engine's own control or fuel index; shaft power limitation through shaft power measurement and a limiting control
  • the limitation must be VERIFIABLE and sealed or otherwise secured, with its arrangement and setting recorded in the ship's technical file and onboard management manual
  • it must be capable of being OVERRIDDEN TO RELEASE A POWER RESERVE where the safety of the ship or the saving of life at sea requires, with the override recorded and reported
  • a limitation that could not be overridden would itself be a hazard
  • the arrangements follow the Organization's guidelines on power limitation systems and the use of a power reserve
  • THE ENGINEERING CONSEQUENCE IS THE WHOLE POINT OF THIS QUESTION - a power-limited ship will spend her remaining life at a load her engine was not designed for, so POWER LIMITATION MAKES THE LOW-LOAD RETROFITS NECESSARY RATHER THAN OPTIONAL
9. Operational measures, and the judgement
  • PERIODIC LOAD-UP RUNS to burn off deposits and dry out gas ways
  • INCREASED TURBOCHARGER, GAS-WAY AND ECONOMISER CLEANING, with soot blowing and water washing on a revised schedule
  • RAISED JACKET COOLING WATER TEMPERATURE where the maker permits, to keep liner metal temperatures up and cold corrosion down
  • MORE FREQUENT SCAVENGE SPACE INSPECTION, and drain oil analysis trended rather than filed
  • PERFORMANCE MONITORING AGAINST A RE-ESTABLISHED BASELINE after every change, because a retrofit whose effect is not measured cannot be defended
  • REVISED PMS INTERVALS recorded in the maintenance system, and the whole envelope recorded in the SEEMP
  • THE JUDGEMENT - an electronically controlled engine with more than one turbocharger is the easy case: low-load tuning plus turbocharger cut-out, both reversible, cheap and quick
  • a mechanically controlled engine needs hardware, and the case must be made on the remaining life of the ship
  • a ship whose speed reduction is permanent, or which is power-limited for EEXI, should be DE-RATED PROPERLY AND THE PROPELLER RE-MATCHED, because half-measures leave it running badly for years
  • EVERY CASE NEEDS THE LUBRICATION AND INSPECTION REGIME CHANGED, whatever else is done - the cheapest measure and the one that decides whether the liners survive
Model written answer

1. Why the question exists - and why it is regulatory by this sitting

Slow steaming began as a commercial response: with a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged conditions, delivered power varies roughly as the cube of shaft speed, so a modest speed reduction removes a large amount of fuel. Ships built for one speed were then run for years at another.

By this sitting it is no longer only commercial. MARPOL Annex VI as revised is in force since 1 November 2022, and the EEXI requirement and the CII rating regime have applied since 1 January 2023 — eleven months. EEXI compliance is verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date, and for a large part of the existing fleet the practical route to compliance was to limit the installed power. The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, adopted four months before this examination, sets the direction beyond that.

So the engineering question is precise: an engine designed to run near its rated duty is now required to spend its life a long way below it. Everything below is an answer to that.

2. Developments in marine diesel engines

AreaDevelopmentWhy it matters here
GeometryLong-stroke and ultra-long-stroke designs at high stroke-to-bore ratioThe design mechanical duty is reached at lower shaft speed, which permits a larger, slower, more efficient propeller and gives more time per cycle for combustion
ControlElectronically controlled, camshaft-less engines — common rail or servo-actuated injection and electronically actuated exhaust valvesThe single most important development for this question. Injection timing, rate and profile and valve timing become software, so the engine can be re-optimised for a different operating range without new hardware
Air sideHigh-efficiency and two-stage turbocharging, variable turbine area, exhaust bypass, power turbines and waste heat recoveryAir supply stops being fixed at one matched point and becomes adjustable across the load range
EmissionsPrimary measures — injection and valve timing, scavenge air moisturising, exhaust gas recirculation; and secondary — selective catalytic reduction for Tier IIIKept short here: it is a large subject of its own. It matters because after-treatment has its own minimum temperature, and very low load can take an engine outside it
LubricationElectronically controlled timed cylinder lubrication, feed proportional to load and fuel sulphur; anti-polishing ringsDirectly enables low load: feed rate can be cut with load without losing the neutralising and film-forming duty
FuelsDual-fuel operation on gas and on methanol, in service; ammonia and hydrogen under development and not in commercial service at this sittingChanges the combustion problem rather than solving the low-load one — and ammonia is not zero-emission at the funnel: unburnt ammonia slip and nitrous oxide, a far more potent greenhouse gas than carbon dioxide, both require after-treatment
DigitalContinuous performance monitoring, electronic indication, remote diagnostics and predictive maintenanceMakes the effect of a retrofit measurable, which is what allows an interval or a setting to be changed on evidence

The development that changed retrofitting. On a mechanically controlled engine, timing lives in a camshaft and changing it means cams, links and hardware. On an electronically controlled engine it lives in a control system. That is why modern low-load work is often a tuning package rather than a shipyard job, and why the retrofit menu below divides sharply by which kind of engine is in the ship.

3. What actually goes wrong at prolonged low load

Every retrofit that follows answers one of these, so it is worth stating them first:

  • Not enough air. The turbocharger is off its matched point, so scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio fall and the auxiliary blowers run for long periods. This is the root failure and most retrofits attack it.
  • Low compression pressure and temperature, which lengthens ignition delay and worsens combustion.
  • Poor atomisation at low injection pressure, giving incomplete combustion, smoke and unburnt fuel.
  • Deposits — on rings and ring grooves, in the scavenge spaces, in the gas ways, on the turbine and in the exhaust gas economiser. Hence scavenge fires and economiser soot fires, which are the real safety consequence.
  • Cold corrosion. Where metal temperatures fall, sulphuric acid condenses on the liner surface; if the cylinder oil base number and feed rate are not matched to the load and the fuel, corrosive wear follows.
  • Turbocharger surging, and unstable running near the barred speed range.
  • Falling waste heat. Exhaust temperature and mass flow drop, so the economiser makes less steam and the auxiliary boiler has to fire — burning fuel to replace the heat the main engine is no longer wasting.
  • Propeller mismatch. The propeller was designed for a speed the ship no longer runs at, so it is now working off its best efficiency point.
  • Rising SFOC. The engine burns more fuel per kilowatt precisely when the point of the exercise was to burn less.

4. Air-side retrofits - the first resort

  1. Turbocharger cut-out. On an engine with two or more turbochargers, one is blanked off so that the remaining units work near their design point and scavenge pressure is restored at low load. It is the cheapest effective measure on a multi-turbocharger engine, requiring a cut-out kit with blanking and isolating arrangements, and it is reversible for high-load running.
  2. Variable turbine area. An adjustable nozzle ring changes the turbine's effective area with load, so the turbocharger stays near its match across a wide range instead of at one point. It converts a fixed match into a variable one.
  3. Nozzle ring exchange, or turbocharger re-matching. Where the low-load duty is permanent, a smaller nozzle ring or a smaller or higher-efficiency turbocharger is fitted, permanently re-matching the air side.
  4. Exhaust gas bypass and waste-gate re-setting. Adjusted so that the bypass no longer robs the turbine of energy it now needs.
  5. Auxiliary blower control. Revised cut-in and cut-out logic and, where fitted, frequency-controlled blowers that run continuously at reduced speed rather than cycling. Continuous cycling is hard on the machinery and on the electrical plant.

5. Combustion-side retrofits

  1. Low-load or part-load tuning. On an electronically controlled engine, a software package re-optimises injection timing, rate and profile and exhaust valve timing for the load range actually used. No hardware changes, and it is normally reversible — the least disruptive retrofit available.
  2. Variable injection timing adjustment. On a mechanically controlled engine, the equivalent is re-setting the variable injection timing and, where necessary, re-timing the fuel cams.
  3. Compression ratio increase. Raising the compression ratio — by shims or by a modified piston crown — restores compression pressure and temperature at low load, shortening ignition delay and improving combustion. This is real hardware and has to be checked against the maximum pressure limit.
  4. Slide-type fuel valves in place of conventional valves. Eliminating the sac volume removes after-dripping, which is a principal source of smoke and deposits at low load.
  5. Full de-rating. The permanent option: the engine is re-tuned to a lower rated output, typically combining new injection timing, a compression ratio change, a new turbocharger nozzle ring and sometimes new fuel valve nozzles. The engine is then genuinely designed for the duty it performs, and its specific consumption at the new operating point improves. It is the most thorough answer and the least reversible.

6. Lubrication and wear retrofits

  • Electronically controlled timed cylinder lubrication, replacing mechanical lubricators. Feed rate becomes proportional to load and to fuel sulphur, and injection is timed to the ring pack, so feed can be reduced with load without losing the neutralising duty. On a slow-steaming ship this is what stands between low load and corrosive liner wear.
  • Cylinder oil base number matched to the fuel and the load — too low invites cold corrosion, too high leaves deposits.
  • Anti-polishing rings at the top of the liner, to scrape the deposit collar off the piston crown and control bore polishing.
  • Revised scavenge space inspection and drain monitoring, and drain oil analysis to see wear and residual base number before they become damage. This is a PMS change and belongs in the maintenance system, not in somebody's notebook.

7. Propulsion-train and heat-balance retrofits

The engine is not the only thing matched to the old speed:

  • Propeller re-pitching or replacement — a new propeller optimised for the speed and power the ship now uses, often of larger diameter and lower revolutions, which is the largest single efficiency gain available on a genuinely re-purposed ship.
  • Energy-saving devices — pre-swirl ducts and stators, boss cap fins, rudder bulbs and twisted rudders, all of which recover swirl energy and matter more at the lower wake speeds of slow steaming.
  • Hull coating and cleaning regime — a slower ship gives a foul release coating less shear to work with, so the coating and cleaning policy must be revisited with the speed.
  • Torsional vibration re-assessment. Changing propeller, pitch or engine rating moves the barred speed range, and the new range must be established and observed. This is a safety item, not an optimisation.

And the heat balance changes with the speed — the most commonly forgotten consequence of all:

  • Exhaust gas economiser. Lower exhaust mass flow and temperature mean less steam and more soot. Retrofits are soot blowers, water-washing arrangements, dry-running protection and revised circulation control — and the economiser fire risk rises sharply, so the cleaning regime changes with the operating profile.
  • Auxiliary boiler. It now fires much more often, so its burner control, maintenance and fuel consumption all move — and part of the main engine saving is quietly given back here.
  • Waste heat recovery. Systems sized for full load produce much less at low load; bypass and control changes may be needed, and a power turbine may cease to be usable at all below a certain output.

8. The regulatory retrofit unique to this period

Engine power limitation and shaft power limitation. For a large part of the existing fleet, the practical EEXI compliance route was not to change the ship but to limit the power that may be delivered, so that the attained index falls to the required value.

  • Engine power limitation is applied to the engine's own control or fuel index; shaft power limitation is applied through shaft power measurement and a limiting control.
  • The limitation must be verifiable and sealed or otherwise secured, and its arrangement and setting recorded in the ship's technical file and onboard management manual.
  • It must be capable of being overridden to release a power reserve where the safety of the ship or the saving of life at sea requires it — with the override recorded and reported. A limitation that could not be overridden would itself be a hazard.
  • The arrangements follow the Organization's guidelines on power limitation systems and the use of a power reserve.

The engineering consequence is the whole point of this question: a power-limited ship is a ship that will spend its remaining life at a load its engine was not designed for. Power limitation makes the low-load retrofits necessary rather than optional.

9. Operational measures, and the judgement

No retrofit works on its own. The supporting practice is part of the answer:

  • Periodic load-up runs to burn off deposits and dry out gas ways.
  • Increased turbocharger, gas-way and economiser cleaning, with soot blowing and water washing on a revised schedule.
  • Raised jacket cooling water temperature where the maker permits, to keep liner metal temperatures up and cold corrosion down.
  • More frequent scavenge space inspection, and drain oil analysis trended rather than filed.
  • Performance monitoring against a re-established baseline after every change, because a retrofit whose effect is not measured cannot be defended.
  • Revised PMS intervals recorded in the maintenance system, and the whole envelope recorded in the SEEMP.

The judgement to close on. There is no single answer, and the mark is for saying which ship needs what:

  1. An electronically controlled engine with more than one turbocharger is the easy case: low-load tuning plus turbocharger cut-out, both reversible, both cheap, both quick.
  2. A mechanically controlled engine needs hardware — timing changes, compression ratio, slide fuel valves, nozzle ring — and the case has to be made on the remaining life of the ship.
  3. A ship whose speed reduction is permanent, or which is power-limited for EEXI, should be de-rated properly and the propeller re-matched, because half-measures leave it running badly for years.
  4. Every case needs the lubrication and inspection regime changed, whatever else is or is not done. That is the cheapest measure and the one that decides whether the liners survive.

“Hassle free” slow steaming, in the phrase the paper uses, is therefore not a single product. It is an engine given enough air, a combustion process given back its compression and timing, a cylinder oil regime matched to the new duty, a propeller matched to the new speed, and a heat balance that has been re-thought — and the ships that got it wrong are the ones that found out through scavenge fires, economiser fires and liner wear.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Sixteen marks in one unlimbed block, with the word “including” doing the apportioning: developments in marine diesel engines generally, including the retrofits for slow and ultra-slow steaming. That makes the retrofits the larger task, and roughly a third of the page belongs to the developments.

The structural decision that earns the most is to insert a section on what actually goes wrong at low load between the two. Without it, the retrofit list is a catalogue and reads like one. With it, every retrofit is visibly an answer to a named failure — not enough air, lost compression, deposits, cold corrosion, lost waste heat, propeller mismatch — and the answer acquires an argument.

The second decision is to keep the emissions material short. Tier III, SCR, EGR and scavenge air moisturising are a whole question of their own and appear elsewhere in the corpus; here they are one row of a table, mentioned only because after-treatment has a minimum temperature that very low load can fall below.

The one development that explains the whole retrofit menu

Electronic control. On a mechanically controlled engine, injection and valve timing live in a camshaft, so changing them is a hardware job. On an electronically controlled engine they live in software.

That single difference splits the retrofit menu in two and is the basis of the judgement at the end of the answer. An electronically controlled engine can often be re-optimised for a new load range with a tuning package and a turbocharger cut-out — reversible, cheap, quick. A mechanically controlled engine needs timing changes, a compression ratio change, slide fuel valves and a nozzle ring, and the business case has to be made against the ship's remaining life.

The failures, and which retrofit answers which

Failure at low loadRetrofit that answers it
Not enough air - turbocharger off its match, blowers running continuouslyTurbocharger cut-out; variable turbine area; nozzle ring exchange or turbocharger re-match; bypass re-setting; blower control
Low compression pressure and temperatureCompression ratio increase; raised jacket water temperature
Poor atomisation, smoke, depositsLow-load tuning or variable injection timing adjustment; slide-type fuel valves
Cold corrosion and liner wearElectronically controlled timed cylinder lubrication; base number matching; anti-polishing rings
Scavenge and economiser firesCleaning regime, soot blowing and water washing; load-up runs; inspection frequency
Propeller off its design pointRe-pitch or replace the propeller; energy-saving devices; re-assess the barred speed range
Lost waste heatEconomiser control and protection; auxiliary boiler; waste heat recovery bypass
Permanent mismatchFull de-rating to a lower rated output

What is unique about a 2023 answer

This question would have been answered differently in 2015. By November 2023, slow steaming has become regulatory, and three dates carry the change:

  • MARPOL Annex VI as revised is in force from 1 November 2022 — twelve months before the sitting.
  • EEXI and CII apply from 1 January 2023 — eleven months. EEXI is verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date, and for much of the existing fleet the compliance route was power limitation. 2023 is the first CII data year and it is still running at this sitting.
  • The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy was adopted on 7 July 2023 — four months before, so it is operative here and sets the direction beyond EEXI.

The connecting sentence is the one worth learning: power limitation makes the low-load retrofits necessary rather than optional, because a power-limited ship will spend its remaining life at a load its engine was not designed for.

Common mistakes

  • Writing a catalogue of retrofits with no statement of what problem each solves.
  • Spending the page on emissions technology — SCR, EGR, Tier III — which is a different question.
  • Ignoring the word “developments” and answering only on retrofits, or the reverse.
  • Treating slow steaming as purely commercial in 2023 and never mentioning EEXI, CII or power limitation.
  • Forgetting the air side is the root failure. Most low-load trouble is insufficient scavenge air.
  • Omitting cylinder lubrication, which is what actually decides whether the liners survive years of low load.
  • Omitting cold corrosion, or confusing it with hot corrosion from vanadium and sodium.
  • Forgetting the heat balance — less waste heat, more auxiliary boiler firing, and part of the saving quietly given back.
  • Forgetting the propeller, which was matched to a speed the ship no longer uses.
  • Not mentioning that changing propeller, pitch or rating moves the barred speed range.
  • Naming maker products and tuning packages. Describe the mechanism — it is what earns the mark and it does not date.
  • Quoting percentage savings and de-rating figures. They are ship-specific and unsupportable.

Examiner traps

  • “What is the first thing that goes wrong at low load?” Insufficient scavenge air — the turbocharger is off its matched point.
  • “What is turbocharger cut-out and when can you use it?” Blanking off one turbocharger on a multi-turbocharger engine so the rest work near their design point. Only where there is more than one, and it is reversible.
  • “What does variable turbine area do?” Changes the turbine's effective area with load, so the match follows the load instead of being fixed at one point.
  • “Why fit slide fuel valves?” They eliminate the sac volume and so the after-dripping that causes smoke and deposits at low load.
  • “Why raise the compression ratio?” To restore compression pressure and temperature at low load, shortening ignition delay — and it must be checked against the maximum pressure limit.
  • “What is cold corrosion and what do you do about it?” Sulphuric acid condensing on the liner when metal temperatures are low. Match cylinder oil base number and feed rate to load and fuel, raise jacket water temperature where permitted, and monitor drain oil.
  • “Why do economiser fires become more likely?” Lower exhaust temperature and mass flow mean soot deposits and dry surfaces — hence soot blowing, water washing and dry-running protection.
  • “What happens to your steam?” Less of it. The auxiliary boiler fires more, and part of the main engine saving is given back.
  • “What is EPL and can it be overridden?” Engine power limitation for EEXI compliance — verifiable, secured, and yes: it must be capable of override to release a power reserve where safety or saving life at sea requires, with the override recorded and reported.
  • “You re-pitch the propeller. What must be re-checked?” The barred speed range, because it moves.
  • “Which is the cheapest measure of all?” Changing the cylinder lubrication and inspection regime — and it is the one that decides whether the liners survive.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Why does slow steaming save so much fuel? Because with a fixed-pitch propeller in unchanged conditions, power varies roughly as the cube of shaft speed.
  • Then why not go slower still? Because specific fuel consumption rises off the matched point, and deposits, fouling and maintenance costs rise with it.
  • What is de-rating? Permanently re-tuning the engine to a lower rated output, usually with new injection timing, a compression ratio change and a new turbocharger nozzle ring.
  • What is the difference between de-rating and power limitation? De-rating changes the engine; power limitation restricts the power that may be delivered, for EEXI compliance, and can be overridden for safety.
  • Which retrofit needs no hardware? Low-load tuning on an electronically controlled engine.
  • Why do auxiliary blowers matter? Because at low load they run for long periods, and continuous cycling is hard on the machinery and the electrical plant.
  • What does an anti-polishing ring do? Scrapes the deposit collar from the piston crown and controls bore polishing.
  • What do you monitor to see whether the lubrication regime is right? Drain oil analysis - wear metals and residual base number - and scavenge space condition.
  • Are ammonia or hydrogen engines in service? Not at this time; they are under development.
  • Where is the whole operating envelope recorded? In the SEEMP.

Memory framework

Two halves: DEVELOPMENTS then RETROFITS, with WHAT GOES WRONG between them as the hinge.

For the developments, six words: STROKE, ELECTRONICS, AIR, EMISSIONS, LUBRICATION, FUELS — and one sentence: electronics turned timing from a camshaft into software.

For what goes wrong, five words in the order they bite: AIR, COMPRESSION, DEPOSITS, CORROSION, HEAT.

For the retrofits, the same five plus two: AIR (cut-out, variable turbine area, nozzle ring, blowers), COMBUSTION (tuning, timing, compression ratio, slide valves, de-rating), WEAR (timed lubrication, base number, anti-polishing ring), PROPELLER (re-pitch, energy-saving devices, barred range), HEAT (economiser, boiler, waste heat recovery) — and LAW (engine or shaft power limitation).

Regulation and source map

  • MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76) — in force 1 November 2022. See the uncertainty section on the corpus register's date.
  • EEXI and the CII rating regime — applicable from 1 January 2023. EEXI verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date. 2023 is the first CII data year and it is still running at this sitting.
  • SEEMP with Part III mandatory from 1 January 2023 — where the operating envelope and improvement measures are recorded. The operative guidance is the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.346(78); the 2024 successor is future and is not cited.
  • MEPC.377(80), the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, adopted 7 July 2023 — four months before this sitting, therefore operative. A strategy, not law.
  • Engine or shaft power limitation for EEXI compliance, with override to release a power reserve, following the Organization's guidelines. The guidelines are referred to descriptively and not by resolution number; see the uncertainty section.
  • No engine maker, engine type, tuning package, retrofit kit, turbocharger model or proprietary device is named anywhere, and no numerical de-rating percentage, load threshold or fuel saving is asserted.

Uncertainty and currency

This is an engineering answer with a thin but real regulatory layer, and the two are kept apart deliberately.

The regulatory frame is primary by identity and status: the revised Annex VI in force 1 November 2022, EEXI and CII applicable from 1 January 2023, SEEMP Part III mandatory from the same date, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy adopted 7 July 2023 and therefore operative here. The corpus register records the entry into force of the revised Annex VI a year late; an existing correction request covers exactly that point and is carried rather than re-raised, with the correct date used. The register also marks the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines as superseded — a 2024 event, reversed for this sitting.

The engineering is authoritative secondary and engineering judgement throughout, and it is deliberately anonymous. No engine maker, engine type, tuning package, retrofit kit, turbocharger model, lubricator system or proprietary energy-saving device is named anywhere in this answer. Every retrofit is described by its mechanism — which is what the examiner is testing, and which also means the answer cannot be dated by a product that has since been renamed or withdrawn.

No numerical value is asserted for a low-load threshold, a de-rating percentage, an SFOC gain, a compression ratio, a blower cut-in load, a fuel saving or a payback period. All are ship-specific and no held source records any of them. For the same reason no ranking of the retrofit families is offered: their merits depend on engine type, age, control system and trading pattern.

Two identity limits are recorded rather than guessed. The Organization's guidelines on the shaft or engine power limitation arrangement and the use of a power reserve are referred to descriptively and not by resolution number, because the instrument identity could not be established from a held source. And the EEXI verification trigger is described rather than quoted, the standing corpus position being that MARPOL Annex VI resolves to identity and provenance and never to text.

Currency. Two statements are pinned to this sitting and must not drift. Ammonia and hydrogen are described as under development and not in commercial service, which is correct at November 2023 and would be wrong of a later paper. And 2023 is the first CII data year and it is still running — no ship has yet been issued a rating, so an inherited sentence about ships already holding ratings would be false here.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Why the question exists - and why it is regulatory by this sitting
  2. __________Developments in marine diesel engines
  3. __________What actually goes wrong at prolonged low load
  4. __________Air-side retrofits - the first resort
  5. __________Combustion-side retrofits
  6. __________Lubrication and wear retrofits
  7. __________Propulsion-train and heat-balance retrofits
  8. __________The regulatory retrofit unique to this period
  9. __________Operational measures, and the judgement
Flashcards 11

Insufficient scavenge air. The turbocharger is driven by exhaust energy the engine is no longer producing, so it falls off its matched point, scavenge pressure and air-fuel ratio drop and the auxiliary blowers run continuously. Lost compression, poor atomisation, deposits, scavenge and economiser fires and cold corrosion all follow from it - which is why most retrofits attack the air side first.

Naming the root failure is what turns a catalogue of retrofits into an argument.

Blanking off one turbocharger on an engine fitted with two or more, so that the remaining units operate near their design point and scavenge pressure is restored at low load. It requires a cut-out kit with blanking and isolating arrangements, is reversible for high-load running, and is the cheapest effective measure available on a multi-turbocharger engine. It is not available on a single-turbocharger engine.

It is the most commonly fitted low-load retrofit and its one precondition is easy to forget.

On a mechanically controlled engine, injection and exhaust valve timing live in a camshaft, so changing them means cams, links and hardware. On an electronically controlled engine they live in the control system, so a low-load or part-load tuning package re-optimises timing, injection rate and profile in software - no hardware change, and normally reversible.

It splits the whole retrofit menu in two and is the basis of the judgement that closes the answer.

De-rating permanently re-tunes the engine to a lower rated output - new injection timing, a compression ratio change, a new turbocharger nozzle ring and sometimes new fuel valve nozzles - so the engine is genuinely designed for the duty it now performs. Power limitation does not change the engine: it restricts the power that may be delivered, as an EEXI compliance route, is verifiable and secured, and must be capable of being overridden to release a power reserve where safety or the saving of life at sea requires.

They are routinely confused, and the override requirement is a standard oral question.

Because the revised Annex VI has been in force since 1 November 2022, and EEXI and the CII rating regime have applied since 1 January 2023. EEXI is verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date, and for much of the existing fleet the compliance route was power limitation. The 2023 GHG Strategy, adopted 7 July 2023, sets the direction beyond it.

It is what makes this a 2023 answer rather than a 2015 one, and the dates are exactly what a later editor would drift.

Sulphuric acid condensing on the cylinder liner where metal temperatures are low, attacking the liner surface. The answer is electronically controlled timed cylinder lubrication, which makes feed rate proportional to load and fuel sulphur so it can be reduced with load without losing the neutralising duty; with cylinder oil base number matched to the fuel and load, anti-polishing rings to control bore polishing, raised jacket water temperature where permitted, and drain oil analysis trended rather than filed.

It is distinct from vanadium and sodium high-temperature corrosion, and it is what decides whether liners survive years of low load.

Exhaust temperature and mass flow fall, so the exhaust gas economiser makes less steam and collects more soot, raising the economiser fire risk. The auxiliary boiler therefore fires much more often, burning fuel to replace the heat the main engine is no longer wasting - so part of the saving is quietly given back. Waste heat recovery sized for full load produces much less, and a power turbine may cease to be usable below a certain output.

It is the most commonly omitted consequence of slow steaming and it has a real safety component.

Slide valves eliminate the sac volume of a conventional valve, removing the after-dripping that causes smoke and deposits at low load. Raising the compression ratio - by shims or a modified piston crown - restores compression pressure and temperature at low load, shortening ignition delay and improving combustion; it is real hardware and must be checked against the engine's maximum pressure limit.

They are the two combustion-side hardware measures available on a mechanically controlled engine.

Re-assess the torsional vibration characteristics, because changing propeller, pitch or engine rating MOVES the barred speed range, and the new range must be established and observed. It is a safety item and not an optimisation. The hull coating and cleaning policy should also be revisited, since a slower ship gives a foul release coating less shear to work with.

The barred speed range is the safety consequence of a propulsion retrofit and is almost always forgotten.

An electronically controlled engine with more than one turbocharger: low-load tuning plus turbocharger cut-out - both reversible, cheap and quick. A mechanically controlled engine: hardware - timing changes, compression ratio, slide fuel valves, nozzle ring - and the case must be made on the ship's remaining life. A ship whose speed reduction is permanent or which is power-limited for EEXI: de-rate properly and re-match the propeller. And every case needs the cylinder lubrication and inspection regime changed, whatever else is done.

The question says 'discuss', and a judgement about which ship needs what is what discussion means.

Dual-fuel operation on gas and on methanol is in service. Ammonia and hydrogen are under development and NOT in commercial service at this time. And ammonia is not a zero-emission fuel at the funnel: unburnt ammonia slip and nitrous oxide, a far more potent greenhouse gas than carbon dioxide, both have to be controlled by after-treatment.

Technology maturity is a date, and it is exactly what a later reader would silently update.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Why: power varies roughly as the cube of shaft speed, so slowing saves heavily — and by this sitting it is regulatory as well as commercial: revised Annex VI in force 1 November 2022, EEXI and CII from 1 January 2023, 2023 GHG Strategy adopted 7 July 2023. Developments: long-stroke geometry; electronically controlled camshaft-less engines (timing becomes software — the key one); high-efficiency and two-stage turbocharging with variable turbine area; primary and secondary emission control; electronically controlled timed cylinder lubrication; dual-fuel gas and methanol in service, ammonia and hydrogen under development only — and ammonia is not a zero-emission fuel at the funnel: unburnt ammonia slip and N2O, a very potent greenhouse gas, both have to be controlled; digital performance monitoring. What goes wrong at low load: air (turbocharger off its match) → compression → deposits (scavenge and economiser fires) → cold corrosion → lost waste heat → propeller mismatch → rising SFOC. Retrofits by family. AIR: turbocharger cut-out, variable turbine area, nozzle ring exchange or re-match, bypass re-setting, blower control. COMBUSTION: low-load tuning (software, reversible), VIT adjustment and cam re-timing, compression ratio increase, slide fuel valves, full de-rating. WEAR: timed electronic cylinder lubrication, base number matching, anti-polishing rings, revised inspection and drain analysis. PROPELLER: re-pitch or replace, energy-saving devices, re-check the barred speed range. HEAT: economiser soot blowing, water washing and dry-running protection; auxiliary boiler fires more; waste heat recovery bypass. LAW: engine or shaft power limitation for EEXI — verifiable, secured, and overridable to release a power reserve with the override recorded. Judgement: electronic engine with multiple turbochargers → tuning plus cut-out; mechanical engine → hardware; permanent or power-limited → de-rate and re-match the propeller; every case needs the lubrication regime changed.
