Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 is what makes this a 2023 question rather than a general one. The EEXI requirement and the CII rating regime apply from 1 January 2023, EEXI being verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date; the SEEMP with Part III mandatory from the same date is where the operating envelope and its improvement measures are recorded. Annex VI at this sitting is the MEPC.328(76) revision, in force 1 November 2022, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy was adopted 7 July 2023 and is therefore operative. Engine or shaft power limitation is the EEXI compliance route that made these retrofits necessary, and it must be capable of override to release a power reserve where safety or the saving of life at sea requires.
Major trap
Presenting the retrofits as a catalogue of products instead of as answers to named failures. The examiner is testing whether the candidate knows what actually goes wrong at prolonged low load — and the root failure is insufficient scavenge air, because the turbocharger is off its matched point, with lost compression, deposits, cold corrosion and lost waste heat following from it. The three most commonly omitted items are cylinder lubrication (which decides whether the liners survive), the heat balance (less waste steam means the auxiliary boiler fires more and quietly gives back part of the saving), and the propeller, which was matched to a speed the ship no longer runs at — and re-pitching it moves the barred speed range.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q2 - mean piston speed and the propeller cube law, which is the physics behind why slow steaming saves fuel at all · QP2311 Q6 - the planned maintenance system, where a retrofit's changed inspection intervals and cylinder oil regime must be recorded

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Two different kinds of insurance sit behind every ship and it helps to keep them apart from the first line. One covers the ship herself — the hole in her own bow after a collision. The other covers what her owner owes to everybody else: the injured crew member, the passenger, the cargo that arrived wet, the quay she hit, the wreck somebody now has to lift, the oil somebody now has to clean up. The second of those is what these clubs do, and the reason they are called clubs rather than companies is that the shipowners insure each other. There is no outside company taking a risk for profit; the members put money in, the money pays each other's claims, and if the year turns out worse than expected they are asked for more. That single fact explains almost everything about how they work — why the payments are called calls rather than premiums, why a member leaving has to buy his way out of the years still open, and why a bad claims record raises his own contribution: he has been spending his fellow members' money. The two old words in the name are just history. Protection was the part that grew up to cover injury to people and damage to things outside the ship; indemnity was added later to cover claims about the cargo and the promises made in the contract to carry it. Modern rules no longer separate them, and saying so is worth more than reciting a tidy list. The last idea is the practical one: cover is not something an owner simply buys. It is conditional, continuously — the ship must stay in class, keep her certificates, stay seaworthy, and the owner must pay when asked, tell the club when anything material changes, and after an accident say nothing and let the club run it.

Knowledge map
P&I Clubs - Nature, Calls, Protection and Indemnity Risks, and the Minimum for Cover
  1. 1What a club is
    • a MUTUAL, non-profit association of shipowners, operators, managers and demise charterers
    • formed to cover the THIRD-PARTY LIABILITIES arising out of operating their ships
    • MUTUALITY - the members are simultaneously the insured and the insurers; no external shareholder and no profit motive
  2. 2How the money is collected - the call system
    • a club does not charge a fixed premium; it levies CALLS, and the difference is the whole of mutuality
    • ADVANCE CALL - levied at the start of the policy year on an estimated total call, normally in instalments, sometimes with a DEFERRED portion
    • SUPPLEMENTARY OR ADDITIONAL CALL - where the year's claims and reinsurance costs exceed the estimate; THE DEFINING FEATURE OF A MUTUAL
  3. 3Where the two words came from
    • THE SPLIT IS HISTORICAL, NOT CONTRACTUAL, and saying so earns the mark a list alone does not
    • the clubs grew out of the nineteenth-century mutual HULL clubs
    • when the hull policy left owners exposed to a proportion of collision liability and to growing personal injury liability, owners formed PROTECTING associations to carry it
  4. 4Protection risks - the owner's liabilities as operator of the ship
    • CREW - personal injury, illness and death; medical, hospital and funeral expenses; repatriation and substitute crew; loss of personal effects; unrecoverable wages; shipwreck unemployment indemnity
    • OTHER PEOPLE - injury or death of passengers, stevedores, pilots, visitors and shore personnel
    • COLLISION LIABILITY - the proportion not recoverable under the hull policy, and liability in excess of the hull value
  5. 5Indemnity risks - cargo and contractual liabilities
    • CARGO CLAIMS - loss, shortage, damage, delay, contamination and MIS-DELIVERY of the cargo carried
    • LIABILITY UNDER THE CONTRACT OF CARRIAGE - the bill of lading and the carriage regime it incorporates
    • CARGO'S PROPORTION OF GENERAL AVERAGE where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a BREACH of the contract of carriage by the owner
  6. 6What is NOT covered - and the limb is only half answered without it
    • DAMAGE TO THE ENTERED SHIP HERSELF AND HER MACHINERY - the hull and machinery underwriter's subject
    • WAR RISKS - separately insured through a war risks association or the market
    • FREIGHT, DEMURRAGE AND DEFENCE - the cost of pursuing and defending commercial disputes is a SEPARATE CLASS of cover
  7. 7Getting the cover - entry
    • BE ELIGIBLE - cover is for the owner, operator, manager or demise charterer, a person with a real interest in operating the ship
    • APPLY AND DISCLOSE FULLY - marine insurance is a contract of UTMOST GOOD FAITH, and every material fact must be disclosed: age, type, trade, class, management, claims record, ownership structure
    • NON-DISCLOSURE IS THE CHEAPEST WAY TO LOSE A CLAIM YEARS LATER
  8. 8Keeping the cover - the continuing conditions
    • MAINTAIN CLASS - the ship must be and remain classed with a society approved by the club; conditions of class and recommendations reported and complied with by their date; any change or withdrawal of class reported at once
    • this is the single most commonly breached condition of cover, and it is why the survey regime is an insurance matter as well as a technical one
    • MAINTAIN STATUTORY CERTIFICATION AND FLAG STATE COMPLIANCE - the statutory suite, the Document of Compliance and Safety Management Certificate, the ship security certificate and the maritime labour certification
  9. 9Using the cover - conduct after a casualty
    • GIVE PROMPT NOTICE of any casualty, incident or claim likely to give rise to a liability; late notice prejudices the club's ability to defend and can prejudice the cover
    • PRESERVE THE EVIDENCE - logs, records, charts, printouts, photographs, samples and statements - and take them while they exist
    • DO NOT ADMIT LIABILITY and do not settle without the club; the club has the conduct of the claim
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. What a club is
  2. How the money is collected - the call system
  3. Where the two words came from
  4. Protection risks - the owner's liabilities as operator of the ship
  5. Indemnity risks - cargo and contractual liabilities
  6. What is NOT covered - and the limb is only half answered without it
  7. Getting the cover - entry
  8. Keeping the cover - the continuing conditions
  9. Using the cover - conduct after a casualty

Memory cue. Limb (a): MUTUAL, NOT COMMERCIAL; LIABILITIES, NOT THE SHIP (1) - then ADVANCE, SUPPLEMENTARY, RELEASE, OVERSPILL (2). Limb (b): PROTECTION IS PEOPLE AND THINGS; INDEMNITY IS CARGO AND CONTRACTS (4, 5), and three exclusions - OWN HULL, WAR, DEFENCE (6). Limb (c): GET IT, KEEP IT, USE IT (7, 8, 9).

Remember9 route headings
Cover65 core points beneath them

Memorise the 9 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 65 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What a club is
  • a MUTUAL, non-profit association of shipowners, operators, managers and demise charterers
  • formed to cover the THIRD-PARTY LIABILITIES arising out of operating their ships
  • MUTUALITY - the members are simultaneously the insured and the insurers; no external shareholder and no profit motive
  • a member's contribution is therefore NOT CAPPED at what he paid in advance
  • governed by its own RULES, with a board drawn from the membership rather than from an insurance market
  • IT INSURES LIABILITIES, NOT THE SHIP - the hull and machinery underwriter covers damage to the ship herself, the club covers what the owner OWES TO OTHER PEOPLE
  • the classic illustration is a collision: the hull policy pays for the owner's own ship, the club picks up the part of the collision liability the hull policy does not
  • ABOVE THE INDIVIDUAL CLUB - claims above each club's retention are POOLED between the principal clubs, above the pool the group buys common EXCESS-LOSS REINSURANCE, and above that sits a residual layer of member liability
  • the effect is that a club of modest size can carry a catastrophic pollution or passenger claim, which is why compulsory-insurance regimes accept a club certificate
2. How the money is collected - the call system
  • a club does not charge a fixed premium; it levies CALLS, and the difference is the whole of mutuality
  • ADVANCE CALL - levied at the start of the policy year on an estimated total call, normally in instalments, sometimes with a DEFERRED portion
  • SUPPLEMENTARY OR ADDITIONAL CALL - where the year's claims and reinsurance costs exceed the estimate; THE DEFINING FEATURE OF A MUTUAL
  • RELEASE CALL - payable by a member leaving the club, buying out his continuing exposure to future calls on the policy years still open
  • OVERSPILL CALL - levied across the membership where a single claim exceeds the top of the reinsurance programme; the ultimate expression of mutuality and extremely rare
  • LAID-UP RETURNS - a credit rather than a call, where a ship is laid up for an extended period
  • calls are supported by the club's FREE RESERVES and INVESTMENT INCOME, which is why a well-reserved club can hold calls steady through a poor year
  • BASIS OF ASSESSMENT - entered tonnage as the primary measure; ship type and trade; the member's own claims record; the deductibles accepted; and any general increase applied across the membership
  • the policy year conventionally runs from noon on 20 February to noon on 20 February
3. Where the two words came from
  • THE SPLIT IS HISTORICAL, NOT CONTRACTUAL, and saying so earns the mark a list alone does not
  • the clubs grew out of the nineteenth-century mutual HULL clubs
  • when the hull policy left owners exposed to a proportion of collision liability and to growing personal injury liability, owners formed PROTECTING associations to carry it
  • when they later needed cover for CARGO claims, which the protecting associations would not take, INDEMNITY associations were formed, and the two merged
  • MODERN CLUB RULES DO NOT DIVIDE THEIR COVER INTO TWO FORMALLY SEPARATE CLASSES - the words describe the ORIGIN of two groups of risk, not a present-day boundary
4. Protection risks - the owner's liabilities as operator of the ship
  • CREW - personal injury, illness and death; medical, hospital and funeral expenses; repatriation and substitute crew; loss of personal effects; unrecoverable wages; shipwreck unemployment indemnity
  • OTHER PEOPLE - injury or death of passengers, stevedores, pilots, visitors and shore personnel
  • COLLISION LIABILITY - the proportion not recoverable under the hull policy, and liability in excess of the hull value
  • DAMAGE TO FIXED AND FLOATING OBJECTS - berths, quays, buoys, cranes, submarine cables and pipelines
  • WRECK REMOVAL - raising, removing, destroying, lighting or marking the wreck where compulsory; very often the largest single claim a club will see
  • POLLUTION - liability for escape or discharge, clean-up costs, preventive measures and consequential third-party loss
  • PERSONS - stowaways, refugees and persons saved at sea with their diversion and maintenance costs; and life salvage
  • PUBLIC HEALTH AND ADMINISTRATION - quarantine and disinfection expenses, and certain FINES including customs, immigration, pollution and cargo shortage, subject to the club's discretion
  • LEGAL AND INVESTIGATION COSTS of all of the above, and the expense of attending official inquiries
5. Indemnity risks - cargo and contractual liabilities
  • CARGO CLAIMS - loss, shortage, damage, delay, contamination and MIS-DELIVERY of the cargo carried
  • LIABILITY UNDER THE CONTRACT OF CARRIAGE - the bill of lading and the carriage regime it incorporates
  • CARGO'S PROPORTION OF GENERAL AVERAGE where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a BREACH of the contract of carriage by the owner
  • PROPERTY ON BOARD OTHER THAN CARGO - containers, equipment and property belonging to others
  • CONTRACTUAL LIABILITIES under towage, indemnity and similar contracts, BUT ONLY WHERE THE CLUB HAS APPROVED THE TERMS - this is where owners most often lose cover
  • SUE AND LABOUR - the costs of measures reasonably taken to avert or minimise a liability that would otherwise fall on the club
6. What is NOT covered - and the limb is only half answered without it
  • DAMAGE TO THE ENTERED SHIP HERSELF AND HER MACHINERY - the hull and machinery underwriter's subject
  • WAR RISKS - separately insured through a war risks association or the market
  • FREIGHT, DEMURRAGE AND DEFENCE - the cost of pursuing and defending commercial disputes is a SEPARATE CLASS of cover
  • LIABILITIES UNDER UNAPPROVED CONTRACTS, and liabilities assumed by carrying on terms LESS FAVOURABLE than the applicable carriage regime - which is why a bill of lading must not be signed on wider terms than the charter permits
  • WILFUL MISCONDUCT, and the consequences of the member's own default in the conditions of cover
7. Getting the cover - entry
  • BE ELIGIBLE - cover is for the owner, operator, manager or demise charterer, a person with a real interest in operating the ship
  • APPLY AND DISCLOSE FULLY - marine insurance is a contract of UTMOST GOOD FAITH, and every material fact must be disclosed: age, type, trade, class, management, claims record, ownership structure
  • NON-DISCLOSURE IS THE CHEAPEST WAY TO LOSE A CLAIM YEARS LATER
  • OBTAIN THE CERTIFICATE OF ENTRY - it records the ship, the member, the terms and limits, the deductibles and any special terms or warranties, and its terms must actually be read
  • SUBMIT TO A CONDITION SURVEY where the club requires one - commonly for older ships, on entry, or on a change of class or management - and RECTIFY THE RECOMMENDATIONS WITHIN THE TIME ALLOWED
8. Keeping the cover - the continuing conditions
  • MAINTAIN CLASS - the ship must be and remain classed with a society approved by the club; conditions of class and recommendations reported and complied with by their date; any change or withdrawal of class reported at once
  • this is the single most commonly breached condition of cover, and it is why the survey regime is an insurance matter as well as a technical one
  • MAINTAIN STATUTORY CERTIFICATION AND FLAG STATE COMPLIANCE - the statutory suite, the Document of Compliance and Safety Management Certificate, the ship security certificate and the maritime labour certification
  • KEEP THE SHIP SEAWORTHY AND EXERCISE DUE DILIGENCE - want of due diligence is a defence available to the club
  • PAY THE CALLS - advance, deferred and supplementary, on their due dates; NON-PAYMENT CAN SUSPEND OR TERMINATE COVER
  • COMPLY WITH THE RULES AND WARRANTIES - trading limits and warranties, crewing warranties, no unapproved contracts, and no carriage on terms less favourable than the applicable regime
  • NOTIFY MATERIAL CHANGES - ownership, management, flag, class, trade or lay-up; cover is written on a described risk
  • BEAR THE DEDUCTIBLE on each claim
  • MAINTAIN THE COMPULSORY CERTIFICATES the club issues on the strength of the entry - the blue cards and certificates required by the civil liability, bunkers, wreck removal and maritime labour financial-security regimes; WITHOUT THESE THE SHIP CANNOT LAWFULLY TRADE
9. Using the cover - conduct after a casualty
  • GIVE PROMPT NOTICE of any casualty, incident or claim likely to give rise to a liability; late notice prejudices the club's ability to defend and can prejudice the cover
  • PRESERVE THE EVIDENCE - logs, records, charts, printouts, photographs, samples and statements - and take them while they exist
  • DO NOT ADMIT LIABILITY and do not settle without the club; the club has the conduct of the claim
  • USE THE CLUB CORRESPONDENT at the port and follow the club's instructions on surveyors, lawyers and security
  • MITIGATE - take reasonable steps to minimise the loss, which is itself a recoverable head
  • CO-OPERATE FULLY with the club's investigation and produce the ship's records, which is where the maintenance and survey records are actually tested
  • PAY TO BE PAID - the general rule is that the member must first DISCHARGE the liability before recovering from the club, subject to the direct rights of action certain compulsory-insurance regimes confer on claimants
  • THE MINIMUM IN ONE SENTENCE - enter with full disclosure, keep her in class and in certification, keep her seaworthy, pay the calls, obey the rules and warranties, notify changes and casualties promptly, and let the club run the claim
Model written answer

(a) What P&I clubs are, and how they raise their funds

1. What a club is

A protection and indemnity club is a mutual, non-profit association of shipowners, operators, managers and demise charterers, formed to provide its members with cover against the third-party liabilities that arise out of operating their ships.

Two features distinguish it from a commercial insurer, and both are worth stating explicitly:

  1. Mutuality. The members are simultaneously the insured and the insurers. There is no external shareholder and no profit motive; the members share each other's losses, and a member's contribution is therefore not capped at what he paid in advance. The club is governed by its own Rules, and its board is drawn from the membership rather than from an insurance market.
  2. It insures liabilities, not the ship. The hull and machinery underwriter covers damage to the ship herself. The club covers what the owner owes to other people — the crew, the passengers, the cargo, the harbour authority, the coastal State. The two are complementary, and the classic illustration is a collision: the hull policy pays for the owner's own ship, and the club picks up the part of the collision liability the hull policy does not.

Above the individual club there is a structure that matters. The principal clubs act together: claims above each club's own retention are pooled between them; above the pool the group buys a common excess-loss reinsurance programme in the commercial market; and above that sits a residual layer of member liability. The effect is that a single club of modest size can carry a catastrophic pollution or passenger claim — which is precisely why compulsory-insurance regimes are content to accept a club certificate.

2. How the money is collected - the call system

A club does not charge a fixed premium. It levies calls, and the difference is the whole of mutuality:

CallWhen and why
Advance callLevied at the start of the policy year on an estimated total call, normally payable in instalments. It is the working capital for the year, sometimes with a deferred portion payable later
Supplementary (additional) callLevied where the year's claims and reinsurance costs exceed the estimate. This is the defining feature of a mutual - the member's liability is not limited to what he has already paid
Release callPayable by a member leaving the club, to buy out his continuing exposure to future supplementary calls on the policy years still open
Overspill callLevied across the membership where a single claim exceeds the top of the reinsurance programme - the ultimate expression of mutuality and, in practice, extremely rare
Laid-up returnsA credit rather than a call - an allowance where a ship is laid up for an extended period and the exposure falls

Calls are supported by the club's free reserves and its investment income, which is why a well-reserved club can hold its calls steady through a poor claims year.

The basis of assessment — what decides how much a particular owner pays:

  • Entered tonnage, which is the primary measure;
  • ship type and trade — a passenger ship, a chemical tanker and a bulk carrier carry very different liability exposures;
  • the member's own claims record, which is what makes the system reward good operators;
  • the deductibles the member accepts; and
  • any general increase the club applies across its whole membership for the coming year.

The policy year conventionally runs from noon on 20 February to noon on 20 February, which is why renewal negotiations and general increases are an annual February event across the industry.

(b) The risks covered under "protection" and under "indemnity"

3. Where the two words came from

The split is historical, not contractual, and saying so earns the mark that a list alone does not. The clubs grew out of the nineteenth-century mutual hull clubs. When the hull policy left owners exposed to a proportion of collision liability and to a growing body of personal injury liability, owners formed protecting associations to carry it. When they later needed cover for cargo claims, which the protecting associations would not take, indemnity associations were formed, and the two merged.

Modern club rules do not divide their cover into two formally separate classes. The words describe the origin of two groups of risk, not a present-day boundary — and a candidate who says that has understood the question rather than memorised a table.

4. Protection risks - the owner's liabilities as operator of the ship

  • Crew — personal injury, illness and death; medical, hospital and funeral expenses; repatriation and substitute crew; loss of personal effects; unrecoverable wages; and shipwreck unemployment indemnity.
  • Other people — injury or death of passengers, stevedores, pilots, visitors and shore personnel.
  • Collision liability — the proportion not recoverable under the hull policy, and liability in excess of the hull value.
  • Damage to fixed and floating objects — berths, quays, buoys, cranes, submarine cables and pipelines.
  • Wreck removal — the cost of raising, removing, destroying, lighting or marking the wreck where that is compulsory, which is very often the largest single claim a club will see.
  • Pollution — liability for escape or discharge of oil and other substances, clean-up costs, the cost of preventive measures, and consequential third-party loss.
  • Persons — stowaways, refugees and persons saved at sea, with the associated diversion and maintenance costs; and life salvage.
  • Public health and administration — quarantine and disinfection expenses, and certain fines: customs and immigration penalties, pollution fines, and fines for short or over-delivery of cargo, subject to the club's discretion.
  • Legal and investigation costs of all of the above, and the expense of attending official inquiries.

5. Indemnity risks - cargo and contractual liabilities

  • Cargo claims — loss, shortage, damage, delay, contamination and mis-delivery of the cargo carried.
  • Liability under the contract of carriage — the bill of lading and the carriage regime it incorporates.
  • Cargo's proportion of general average where it cannot be recovered from the cargo interests because of a breach of the contract of carriage by the owner.
  • Property on board other than cargo — containers, equipment and property belonging to others.
  • Contractual liabilities — under towage, indemnity and similar contracts, but only where the club has approved the terms. This is where owners most often lose cover.
  • Sue and labour — the costs of measures reasonably taken to avert or minimise a liability that would otherwise fall on the club.

6. What is NOT covered - and the limb is only half answered without it

  • Damage to the entered ship herself and her machinery — that is the hull and machinery underwriter's subject.
  • War risks — separately insured, through a war risks association or the market.
  • Freight, demurrage and defence — the cost of pursuing and defending commercial disputes is a separate class of cover, not part of ordinary P&I.
  • Liabilities under unapproved contracts, and liabilities assumed by carrying on terms less favourable than the applicable carriage regime — which is exactly why a bill of lading must not be signed on wider terms than the charter permits.
  • Wilful misconduct, and the consequences of the member's own default in the conditions of cover.

(c) The minimum an owner must do for the ship to have cover

7. Getting the cover - entry

  1. Be eligible. Cover is for the owner, operator, manager or demise charterer — a person with a real interest in operating the ship.
  2. Apply, and disclose fully. Marine insurance is a contract of utmost good faith. Every material fact — age, type, trade, class, management, claims record, ownership structure — must be disclosed. Non-disclosure is the cheapest way to lose a claim years later.
  3. Obtain the Certificate of Entry. It records the ship, the member, the terms and limits, the deductibles and any special terms or warranties. It is the contract document and its terms must actually be read.
  4. Submit to a condition survey where the club requires one — commonly for older ships, on entry, or on a change of class or management — and rectify the surveyor's recommendations within the time allowed.

8. Keeping the cover - the continuing conditions

  1. Maintain class. The ship must be, and remain, classed with a classification society approved by the club. Any condition of class or recommendation must be reported to the club and complied with by its date, and any change or withdrawal of class reported at once. This is the single most commonly breached condition, and it is why the survey regime in Q1 of this paper is an insurance matter as well as a technical one.
  2. Maintain statutory certification and flag State compliance — the full statutory suite, the Document of Compliance and Safety Management Certificate under the ISM Code, the International Ship Security Certificate, and the maritime labour certification.
  3. Keep the ship seaworthy and exercise due diligence. The club does not insure the consequences of an unseaworthy ship knowingly sent to sea; want of due diligence is a defence available to it.
  4. Pay the calls. Advance, deferred and supplementary calls, on their due dates. Non-payment can suspend or terminate cover, and it is the simplest way to be uninsured without noticing.
  5. Comply with the Rules and the warranties — trading limits and trading warranties, crewing warranties, and the requirement not to enter unapproved contracts or to carry on terms less favourable than the applicable carriage regime.
  6. Notify material changes — ownership, management, flag, class, trade or lay-up. Cover is written on a described risk and a changed risk must be re-agreed.
  7. Bear the deductible on each claim.
  8. Maintain the compulsory certificates the club issues on the strength of the entry — the blue cards and certificates required by the civil liability, bunkers, wreck removal and maritime labour financial-security regimes. Without these the ship cannot lawfully trade, and they are the most visible practical product of the entry.

9. Using the cover - conduct after a casualty

  1. Give prompt notice of any casualty, incident or claim likely to give rise to a liability. Late notice prejudices the club's ability to defend and can prejudice the cover.
  2. Preserve the evidence — logs, records, charts, printouts, photographs, samples and statements — and take them while they exist.
  3. Do not admit liability and do not settle without the club. The club has the conduct of the claim.
  4. Use the club correspondent at the port and follow the club's instructions on surveyors, lawyers and security.
  5. Mitigate — take reasonable steps to minimise the loss, which is itself a recoverable head.
  6. Co-operate fully with the club's investigation, and produce the ship's records — which is where the maintenance and survey records discussed elsewhere on this paper are actually tested.
  7. Be aware of “pay to be paid”: the general rule is that the member must first discharge the liability before recovering from the club. Certain compulsory-insurance regimes give the claimant a direct right of action against the insurer and displace that rule; ordinary cargo and crew claims do not.

The minimum, reduced to one sentence: enter the ship with full disclosure; keep her in class and in statutory certification; keep her seaworthy; pay the calls; obey the Rules and the warranties; notify changes and casualties promptly; and let the club run the claim. Every one of those is a condition, and each of them has been the reason a claim failed.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The marks are printed 5 + 5 + 6. Limb (a) contains two questions and both must be answered — what a club is, and how it collects funds — and the second is the one that gets dropped. The calls therefore go in a table, because five named calls are a countable, scannable deliverable.

Limb (b) names two words and gets two lists, prefaced by one short paragraph explaining where the words came from. That paragraph earns more than another six bullet points would, because it shows the split is understood rather than memorised. A third short list of exclusions closes it: a limb about what is covered is only half answered without what is not.

Limb (c) carries the most marks and is the one that differs from every other version of this question in circulation. It is written in three stages — getting the cover, keeping it, and using it — because that ordering is what makes a list of conditions into a chronology a candidate can reconstruct under pressure.

The word "averages" in limb (c)

“Average” is a hull and machinery word, not a P&I one. In marine insurance it means loss or damage, and its two familiar compounds — general average and particular average — belong to the world of the hull policy, the ship, the cargo and the adjuster. A P&I club does not pay “averages”; it indemnifies third-party liabilities.

The stem is preserved as printed and not corrected. The examiner's meaning is nonetheless plain: what must the owner minimally do for his ship to have effective P&I cover and to be able to recover on it? Say the terminological point once, in a single sentence, and then answer the question that was asked. A candidate who spends a paragraph correcting the examiner has spent six marks' worth of time on grammar.

One genuine connection is worth making, because it shows the point was understood rather than merely spotted: a club does meet cargo's proportion of general average where that proportion cannot be recovered from the cargo interests because of a breach of the contract of carriage. That is the one place where average and P&I really do meet.

Mutuality in one line

The members are both the insured and the insurers. Everything else about a club follows from that:

  • there is no fixed premium, because there is no underwriter taking a risk for profit — there are calls;
  • a member's liability is not capped at what he has paid, which is why supplementary calls exist and why a departing member owes a release call;
  • the club is governed by Rules the members themselves set, and by a board drawn from the membership; and
  • a bad claims record raises his call, because he is spending his fellow members' money.

Hull and machinery against P&I - the collision illustration

Hull and machineryP&I club
InsuresThe ship herselfWhat the owner owes to others
BasisCommercial market, fixed premiumMutual, calls
In a collisionDamage to the member's own ship, and the greater part of the collision liability to the other shipThe balance of the collision liability not recoverable under the hull policy, and liability in excess of hull value
Also-Crew, passengers, cargo, wreck removal, pollution, fixed and floating objects, fines, legal costs

Common mistakes

  • Describing a club as an insurance company. It is a mutual association owned by its members.
  • Answering limb (a) on what a club is and never describing how it collects funds. The limb asks both.
  • Giving only the advance call. Supplementary, release and overspill calls are what distinguish a mutual from a premium insurer.
  • Presenting protection and indemnity as a formal division in the rules. It is historical, and modern rules do not draw the line.
  • Putting cargo claims under protection or crew claims under indemnity.
  • Claiming the club covers damage to the entered ship. It does not — that is hull and machinery.
  • Forgetting the exclusions, and in particular that war risks and freight, demurrage and defence are separately covered.
  • In limb (c), listing what the club does rather than what the owner must do. This paper asks the owner's side; the versions in circulation ask the club's.
  • Omitting maintenance of class, which is the single most commonly breached condition of cover.
  • Omitting payment of calls, which is the simplest way to become uninsured without noticing.
  • Omitting the compulsory certificates - the blue cards - which are the most visible practical product of the entry.
  • Correcting the examiner's use of “averages” at length instead of noting it once and answering.

Examiner traps

  • “Is a P&I club an insurance company?” No — a mutual, non-profit association whose members are both the insured and the insurers.
  • “What is a supplementary call?” An additional levy where the year's claims exceed the estimate. It is why a member's liability is not capped at the advance call.
  • “You are leaving your club. What do you owe?” A release call, buying out your exposure to future calls on the open policy years.
  • “When does the policy year begin?” Conventionally at noon on 20 February.
  • “What decides how much you pay?” Entered tonnage, ship type and trade, claims record, deductibles chosen, and the club's general increase.
  • “Who pays for the hole in your own bow after a collision?” The hull and machinery underwriter. The club pays the part of the liability to the other ship that the hull policy does not.
  • “Name three protection risks and three indemnity risks.” Protection: crew injury, wreck removal, pollution. Indemnity: cargo damage, cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable through breach of the contract of carriage, approved towage contract liabilities.
  • “Is FD&D part of P&I?” No — freight, demurrage and defence is a separate class of cover.
  • “What is a blue card?” The club's evidence of insurance supporting the compulsory certificates required by the civil liability, bunkers, wreck removal and maritime labour financial-security regimes.
  • “Your ship loses class. What happens to your cover?” Class must be maintained and any change or withdrawal reported at once; cover may cease automatically under the rules.
  • “What is 'pay to be paid'?” The member must first discharge the liability before recovering from the club — subject to the direct rights of action certain compulsory-insurance regimes give claimants.
  • “A stevedore is injured and asks you to sign an admission.” Do not admit liability; notify the club at once, preserve the evidence, and let the club and its correspondent handle it.

Likely oral follow-up

  • What does P&I stand for? Protection and indemnity.
  • Who can enter a ship in a club? The owner, operator, manager or demise charterer.
  • What document evidences the cover? The certificate of entry.
  • What is an overspill call? A levy across the membership where a single claim exceeds the top of the group reinsurance programme.
  • Why do clubs pool claims? So that a club of modest size can carry a catastrophic pollution or passenger claim.
  • Name two things the club will want after a casualty. Prompt notice, and the preserved evidence - logs, records, photographs and statements.
  • Who is the club correspondent? The club's local representative at the port, who arranges surveyors, lawyers and security.
  • What is a condition survey? A survey the club requires - typically for older ships or on entry or change of management - whose recommendations must be rectified within the time allowed.
  • Name a liability the club will not cover under a towage contract. Any liability under terms the club has not approved.
  • Which Indian statutes frame this at present? The Merchant Shipping Act, 1958, the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, and the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993.

Memory framework

For limb (a), two sentences and five words. MUTUAL, NOT COMMERCIAL. LIABILITIES, NOT THE SHIP. Then the calls: ADVANCE, SUPPLEMENTARY, RELEASE, OVERSPILL — and LAID-UP RETURN as the one that goes the other way.

For limb (b), one question each: PROTECTION is about PEOPLE AND THINGS OUTSIDE THE SHIP — crew, passengers, the other ship, the quay, the wreck, the sea. INDEMNITY is about WHAT SHE WAS CARRYING AND WHAT SHE PROMISED — cargo and contracts. Then three exclusions: HER OWN HULL, WAR, AND DEFENCE COSTS.

For limb (c), three stages: GET IT, KEEP IT, USE IT. Get it — disclose, enter, survey. Keep it — CLASS, CERTIFICATES, SEAWORTHY, CALLS, RULES, CHANGES. Use it — NOTIFY, PRESERVE, ADMIT NOTHING, CO-OPERATE.

Regulation and source map

  • The club's own Rules are the governing document of the relationship. They are private contractual documents. No club is named and no rule is cited or quoted in this answer; see the uncertainty section.
  • The compulsory-insurance regimes behind the certificates a club issues — the civil liability and fund regime for oil pollution from tankers; the bunkers convention for bunker oil pollution; the wreck removal convention, in force since 14 April 2015; and the maritime labour financial-security provisions introduced by the 2014 amendments and in force since 2017. Named at identity level; nothing quoted.
  • MLC 2006 is at its 2018 amendment state at this sitting. The 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024 and are future here.
  • Hague-Visby Rules, through the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993, which is the carriage regime the indemnity side responds to.
  • Marine Insurance Act, 1963 (India) — the general law of marine insurance, including the duty of utmost good faith.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 — the governing Indian shipping statute at this sitting. The 2025 Act commenced 15 March 2026 and is prohibited here.

Uncertainty and the limits of the evidence

This question is answered out of documents the corpus does not hold and could not hold.

A P&I club is a private mutual association governed by its own Rules, and those Rules are contractual documents between a club and its members. They are not corpus candidates. The True Source corpus holds no marine insurance instrument of any kind — its reference resolver contains 320 entries and every one is MARPOL Annex VI. The nature of a club, the call system, the two groups of risk and the conditions of entry and continuation are uncontroversial and consistently recorded across the industry, and are carried at authoritative-secondary level.

The consequences are deliberate and they are the reason this answer contains almost no numbers. No club is named. No club rule is cited, numbered or quoted. No monetary figure is asserted anywhere — no individual club retention, no pooling layer, no reinsurance limit, no overspill threshold, no call rate, no deductible and no premium — because all of them change annually and none is held. The number of clubs in the group is not stated for the same reason; the structure is described instead. And no time limit is given for notifying a claim, because that is set by the individual club's rules.

One boundary is left deliberately soft. The precise line between the protection heads and the indemnity heads is not asserted as a rule-based division, because modern club rules do not draw one. The answer says so in its own text and treats the terms as historical, which is more honest than presenting a tidy table as though it were the rules.

The compulsory-insurance regimes behind the certificates are named at identity level with their in-force status taken from the corpus amendment register; no article or regulation of any of them is quoted and no limitation or fund limit is given. The “pay to be paid” rule and the statutory direct-action exception are stated in outline with no case, rule or provision cited, because none is held.

Currency. Two dates are pinned to this sitting. MLC 2006 is at its 2018 amendment state here; the 2022 amendments enter into force on 23 December 2024, thirteen months after this examination, and are not cited. And the Indian statutory frame is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 with the Marine Insurance Act, 1963 — the Merchant Shipping Act, 2025 commenced 15 March 2026, twenty-eight months after this examination, and is the single easiest anachronism to import into a 2023 answer.

Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 9 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What a club is
  2. __________How the money is collected - the call system
  3. __________Where the two words came from
  4. __________Protection risks - the owner's liabilities as operator of the ship
  5. __________Indemnity risks - cargo and contractual liabilities
  6. __________What is NOT covered - and the limb is only half answered without it
  7. __________Getting the cover - entry
  8. __________Keeping the cover - the continuing conditions
  9. __________Using the cover - conduct after a casualty
Flashcards 11

A mutual, non-profit association of shipowners, operators, managers and demise charterers, providing cover against third-party liabilities arising out of operating their ships. Two differences: mutuality - the members are simultaneously the insured and the insurers, so there is no premium and no profit motive and a member's liability is not capped at what he has paid; and subject matter - the club insures what the owner OWES TO OTHERS, while the hull and machinery underwriter insures the ship herself.

Mutual, not commercial; liabilities, not the ship - the two lines that carry the whole of limb (a).

Advance call at the start of the policy year on an estimated total call, often in instalments and sometimes with a deferred portion. Supplementary or additional call where the year's claims and reinsurance costs exceed the estimate - the defining feature of a mutual. Release call payable by a member leaving, buying out his exposure to future calls on the open policy years. Overspill call across the membership where a single claim exceeds the top of the reinsurance programme. And laid-up returns, which are a credit rather than a call.

'Describe how they collect fund' is the half of limb (a) that gets dropped, and four named calls make it undroppable.

On entered tonnage as the primary measure, the ship's type and trade, the member's own claims record, the deductibles he accepts, and any general increase the club applies across its membership. The policy year conventionally runs from noon on 20 February to noon on 20 February, which is why renewals and general increases are an annual February event.

The 20 February policy year is the one date in this question, and the claims-record element is what makes mutuality reward good operators.

The clubs grew out of the nineteenth-century mutual hull clubs. Protecting associations were formed to carry the collision liability the hull policy left uncovered and the growing body of personal injury liability; indemnity associations were formed later for cargo claims, which the protecting associations would not take, and the two merged. Modern club rules do NOT divide their cover into two formally separate classes - the words describe the origin of two groups of risk, not a present-day boundary.

Explaining the split is worth more than another six bullet points of it.

Crew injury, illness, death and repatriation with medical and substitute-crew costs; injury or death of passengers, stevedores, pilots and visitors; the proportion of collision liability not recoverable under the hull policy and liability in excess of hull value; damage to fixed and floating objects such as quays, buoys and submarine cables; wreck removal where compulsory; and pollution liability, clean-up and preventive measures - together with stowaways, life salvage, quarantine, certain fines and the legal costs of all of it.

Protection is about people and things outside the ship - that phrase recovers the whole list.

Cargo loss, shortage, damage, delay, contamination and mis-delivery; liability arising under the contract of carriage; cargo's proportion of general average where it cannot be recovered from the cargo interests because of a breach of the contract of carriage; damage to property on board other than cargo; liabilities under towage and other contracts WHERE THE CLUB HAS APPROVED THE TERMS; and sue and labour costs.

Indemnity is about what she was carrying and what she promised - cargo and contracts.

Damage to the entered ship herself and her machinery - that is hull and machinery. War risks, which are separately insured. Freight, demurrage and defence, which is a separate class of cover for pursuing and defending commercial disputes. And liabilities under unapproved contracts or assumed by carrying on terms less favourable than the applicable carriage regime - together with wilful misconduct.

A limb about what is covered is only half answered without what is not.

Maintenance of class. The ship must be and remain classed with a society approved by the club; any condition of class or recommendation must be reported and complied with by its date; and any change or withdrawal of class must be reported at once, cover being liable to cease under the rules. It is why the classification survey regime is an insurance matter as well as a technical one.

It links the commercial question directly to the engineer's own responsibilities.

GET IT - apply with full disclosure in utmost good faith, obtain and read the certificate of entry, and pass any condition survey. KEEP IT - maintain class and statutory certification, keep the ship seaworthy, pay the calls when due, obey the rules and warranties, notify changes of ownership, management, flag, class or trade, and hold the compulsory certificates. USE IT - prompt notice, preserved evidence, no admission of liability, and co-operation with the club.

This is the limb that differs from every other version of this question, and the three-stage ordering is what makes it reconstructable.

The club's evidence of insurance, issued on the strength of the entry, which supports the compulsory certificates required by the oil pollution civil liability regime, the bunkers convention, the wreck removal convention and the maritime labour financial-security provisions. Without those certificates the ship cannot lawfully trade, which makes them the most visible practical product of a P&I entry.

It is where the club's cover becomes a trading requirement rather than a commercial choice.

No. 'Average' is a hull and machinery term meaning loss or damage, and general and particular average belong to the hull policy and the adjuster; a club indemnifies third-party liabilities and does not pay averages. The stem is preserved as printed and the point is made in one sentence, then the question the examiner plainly asked is answered. The one genuine overlap is that a club does meet cargo's proportion of general average where it cannot be recovered from cargo interests because of a breach of the contract of carriage.

Noting a printed looseness once and moving on is a skill; correcting the examiner at length costs marks.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A P&I club is a MUTUAL, non-profit association of owners, operators, managers and demise charterers, covering third-party liabilities — not the ship herself, which is hull and machinery. The members are both the insured and the insurers, so there is no premium: there are calls. Advance call at the start of the year; supplementary call if claims exceed the estimate (the defining mutual feature); release call on leaving, to buy out the open years; overspill call above the reinsurance programme; laid-up returns the other way. Assessed on entered tonnage, ship type and trade, claims record, deductibles and the general increase; policy year from noon on 20 February. Above the club, claims are pooled between the principal clubs with common excess-loss reinsurance above the pool. Protection (from the old hull clubs) — crew injury, illness, death and repatriation; passengers and third parties; the collision liability the hull policy does not cover; fixed and floating objects; wreck removal; pollution; stowaways and life salvage; quarantine and certain fines; legal costs. Indemnity — cargo loss, shortage, damage, delay and mis-delivery; liability under the contract of carriage; cargo's proportion of general average unrecoverable through breach; property on board; approved towage and contractual liabilities; sue and labour. The split is historical, not a division in modern rules. Not covered: the entered ship, war risks, freight demurrage and defence, unapproved contracts, wilful misconduct. The owner's minimum: GET IT — disclose fully, enter, take the certificate of entry, pass the condition survey; KEEP IT — maintain class and statutory certification, stay seaworthy, pay the calls, obey the rules and warranties, notify changes, hold the blue cards; USE IT — prompt notice, preserve evidence, admit nothing, co-operate, mitigate, and remember pay to be paid.
Keywords
P&I clubmutual associationthird-party liabilityhull and machinerypoolingexcess loss reinsuranceadvance callsupplementary calladditional callrelease calloverspill calllaid-up returnentered tonnageclaims recorddeductiblegeneral increase20 Februarycertificate of entryprotection riskscrew injuryrepatriationcollision liabilityfixed and floating objectswreck removalpollution liabilitystowawayslife salvagefinesindemnity riskscargo claimsmis-deliverygeneral average proportionapproved towage contractsue and labourwar risksfreight demurrage and defenceutmost good faithcondition surveymaintenance of classblue cardpay to be paidclub correspondent
Critical numbers
  • The policy year conventionally runs from noon on 20 FEBRUARY to noon on 20 February
  • Four kinds of call: advance, supplementary (additional), release, overspill - plus laid-up returns as a credit
  • The wreck removal convention has been in force since 14 April 2015
  • MLC 2006 financial security provisions in force from the 2014 amendments; the 2022 amendments enter into force 23 December 2024 and are FUTURE at this sitting
  • No monetary figure is stated anywhere in this answer - no retention, pool layer, reinsurance limit, call rate or deductible
Critical regulation
There is no single instrument here: a club is governed by its own Rules, which are private contractual documents between the association and its members and are neither cited nor quoted in this answer. What the law supplies is the framework around the cover — the compulsory-insurance regimes for oil pollution from tankers, for bunker oil pollution, for wreck removal (in force since 14 April 2015) and for maritime labour financial security, all of which require certificates the club issues on the strength of the entry; the Hague-Visby Rules through the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993, which is the carriage regime the indemnity side responds to; and the Marine Insurance Act, 1963, which supplies the duty of utmost good faith relied on at entry. The governing Indian shipping statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.
Major trap
Answering limb (c) as though it asked what the club does. Every other version of this question in circulation asks how a club handles a claim; this paper asks what the OWNER must minimally do for his ship to have effective cover — disclose fully and enter, maintain class and statutory certification, keep the ship seaworthy, pay the calls, comply with the rules and warranties, notify material changes, and give prompt notice and preserve evidence after a casualty. The paired traps are describing a club as an insurance company when it is a mutual association whose members are both insured and insurers; giving only the advance call and omitting the supplementary, release and overspill calls that make mutuality real; and presenting protection and indemnity as a formal division in the rules when the split is historical and modern rules do not draw it.

Also on the platform: QP2311 Q3 - the bill of lading and the letter of indemnity, which is where P&I cover is most easily prejudiced by a signature · QP2311 Q1 - the class survey regime, whose maintenance is a condition of the cover described here

First in set First set here. The same task returns at June 2024 Q7, February 2025 Q7, April 2025 Q8.

No question matches that search. Try a topic, a regulation or a question number — for example general average, MARPOL Annex VI or Q5.

November 2023 rapid revision

Every question in one pass. Generated from each question's Quick Revision block — open the full answer from the question number.

QTopicAnswer routeCritical rule / numberMajor trap
Q1 Continuous Survey of Machinery - Objectives, Method, Technology and Challenges
16 marks		 What the continuous survey of machinery is, and where it sits → Why it matters - the significance → Objectives → Methodology - how the scheme actually runs → The impact of advanced technologies → Enumerated challenges → Matched solutions SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 is the provision the whole scheme is built on: it requires the hull, machinery and equipment of a cargo ship to be surveyed on renewal at intervals not exceeding five years, with intermediate and annual surveys inside the cycle. Continuous Survey of Machinery does not touch that interval — it distributes it across the cycle item by item under classification society rules, accepted by the Administration through the recognized organization acting under regulation 6, with regulation 11 requiring the condition to be maintained after survey.
SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 - machinery renewal survey at intervals NOT EXCEEDING FIVE YEARS		 Writing that continuous survey extends the interval between surveys. It does the opposite of relaxing anything: the five-year deadline in SOLAS chapter I regulation 10 is untouched and every item is still examined inside it — what changes is that the items are staggered rather than shown together. The paired traps are treating CSM as a statutory requirement when it is a classification arrangement accepted in discharge of the statutory survey, and confusing it with the planned maintenance system, which is the company's ISM-driven overhaul regime and the subject of Q6 on this same paper.
Q2 Mean Piston Speed - Fuel Consumption, Rpm, Combustion and a Method for an Old Ship
16 marks		 Definition, formula and units → Why it governs fuel consumption → The rpm relation → Mean piston speed and ideal combustion → Establish the baseline, then prove the measurements → Run a structured performance test and reduce the data → Separate the engine from the hull, the propeller and the fuel → Bound the answer by the real constraints → Decide, implement, and keep it under review Limbs (a) and (b) rest on no instrument at all - they are geometry, the definition of mean effective pressure and the propeller law. The only regulatory content is in limb (c): the operating envelope decided there is recorded and monitored in the ship's SEEMP under MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4, with Part III mandatory since 1 January 2023, and the operative guidance at this sitting is the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.346(78) - not the 2024 successor, which is future here.
c_m = 2LN with N in rev/s, or 2LN/60 with N in rev/min - metres per second		 Concluding that the lowest mean piston speed gives the lowest fuel bill. Two effects run against each other: power demand falls as the cube of speed, which is the saving, but specific fuel oil consumption rises once the engine is well below its matched point, because compression and scavenge pressure fall, the turbocharger is off its match and atomisation degrades. There is therefore an optimum band, not a monotonic rule. The paired traps are writing the formula as LN instead of 2LN, and claiming that fuel per mile follows the cube - per hour follows the cube, per mile follows roughly the square, because the voyage takes longer.
Q3 Bill of Lading, Signing Precautions, and On-Hire Survey under Three Charters
16 marks		 The three functions → The types worth naming, and the law that governs → The precautions that apply under any charter → Under a VOYAGE charter - protect the incorporation and the lien → Under a TIME charter - signing as presented, and the indemnity → What the on-hire survey is for, and the common core → The three differentiated → The bareboat survey - and the reasons Carriage under a bill of lading is governed by the Hague-Visby Rules, given effect in India by the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925 as amended in 1993, and applied to a particular bill through its Paramount Clause. Transfer of rights under the bill is governed at this sitting by the Indian Bills of Lading Act, 1856, and the ship operates under the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. None of these instruments is quoted in this answer and no article, rule or section is attributed a sub-paragraph.
Three functions of a bill of lading: receipt, evidence of the contract of carriage, document of title		 Signing a clean bill of lading for cargo that is not in apparent good order and condition, in reliance on a letter of indemnity. The indemnity is unenforceable against the party it deceives — the receiver or bank who relied on the clean bill — and issuing a bill the master knows to be false prejudices the owner's P&I cover, so the owner carries the liability with no insurance behind it. The same applies to ante-dating to catch a letter of credit expiry. The correct action is always to clause the bill to match the mate's receipt and refer to owners and the club. The second trap, in limb (c), is forgetting that a voyage charter involves no hire and no delivery, so it attracts no on-hire survey properly so called.
Q4 OPRC Convention - Provisions, Member State Role, Challenges and Documents
16 marks		 What OPRC is, and what it deliberately is not → The key provisions, article by article → Significance - in response, and in the sense in which it touches prevention → The role - and a point of precision first → The challenges - analysed → What a Member State maintains → What the ship carries - and why it comes out of MARPOL MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 is the provision that actually reaches the ship, and it is unamended at this sitting. Regulation 37.1 requires every oil tanker of 150 gross tonnage and above and every other ship of 400 gross tonnage and above to carry a shipboard oil pollution emergency plan approved by the Administration; 37.2 requires it to be based on the Organization's guidelines, written in the working language of the master and officers, and to consist at least of the reporting procedure, the contact list, the description of immediate action and the shipboard co-ordination procedures and point of contact. OPRC article 3 obliges the Party to require the plan; regulation 37 is what specifies it.
OPRC adopted 30 November 1990; entered into force 13 May 1995		 Answering limb (b) as though it were a flag State question. It is not — it asks about Member States, and that puts the article 6 national system squarely in scope: the national contingency plan, the competent national authority and operational contact point, the pre-positioned equipment and the exercise and training programme. That is the heaviest obligation in the Convention and it is owed in the coastal and territorial capacity, so an answer confined to flag duties omits the most substantial thing OPRC requires. The paired traps are believing that OPRC itself requires the ship to carry a SOPEP — it binds States, and MARPOL Annex I regulation 37 binds the ship — and ignoring the printed word “preventing” instead of stating the boundary and answering within it.
Q5 Antifouling Alternatives - Hull Roughness, Oil-Enhanced Foul Release, and Application Safety
16 marks		 The setting, in two sentences → What hull roughness is, and why it costs money → The four families compared → First, the mechanism being enhanced → What incorporated oil does → The limits - which is where the marks are → The hazards → The controls, in the order they must be applied The AFS Convention 2001, in force 17 September 2008, is the instrument behind every coating in this question: it banned the application of organotin biocides from 1 January 2003 and their presence on ship surfaces from 1 January 2008. The live control at this sitting is MEPC.331(76), adopted 17 June 2021, which added cybutryne and applied from 1 January 2023 - ten months before this examination, with existing systems running to the next scheduled anti-fouling renewal and in any event to no later than sixty months after last application.
AFS Convention in force 17 September 2008		 Answering the preamble instead of the limbs. The opening words invite the standard AFS Convention and CDP/SPC/hybrid/foul-release essay, and none of the three printed limbs asks for it — the taxonomy is worth two sentences and a table, and everything else belongs to roughness, oil incorporation and safety. The technical paired traps are assuming the smoothest coating is always best (a fouled foul release surface on an idle ship is worse than a clean self-polishing one, and biological roughness costs far more per micrometre than physical), and explaining oil incorporation without first stating the mechanism it enhances — release force follows the square root of modulus times surface energy, so both must be low, and the oil's whole function is to lower the threshold speed at which shear can strip the fouling off.
Q6 PMS and the ISM Code - Corrective Action, Records, System and Intervals
16 marks		 The structure behind the four printed terms → The three things that are not the same → The process, end to end → What a record must contain, and what makes it good → How a mediocre record system is actually improved → The closed loop → Where an interval comes from → Optimising an interval, and changing one safely → Periodicity of inspections ISM Code section 10 is the whole question. It requires the Company to establish procedures to ensure the ship is maintained in conformity with the relevant rules and regulations and with any additional requirements the Company establishes; and, in meeting that, to ensure that inspections are held at appropriate intervals, that any non-conformity is reported with its possible cause where known, that appropriate corrective action is taken, and that records of these activities are maintained. It further requires critical equipment to be identified and given specific reliability measures including regular testing of standby arrangements and equipment not in continuous use, and requires those inspections and measures to be integrated into the ship's operational maintenance routine. Read it with section 9 for corrective action including measures to prevent recurrence, section 11 for document control, and section 12 for internal audits at intervals not exceeding twelve months.
Internal safety audits on board and ashore at intervals NOT EXCEEDING TWELVE MONTHS - ISM Code section 12		 Using “corrective action” to mean repairing the defect. Replacing the failed component is a correction; corrective action removes the cause, and the Code expressly requires the procedures to include measures intended to prevent recurrence, which reaches beyond this ship to the sister vessels. A non-conformity closed on a replaced part with nothing else changed is the commonest audit finding in this area. The paired traps are omitting critical equipment and the standby-testing obligation — equipment that never runs is exactly what fails when it is needed — and implying in limb (d) that the Company may change a statutory interval, when it may change only its own standards and, with the society's agreement, class-credited ones.
Q7 ISO 8217 Marine Fuel Standards, the Fuel Oil Analysis Report and Corrective Action
16 marks		 The present standards - and which edition is present → Salient features of the specification → The content of a fuel oil analysis report → Before any corrective action - four steps that come first → Corrective action, observation by observation → The judgement to close on MARPOL Annex VI regulation 18 is the regulatory scaffolding behind every step of the response to an adverse report: it prescribes the bunker delivery note and the information it must carry, requires it to be retained on board for three years, requires the MARPOL delivered sample to be sealed and signed and retained for at least twelve months and analysed by the prescribed method, gives the port State the right to inspect the note and take certified copies, and imposes follow-up duties where non-compliant fuel oil is found. Read with regulation 14, which supplies the sulphur limits the specification itself defers to. Annex VI at this sitting is the MEPC.328(76) revision, in force 1 November 2022.
ISO 8217 - the SIXTH edition, of 2017, is the present edition at this November 2023 sitting		 Naming the standard without its edition — and, worse on this paper, naming the wrong one. At November 2023 the present edition is the sixth, of 2017; the seventh edition was published in 2024 and did not exist when this paper was sat. The second trap is starting corrective action before the governance steps: verify the sample identity, apply the ISO 4259 precision, segregate and do not burn the fuel, and notify inside the contractual window. Burning a disputed bunker destroys both the evidence and the claim. The third is failing to separate the findings that are treated from the three that are refused — a flash point below 60 °C, sulphur above the applicable statutory limit, and used-lubricating-oil or chemical contamination.
Q8 Marine Diesel Engine Developments and Retrofits for Slow and Ultra-Slow Steaming
16 marks		 Why the question exists - and why it is regulatory by this sitting → Developments in marine diesel engines → What actually goes wrong at prolonged low load → Air-side retrofits - the first resort → Combustion-side retrofits → Lubrication and wear retrofits → Propulsion-train and heat-balance retrofits → The regulatory retrofit unique to this period → Operational measures, and the judgement MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 is what makes this a 2023 question rather than a general one. The EEXI requirement and the CII rating regime apply from 1 January 2023, EEXI being verified at the first annual, intermediate or renewal survey of the air pollution prevention certificate on or after that date; the SEEMP with Part III mandatory from the same date is where the operating envelope and its improvement measures are recorded. Annex VI at this sitting is the MEPC.328(76) revision, in force 1 November 2022, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy was adopted 7 July 2023 and is therefore operative. Engine or shaft power limitation is the EEXI compliance route that made these retrofits necessary, and it must be capable of override to release a power reserve where safety or the saving of life at sea requires.
MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76) - in force 1 November 2022		 Presenting the retrofits as a catalogue of products instead of as answers to named failures. The examiner is testing whether the candidate knows what actually goes wrong at prolonged low load — and the root failure is insufficient scavenge air, because the turbocharger is off its matched point, with lost compression, deposits, cold corrosion and lost waste heat following from it. The three most commonly omitted items are cylinder lubrication (which decides whether the liners survive), the heat balance (less waste steam means the auxiliary boiler fires more and quietly gives back part of the saving), and the propeller, which was matched to a speed the ship no longer runs at — and re-pitching it moves the barred speed range.
Q9 P&I Clubs - Nature, Calls, Protection and Indemnity Risks, and the Minimum for Cover
16 marks		 What a club is → How the money is collected - the call system → Where the two words came from → Protection risks - the owner's liabilities as operator of the ship → Indemnity risks - cargo and contractual liabilities → What is NOT covered - and the limb is only half answered without it → Getting the cover - entry → Keeping the cover - the continuing conditions → Using the cover - conduct after a casualty There is no single instrument here: a club is governed by its own Rules, which are private contractual documents between the association and its members and are neither cited nor quoted in this answer. The governing Indian shipping statute at this sitting is the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958.
The policy year conventionally runs from noon on 20 FEBRUARY to noon on 20 February		 Answering limb (c) as though it asked what the club does.
